  [G1.3] Employment details are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that Kim is employed, earning approximately £1,100 per month net, and intends to continue working until age 66, at which point she plans to retire fully in 2026. The report states 'Kim earns approximately £1,100 per month net' and 'Kim intends to continue working until she reaches 66, at which point she plans to retire fully'.
    File:     The Fact Find (Test 1) states Kim's employment status as 'Employed', with occupation listed as 'HGV Driver' employed by 'CDH Recruitment'. It records 'Net Basic Monthly Income' of £1,200.00 and separately 'State Pension' of £230.00 monthly. The Suitability Report also states 'You receive £300 per week as an HGV driver', which equals £1,300 per month (52 weeks ÷ 12 months × £300). The intelliflo planning Baseline scenario shows 'Employment' income of £16,640 annually for one client and £13,200 for another, with combined total of £29,840. The Age column in the cashflow shows ages throughout the plan but does not specify a retirement date or age 66 as retirement point.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts Kim earns 'approximately £1,100 per month net' but Fact Find shows 'Net Basic Monthly Income £1,200.00'
      - Report states 'You receive £300 per week as an HGV driver' which converts to approximately £1,300 per month, contradicting the £1,100 monthly figure
      - Report states retirement age is 66 but no corroborating evidence in the retrieved passages confirms this specific age; the Fact Find does not state an intended retirement age
      - Report states retirement year is 2026 but no supporting evidence confirms this date; the planning documents show scenarios to age 100 but do not state a retirement date of 2026
      - The monthly income figures are internally inconsistent within the report itself: £1,100 per month versus £300 per week (£1,300 per month)
    The report's employment income assertion contains multiple internal inconsistencies. The canonical model extracts one figure of £1,100 per month net for Kim, but the same Suitability Report (category I) also states 'You receive £300 per week as an HGV driver' [P15], which mathematically converts to approximately £1,300 per month, not £1,100. The Fact Find (category B) corroborates the higher figure, showing 'Net Basic Monthly Income £1,200.00' [P13], which aligns more closely with the £300 per week statement than with the £1,100 figure. The discrepancy between £1,100 and £1,200-£1,300 is material to affordability modelling. Regarding retirement age: the report asserts age 66 but this is not matched in the passages. The Fact Find [P5] shows a date of birth of 07/05/1960 with age 65 recorded, which if current as at the assessment date would make age 66 achievable in approximately one year (2026), but the specific retirement age of 66 is not independently stated in the evidence. The retirement date of 2026 appears in the narrative but is not corroborated by a separate dated source confirming the client's stated intention. The cashflow projections use age ranges throughout but do not explicitly confirm 2026 as the retirement trigger date. The guard states 'Intended retirement age in a fact find is often superseded by a later file note — prefer the most recent dated evidence.' The most recent employment income evidence is from the Suitability Report itself [P15], which states £300 per week, contradicting the £1,100 monthly assertion within the same document.

  [G1.4] Income and expenditure figures are consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Current household income comprises: HGV employment £300/week net (£1,300/month), volunteer income £20/week net (£86.67/month), Kim's employment £1,100/month net, client's state pension £230/week net (£1,000/month), and Kim's future state pension £230/week net (£1,000/month from 2027). Total current monthly income stated as £4,486.67. Current household expenditure approximately £1,700/month. Monthly surplus £2,786.67. Cash savings £6,000, no liabilities.
    File:     Fact Find [P1] shows: Total Net Monthly Income £1,430.00; Total Monthly Expenditure £1,718.00; Monthly Disposable Income £-288.00. Employment income shown as £1,200/month net (not £1,300). State Pension £230/month shown in income section [P10]. Detailed expenditure breakdown in [P1] totals £1,718.00 monthly. [P17] confirms £300/week HGV income, £20/week volunteer role, £1,100/month for Kim, £230/week state pension. [P13] confirms £6,000 cash savings. Planning scenarios [P3], [P6], [P11] model forward projections showing surplus positions without deficits.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report derives monthly equivalent of £300/week as £1,300/month; Fact Find [P10] states £1,200/month basic income
      - Report total monthly income £4,486.67 cannot be derived from stated components; sum of £1,300 + £86.67 + £1,100 + £1,000 = £3,486.67 excluding future state pension
      - Report monthly surplus £2,786.67 (£4,486.67 minus £1,700); Fact Find [P1] shows monthly disposable income as £-288.00 (£1,430 minus £1,718)
      - Report consolidates household expenditure at £1,700/month; Fact Find [P1] detailed line-items total £1,718.00
      - Report states £4,486.67 total monthly income includes future state pension for Kim (£1,000/month from 2027), but this is projected income not current, making current income figure unclear
    The Fact Find [P1] shows Total Net Monthly Income of £1,430.00, which reconciles to basic employment (£1,200) plus state pension (£230). The report inflates this to £4,486.67 by including Kim's employment (£1,100), volunteer work (£20/week = £86.67), and client's state pension (£230/week = £1,000), plus Kim's future state pension (£1,000/month from age 67 in 2027). The guard on weekly annualisation applies: £300/week at 52 weeks annualises to £15,600/year or £1,300/month, not the £1,200/month shown in the Fact Find employment line. The report correctly converts £300 × 52 ÷ 12 = £1,300/month and £230 × 52 ÷ 12 = £1,000/month. However, the Fact Find [P1] employment line shows only £1,200/month, a 7.7% shortfall from the stated weekly rate. On expenditure, the report states £1,700/month, but the Fact Find itemised breakdown [P1] totals £1,718.00. The difference is £18/month (1.05%), within rounding tolerance. The report's consolidated figure of £1,700 is rounded; the detail supports £1,718. The monthly surplus calculation in the report (£2,786.67) assumes income of £4,486.67 minus £1,700 expenditure; the Fact Find [P1] shows a deficit of £-288.00 based on £1,430 income versus £1,718 expenditure. This represents a fundamental discrepancy in the income base. The report appears to have correctly converted weekly figures to monthly but the Fact Find baseline employment income appears understated at £1,200 versus the weekly rate of £300. The report's derivation of £1,300/month from £300/week is arithmetically correct; the Fact Find entry of £1,200 is the anomaly. Consolidated household expenditure difference (£1,700 vs £1,718) falls within acceptable rounding.

  [G1.6] State pension entitlement is consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report asserts two state pension positions: (1) John is in receipt of his full State Pension of £230 per week (net), stated on page 3 as 'You are in receipt of your full State Pension of £230 per week'. (2) Kim will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age of 67, stated as 'Kim will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age'. The report also states John receives state pension income of £11,973 per annum and Kim will receive £11,973 per annum from age 67. John's £230 per week converts to £11,960 per annum (£230 × 52), and Kim's stated entitlement is £230 per week, also converting to £11,960 per annum. The report lists Kim's State Pension age as 67.
    File:     The planning documents [P1] and [P5] show State Pension Age as 66 for one client. [P1] lists 'STATE PENSION AGE 66' in the baseline scenario. [P5] states 'You receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum' for John and 'Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67.' The fact find [P15] contains a State Pension Entitlement section but does not disclose specific state pension amounts or ages. The policy information documents [P18], [P19], [P21] do not contain state pension entitlements. The £230 per week figure is corroborated in [P9] which states 'You are in receipt of your full State Pension of £230 per week, and Kim will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age.' The annual figure of £11,973 stated in [P5] is £13 more than £230 × 52 (which equals £11,960).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Kim's State Pension age as 67; planning document [P1] shows State Pension Age as 66 for one client
      - Report states Kim will receive £11,973 per annum; this is £13 more than £230 per week × 52 weeks (£11,960)
      - Report states John receives £11,973 per annum; this is £13 more than £230 per week × 52 weeks (£11,960)
    The guard notes that state pension age and intended retirement age are routinely conflated. The planning document [P1] shows 'STATE PENSION AGE 66' which may refer to the female client (Kim), whose actual state pension age under current UK law for someone born in the 1960s would typically be 66-67 depending on exact birth date. The report asserts Kim's state pension age is 67. The discrepancy between £230 per week stated (£11,960 annual) and the £11,973 annual figure appears in both [P5] and the report. The £11,973 figure is £13 higher than the weekly conversion, suggesting either rounding, a different assumption about the number of weeks in a year, or an error in one of the sources. The weekly figure of £230 is corroborated identically in [P9]. The £11,973 annual figure appears in [P5] but not reconciled to the weekly statement. Reconciliation tolerance for weekly/annual is x52: £230 × 52 = £11,960, which does not equal £11,973 (difference of £13 or 0.11%). This falls within normal rounding variance for pension projections but represents an inconsistency between the two frequencies used in the same documents.

  [G1.8] Data mismatches and unsupported figures within the report itself
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The extraction identified five internal inconsistencies: (1) Aviva existing charge 0.50% with switch impact -0.06% (savings) but narrative states 'plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than recommended solution'; (2) Standard Life existing charge 0.52% but switch table shows current 0.18% and impact +0.26%, while narrative states 'plan itself is 0.08% more expensive'; (3) £300 per week HGV income stated consistently but household expenditure 'approximately £1,700 per month' creates tension with non-pension income; (4) Scottish Widows fund value £211.32 in arrangements table but calculation example uses £200 and estimates net proceeds £170; (5) Recommended solution total transfer value £110,185 but sum of existing arrangements equals £116,998.47, not matching either the recommended total or narrative 'approximately £110,000'.
    File:     Not applicable. This requirement assesses the report against itself and does not require corroboration from external documents. No supporting file passages were searched because the comparison is internal to the suitability report.
    Discrepancies:
      - Aviva charge narrative contradiction: text states 'plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than recommended solution' but Pension Switch Charges table shows -0.06% impact (meaning a saving, i.e. the recommended solution is cheaper)
      - Standard Life charge inconsistency: existing arrangement listed as 0.52% fund charge, but Pension Switch table shows current charge 0.18% and impact +0.26%, while narrative states 'plan itself is 0.08% more expensive'; the three sources yield inconsistent conclusions (0.26% vs 0.08% difference)
      - Scottish Widows fund value and calculation mismatch: fund value stated as £211.32 in arrangements table but calculation explanation uses £200 as the basis; estimated net proceeds £170 represents 80.95% of the actual value £211.32 but only 80% of the example £200, showing the calculation was worked on a different figure than the actual holding
      - Recommended solution total value discrepancy: report recommends total transfer value of £110,185, but arithmetic sum of the five existing arrangements (£6,601.55 + £211.32 + £3,744.36 + £103,439.24 + £3,002.00) equals £116,998.47; narrative also states 'approximately £110,000' which matches neither the actual sum nor the recommended figure
    All five inconsistencies flagged by the extraction are material contradictions within the report itself. (1) Aviva: The narrative and the Pension Switch table tell opposite stories about whether the recommended Aviva plan is more or less expensive than the existing one. A -0.06% impact means the recommended solution costs less; the narrative's statement that it is '0.06% more expensive' directly contradicts this. This is a High severity finding because it relates to a cost comparison that drives the recommendation. (2) Standard Life: The existence of three different numbers (0.52% existing, 0.18% current in table, impact +0.26%, narrative 0.08% difference) is internally incoherent. If the existing charge is 0.52% and the impact is +0.26%, the new charge should be 0.78%, not aligned with the 0.18% shown. The narrative's 0.08% figure does not reconcile to either pair. High severity: the report contradicts itself on a cost-driving figure. (3) HGV income: The £300 per week is stated consistently across the report, so this is not an internal contradiction of the income figure itself. The extraction notes tension between income and expenses but this is more of a logical concern than an arithmetic contradiction within stated figures. The income and expenses themselves are not contradictory numbers; rather, their sufficiency is a planning assumption. This is Lower severity and not an arithmetic mismatch. (4) Scottish Widows: The fund value is £211.32. The explanation uses £200, and the net proceeds calculation yields £170. If £211.32 is withdrawn: 25% tax-free = £52.83; 75% taxable = £158.49. At marginal rate this could net around £170 depending on tax rate, which is plausible. However, the calculation explanation should work from £211.32 not from a rounded £200. This is a Low-to-Moderate severity discrepancy: the underlying logic may be sound but the calculation was demonstrated on a different figure. (5) Transfer value total: This is the most serious. The five arrangements sum to £116,998.47. The report recommends a total of £110,185. That is a difference of £6,813.47 or about 5.8%. The narrative mentions 'approximately £110,000' which could cover the recommended figure but does not explain why the actual holdings total more. If the report is recommending transferring only £110,185 out of £116,998.47 in total assets, this is a material omission in clarity. If it is a calculation error, it affects the entire recommendation basis. High severity: a figure that drives the recommendation contradicts the evidence (here, the report's own figures).

  [G1.10] Values expressed in different frequencies or formats reconcile (weekly/monthly/annual, gross/net, percentages and totals)
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts five income sources with stated frequencies and amounts: (1) John's HGV employment £300 per week net; (2) John's volunteer role £20 per week net; (3) Kim's employment £1,100 per month net; (4) John's State Pension £230 per week net (actual); (5) Kim's State Pension £230 per week net (projected from 2027). The report derives monthly equivalents: £1,300, £86.67, £1,100, £1,000, and £1,000 respectively. It states total monthly income net as £4,486.67 (combining current incomes: £1,300 + £86.67 + £1,100 + £1,000 = £3,486.67, with Kim's state pension of £1,000 starting 2027). Household expenditure is stated as approximately £1,700 per month in narrative sections and listed in the expenditure array at £1,700 (actual) and £1,700 (intended).
    File:     The Fact Find [P2] and [P1] show: John's basic income as £1,200 per month net (not £300 per week); State Pension as £230 per month net (not per week); Total Net Monthly Income calculated as £1,430.00; Total Monthly Expenditure as £1,718.00. The Fact Find [P5] records client's stated retirement planning need as £1,700 net per month household income. The Fact Find detailed expenditure breakdown [P1] and [P2] itemises monthly expenses totalling £1,718.00. Research notes [P12] confirm "Needs £1,700 net per month income in retirement."
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states John earns £300 per week net as HGV driver; Fact Find [P2] lists basic income as £1,200 per month net from CDH Recruitment employment, which is £276.92 per week equivalent (13% lower than £300/week)
      - Report states John volunteers £20 per week net; no matching entry found in Fact Find income schedule—only basic employment and state pension are listed for John
      - Report states Kim earns £1,100 per month net; Fact Find [P2] lists £1,200 per month net basic income, a £100/month (9.1%) discrepancy
      - Report states John's State Pension is £230 per week net; Fact Find [P2] lists State Pension as £230 per month net, not per week—a material difference of factor of 4.33 (£230/week would be £11,960/year vs £2,760/year from £230/month)
      - Report derives John's HGV income monthly equivalent as £1,300 (£300 × 52 ÷ 12); if actual amount is £1,200/month from Fact Find, derived figure should be £1,200, not £1,300
      - Report states total monthly net income as £4,486.67; calculation shown is £1,300 + £86.67 + £1,100 + £1,000 = £3,486.67 (£1,000 discrepancy before Kim's future state pension; the £4,486.67 total includes an unexplained additional £1,000)
      - Report derives volunteer income monthly equivalent as £86.67 (£20 × 52 ÷ 12); Fact Find shows no volunteer income entry, and no evidence of £20/week volunteer income anywhere in file
      - Report states household expenditure approximately £1,700 per month; Fact Find [P1] and [P2] calculate Total Monthly Household Expenditure as £1,718.00, a £18 discrepancy (1% variance)
      - Report lists state pension entitlement frequency inconsistently: asserts £230 per week for both John (actual) and Kim (projected), but Fact Find [P2] shows £230 per month for State Pension
    The comparison reveals multiple material frequency and basis mismatches. The most significant is the State Pension frequency error: the report treats State Pension as £230 per week, but the Fact Find [P2] records it as £230 per month. This is not a rounding difference but a 52-week/12-month annualisation error that inflates the stated pension income by a factor of 4.33. The RangeMatch figures corroborate £230 to [P2], confirming the monthly figure. John's employment income also shows a discrepancy: the report asserts £300/week net, but the Fact Find [P2] shows £1,200/month net (£276.92/week equivalent). The report's stated total monthly income of £4,486.67 does not reconcile to the component figures it provides (£1,300 + £86.67 + £1,100 + £1,000 = £3,486.67 currently, or £2,486.67 without the projected Kim pension). The volunteer income of £20/week appears nowhere in the Fact Find schedules—no corresponding entry exists. The £1,100 vs £1,200 discrepancy for Kim's income (9.1% gap) exceeds the 1% tolerance. Household expenditure differs by £18 (£1,700 stated vs £1,718 calculated in Fact Find [P1])—this is within rounding tolerance under the guards. The internal inconsistency on total income (the report's own sum does not equal its stated total) is an arithmetic contradiction that does not require external evidence. The frequency conversion guard (52 weeks vs 4.33 × 12) explains the method but not why the wrong frequency was selected for State Pension in the first place—this is a data entry or transcription error, not an arithmetic guard.

  [G1.11] Facts used in calculations, cashflow modelling, projections and assumptions match the facts stated in the report narrative
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts cashflow modelling was performed (wasCashflowModelled = true) with disclosed assumptions: General expenditure inflation 2%, Savings inflation 2%, State pensions inflation 2.5%, Aviva personal pensions inflation 5%, Surplus income percentage saved 0%, Mid growth rate for critical yield 2.94%, Annual expenditure in retirement £1,700 per month. The report states the cashflow runs until age 100 assuming all surplus income is spent rather than saved. Scenarios modelled include baseline projection, high inflation stress test (6%), market crash combined with high inflation (-30% immediate loss with 6% inflation), and longevity tests using ONS life expectancy (age 86 for 69-year-old male, age 88 for 65-year-old female). Monthly surplus calculated as £2,786.67. Savings of £6,000 held. Three pension arrangements included in cashflow: Aviva transfer £3,744, Zurich transfer £103,439, Standard Life £3,002.
    File:     Supporting evidence shows: Cashflow assumptions in [P1] disclose General expenditure 2%, Savings 2%, State pensions 2.5%, Aviva personal pensions 5%, 0% surplus income saved. Inflation assumptions in [P11] list General 2%, State Pension 2.5%, but do not mention the 5% Aviva personal pensions rate or the 2.94% mid-growth rate used in critical yield calculation. The proposed scenario in [P7] shows opening assets: Savings £6,000, Peoples pension £6,601, Scottish Widows £211, New Aviva pension £110,185, total stated as £122,997. Baseline scenario in [P14] lists five existing pensions totalling £116,997: Aviva £3,744, Zurich £103,439, Standard Life £3,002, Peoples pension £6,601, Scottish Widows £211. Longevity assumptions in [P22] state life expectancy for 69-year-old male is age 86 and for 65-year-old female is age 88, corroborating the report. The projected scenario in [P7] lists total assets as £122,997 comprising Savings £6,000 and Pensions £116,997, but the report's canonical model shows monthly surplus of £2,786.67 which does not reconcile to any figure in the evidence. Cashflow tables in [P21] show in-year surplus/deficit values but none match £2,786.67. The mid-growth rate of 2.94% stated in the report is corroborated by [P15] but the 5% Aviva personal pensions inflation rate appears in [P1] but is not listed in the general inflation assumptions table in [P11].
    Discrepancies:

      - Report assumes 5% inflation for Aviva personal pensions per [P1], but [P11] inflation assumptions table does not list this rate—only General 2%, School Fees 4%, Property 4.7%, Long Term Care 4.5%, State Pension 2.5%, Lending 4%
      - Monthly surplus calculated in canonical model as £2,786.67 does not match any monthly figure in the supporting cashflow evidence [P21] or [P14]
      - Report narrative states annual expenditure in retirement approximately £1,700 per month [P22], which is internally consistent with canonical model stating £1,700 per month
      - Report includes three pension arrangements in modelling (Aviva £3,744, Zurich £103,439, Standard Life £3,002) per [P22], corroborated by [P14] Baseline scenario showing identical values
    The cashflow modelling assumptions are substantially consistent between the suitability report and the supporting evidence. The 2%, 2.5%, and 0% rates are corroborated in [P1]. The 5% Aviva personal pensions inflation rate is stated in [P1] but does not appear in the standard inflation assumptions table in [P11], suggesting it may be an override or special assumption applied during modelling. The mid-growth rate of 2.94% reported matches [P15] (shown as 2.95%, a rounding variation within tolerance). The longevity assumptions (age 86 for male, age 88 for female) are corroborated by [P22]. The pension arrangements listed in narrative (Aviva £3,744, Zurich £103,439, Standard Life £3,002) are exactly matched in the Baseline scenario [P14]. However, the monthly surplus of £2,786.67 derived in the canonical model cannot be located or reconciled in the supporting cashflow tables. [P14] shows a cashflow statement with opening values and in-year surplus/deficit columns, but the specific figure £2,786.67 does not appear. [P21] shows proposed scenario surplus/deficit values for each year (ranging from £20,788 to £26,149 in the displayed years) but monthly equivalents would be substantially lower. The report's statement that the cashflow 'runs until you reach age 100' is corroborated by [P22]. The assumption that 'all surplus income throughout your lifetime is spent rather than saved' matches [P1] and [P11]. The three pension arrangements referenced in modelling align with [P14] Baseline scenario exactly. The derivation method for the £2,786.67 monthly surplus is not evident from the pack; it may represent an opening position calculation rather than a modelled output, but without supporting calculation in the evidence, this figure cannot be verified.

Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G2.1, G2.2, G2.3, G2.4, G2.5, G2.6, G2.7, G2.8). No discrepancy in the objectives themselves or their priority order. This aligns. The monthly income target of £1,700 net per month is consistently stated across [P2], [P12], and [P7]. The consolidation objective is consistently stated across [P2] and [P12].  A subsidiary discrepancy emerges from [P7]: current total net monthly income is £1,430 and total monthly expenditure is £1,718, yielding a disposable income of -£288. The stated retirement objective requires £1,700 net household income per month. The client currently has a £288 monthly shortfall even before retirement. This suggests the retirement income goal is not met by current earnings alone and is predicated on pension drawdown, but the report does not explicitly connect the consolidation recommendation to this funding gap or explain how the recommended pension structure addresses it.   [G2.1] Stated goals and their priority order
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts three objectives with stated priority ranks: (1) Ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime [rank 1]; (2) Retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month [rank 2]; (3) Consolidate pension arrangements and ensure they are appropriately structured for retirement, preferring a streamlined approach to managing pensions rather than maintaining five separate plans [rank 3]. All three are marked as client-stated (isClientStated = true).
    File:     The evidence corroborates the three objectives. [P2] (Suitability Report) restates: 'You want to ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime. You want to retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month. You would like a clearer understanding of your pension arrangements and to ensure they are appropriately structured for your retirement. You also prefer a more streamlined approach to managing your pensions, rather than maintaining five separate plans with different providers.' [P12] (Fact Find) records: 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month.' [P5] (File notes) confirms capacity for loss: 'you are aware that investment values can rise as well as fall' and discusses income sources and household circumstances supporting the retirement goal. [P1] (Fact Find declaration) states 'I/We the undersigned confirm my/our financial planning objectives are those identified and prioritised in this document,' signed by the client. [P8] (intelliflo planning proposed scenario) shows timeline with 'Retire (John)' at age 71 in 2027, and asset position at retirement. [P7] (Fact Find) shows calculated household expenditure of £1,718 per month against current net income of £1,430 per month.
    Discrepancies:
      - No discrepancy in the objectives themselves or their priority order. However, there is a temporal tension: the report states retirement 'in 2 years time' (from advice date 18/09/2025, implying 2027), and [P8] timeline confirms 'Retire (John)' in 2027 at age 71. This aligns. The monthly income target of £1,700 net per month is consistently stated across [P2], [P12], and [P7]. The consolidation objective is consistently stated across [P2] and [P12].
      -  A subsidiary discrepancy emerges from [P7]: current total net monthly income is £1,430 and total monthly expenditure is £1,718, yielding a disposable income of -£288. The stated retirement objective requires £1,700 net household income per month. The client currently has a £288 monthly shortfall even before retirement. This suggests the retirement income goal is not met by current earnings alone and is predicated on pension drawdown, but the report does not explicitly connect the consolidation recommendation to this funding gap or explain how the recommended pension structure addresses it.
    All three objectives are evidenced in the file. The objectives are client-stated as confirmed by [P1] (signed declaration) and recorded in [P12] (Fact Find narrative). The priority order (lifestyle affordability as primary, retirement income as secondary, pension consolidation as tertiary) is consistent with the sequence in which they appear in [P2] and the Fact Find. The wording differs between report and evidence (e.g., 'Retirement Planning' vs 'retire in 2 years time'), but the substance is identical and the guard permits paraphrasing. The retirement horizon of '2 years' (retirement in 2027 from 2025 advice) is corroborated by [P8] timeline and the client's stated age (69/70 in 2025, 71/67 in 2027). The income target of £1,700 net per month is consistently evidenced across three sources [P2], [P12], [P7]. The consolidation objective (five separate plans to one streamlined structure) is explicitly stated in both [P2] and [P12]. However, a material gap exists: [P7] reveals current household net income (£1,430) falls £288 short of monthly expenditure (£1,718), let alone the retirement target of £1,700 net per month. The suitability report does not reconcile this gap or explain how the recommended solution bridges it. The report states the objectives but does not evidence how the recommendation (consolidation into a new Aviva pension) specifically supports the income sufficiency objective. The consolidation and streamlining objective is addressed structurally (five plans moving to one), but the income adequacy objective—the highest priority—is asserted without demonstrating that the recommended investment strategy, drawdown structure, or pension consolidation generates the stated £1,700 per month. This is weak justification rather than missing evidence, as the objectives themselves are fully evidenced.

  [G2.2] Retirement target date and investment time horizon details
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) One objective with targetDate of 2027-10-14 and termYears of 2; (2) Three timeHorizon values: LongTerm, MediumTerm, MediumTerm; (3) Intended retirement age of 66 for one client (John); (4) Life expectancy for 69-year-old male is age 86; (5) Life expectancy for 65-year-old female is age 88. The report states investment period of 2 years with retirement at age 66 in 2026 and age 71 in 2027.
    File:     Evidence shows: (1) P10 (intelliflo proposed scenario) states timeline: 2026 — 70/66 marked as 'Retire (Kim)' and 2027 — 71/67 marked as 'Retire (John)'; (2) P10 shows clients are currently 69/65 (dated 2025); (3) P1-P9 (pension switch report) reference retirement age 75 for Aviva plan, age 75 for Zurich, age 65 for Standard Life plan, and age 65 for selected retirement age on Zurich; (4) P11 (risk profile) states Investment Period of 2 years; (5) P10 shows state pension age 66 for both parties; (6) P24 (suitability report itself, category I) cites ONS life expectancy: 69-year-old male age 86, 65-year-old female age 88; (7) P15 (Zurich policy) states 'Selected retirement age 65' and 'Planned Retirement Age 75'.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts intendedRetirementAge of 66 but P10 shows retirement events: 'Retire (Kim) age 66 in 2026' and 'Retire (John) age 71 in 2027'—one client retiring at 66, other at 71, not both at 66
      - Report's single targetDate of 2027-10-14 with termYears of 2 conflicts with dual retirement events across two different years (2026 and 2027) for two clients
      - Pension policy documents (P1, P12, P15) show retirement age assumptions ranging from 65 to 75 across different arrangements, but report asserts only one intended retirement age of 66
      - Report timeHorizon array contains three values (LongTerm, MediumTerm, MediumTerm) without clear mapping to which objective each serves or whether they apply to one or multiple clients
      - P15 shows Zurich plan has both 'Selected retirement age 65' (contractual) and 'Planned Retirement Age 75' (default assumption), creating ambiguity about which is the actual target
      - Investment period stated as 2 years in P11 aligns with termYears 2 in report, but this does not reconcile with retirement events in 2026 and 2027 if clients are currently aged 69 and 65 (making their horizons 1 year and 2 years respectively to retirement, not 2 years uniform)
    The comparison can be made but reveals material inconsistency. The report states a single intended retirement age of 66 but the evidence (P10) clearly shows this is only one of two clients: Kim retires at 66 in 2026, John retires at 71 in 2027. The report's extraction captured intendedRetirementAge as an array with only one value (66), when two clients with different retirement dates are present. The targetDate of 2027-10-14 appears to map to John's retirement (October 2027, age 71), but the report asserts age 66 without qualification, failing to distinguish the two clients' different time horizons. The pension switch report documents reference multiple contractual retirement ages across existing plans (65 on Standard Life and Zurich selected, 75 as defaults), but the suitability report does not reconcile these or explain which serves which client or purpose. The timeHorizon array (LongTerm, MediumTerm, MediumTerm) appears three times without explanation of the corresponding objectives. P11 states investment period of 2 years, but with clients aged 69 and 65 in 2025, uniform 2-year horizons do not reconcile with retirement events occurring at different dates. The life expectancy figures (86 for 69-year-old male, 88 for 65-year-old female) are internally consistent between P24 and P10/P24 and correctly cited to ONS, but this does not cure the fundamental absence of clear, client-specific retirement age documentation. The guardnote that 'the switch analysis states its own retirement age and term, and is routinely a different figure from the one in the report and the fact find' applies here: P1-P9 reference ages 65 and 75 for different products, the plannning documents show 2026 and 2027 retirements, and the report asserts only 66 without disambiguation. This is not a guard that explains a difference; it demonstrates that the report's statement is incomplete and unsupported by the totality of evidence.

  [G2.3] Access requirements — emergency, planned and ad-hoc
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) Emergency access needs are for 'Unexpected emergencies' funded from 'Cash savings'; (2) Client has confirmed retention of £6,000 in liquid capital as sufficient for unexpected emergencies; (3) Ad-hoc access needs are 'Future access to pension funds after retirement' required 'After retirement/age 55', funded via 'Pension arrangements via Flexi Access Drawdown' with 10-15 working days administration; (4) Client is 'comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds'; (5) State pension income will cover bills in retirement, allowing pension to grow for 'large ad hoc spends'.
    File:     Evidence supports: (1) Cash savings of £6,000 held [P1, P19]; (2) Planning documents show liquid assets of £6,000 throughout baseline and proposed scenarios [P1, P2]; (3) Flexi-access drawdown (FAD) described as option allowing flexible and tax-efficient withdrawal [P10]; (4) Report states FAD provides access from age 55 with no upper withdrawal limit [P10]; (5) Fact Find records total monthly household expenditure of £1,718 [P11]; (6) Fact Find records total net monthly income of £1,430 [P11]; (7) Planning projections show liquid assets declining gradually over time in both scenarios [P2, P6, P8]; (8) Report explicitly states 'You have confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies' [from canonical model]; (9) Report states income projections incorporate cashflow analysis and surplus income assumptions [P24].
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states 'In retirement, you expect your annual expenditure to be approximately £1,700' [P24], but Fact Find shows calculated total monthly household expenditure of £1,718 [P11], which annualizes to £20,616 per annum [P2, P4]. This is a material discrepancy: the report's £1,700 figure appears to refer to monthly expenditure, not annual, or represents a significant understatement.
      - The report's statement that 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement' [P24] is not clearly reconciled with the cashflow showing required withdrawals from pensions and investments throughout retirement in both baseline and proposed scenarios [P24].
      - While the report mentions ad-hoc access via Flexi Access Drawdown after retirement/age 55, there is no explicit statement of planned or anticipated withdrawals amounts or timing, only that access will 'usually take 10-15 working days'.
    The requirement tests consistency between stated access requirements and supporting evidence. (1) Emergency fund: The report clearly states £6,000 liquid capital held and client confirmation of sufficiency. This is directly corroborated by Fact Find [P19] and planning documents [P1, P2] showing £6,000 savings. The assertion is supported. (2) Ad-hoc access after retirement: The report asserts future pension access via FAD from age 55 onward. This is supported by the FAD mechanism described in the report itself [P10] and the planning scenarios showing accessible pensions throughout retirement [P2, P6]. However, there is a material inconsistency regarding retirement expenditure. The report states 'you expect your annual expenditure to be approximately £1,700' [P24], but the Fact Find documents show monthly expenditure of £1,718 [P11]. If the £1,700 refers to annual expenditure, the planning projections are based on a substantially different figure (£20,616 per annum shown in the planning documents [P2, P4]). This creates ambiguity about whether access needs were properly established and modelled. The statement that 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement' [P24] is not reconciled with the cashflow charts showing required pension withdrawals throughout retirement, suggesting the statement may be incomplete or context-dependent. (3) Client comfort with liquid access: The report cites client confirmation of comfort with liquid cash for instance access [canonical model], supported by language in the suitability report [P20 context suggests client engagement]. (4) The planning documents consistently show £6,000 liquid assets available at plan outset, declining gradually over the projection period [P2, P6, P8], consistent with the assertion that this funds emergency needs. The guard permits 'No access needs identified' as valid; here, emergency needs are explicitly identified and addressed, satisfying that aspect. The principal inconsistency is the expenditure figure discrepancy (£1,700 annual vs £20,616 shown in planning), which affects whether ad-hoc access needs were correctly quantified and whether pension access is genuinely discretionary or required.

  [G2.4] Foreseeable life changes
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   Report identifies four foreseeable life changes: (1) Client retirement expected 2027-10-14 with financial impact of -£300/week (-£1,300/month net) loss of HGV driver income, quoted 'You expect to retire within the next two years'; (2) Kim's retirement expected 2027-01-01, quoted 'Kim intends to continue working until she reaches 66, at which point she plans to retire fully'; (3) Kim begins receiving State Pension 2027-01-01 for around £230/week (£1,000/month net), quoted 'Kim will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age'; (4) Inheritance Tax treatment change 2027-04-06, quoted 'From 6 April 2027, most unused defined contribution pensions and death benefits (such as drawdown funds and uncrystallised pots) will be included in your taxable estate for IHT'. Report also states current situation: client aged 69, Kim aged 65, client earns £300/week net as HGV driver plus £20/week volunteer income, Kim earns £1,100/month net, household expenditure £1,700/month, expects £1,700 net monthly in retirement, state pension £230/week for client already received, Kim will receive £230/week state pension at state pension age. Report objectives include 'retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month'.
    File:     Fact Find (P9, P18, P20) dated 19/09/2025 shows: Date of Birth 07/06/1956 (age 70 at fact find date), Kim's Date of Birth 07/05/1960 (age 65). Income: Employment from CDH Recruitment £1,200/month, State Pension £230/month (P18 shows £230.00 monthly, not weekly), total monthly income £1,430. Expenditure calculated at £1,718/month. Intelliflo planning baseline (P1) shows retirement events: 'Retire' age 66 in 2026 for Kim, 'Retire' age 71 in 2027 for client (JS); State Pension Age 66 for both. Intelliflo proposed scenario (P8) timeline shows: 2026 'Retire (Kim)' at age 70/66, 2027 'Retire (John)' at age 71/67. Suitability Report (P6) states: 'You are currently aged 69 and married to Kim, aged 65... You expect to retire within the next two years, while Kim intends to continue working until she reaches 66, at which point she plans to retire fully.' Suitability Report (P10) states: 'You receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum' (£230/week × 52 = £11,960, approximately £230/week), 'Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67', 'In retirement, you expect your annual expenditure to be approximately £1,700' (stated as monthly equivalent elsewhere). Fact Find (P19) shows total monthly household expenditure £1,718 with no expected changes.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states client currently aged 69; Fact Find shows DOB 07/06/1956 making client age 70 (or 69 if fact find completed before 7 June, but date 19/09/2025 is after that date)
      - Report states client retirement expected 2027-10-14 (within two years from report perspective); Intelliflo planning shows client (JS) retire age 71 in 2027, baseline shows retire age 71 in 2027 (not 2026-10)
      - Report states Kim retires 2027-01-01 at age 66; Intelliflo timeline shows Kim retire 2026 at age 66 and separately 2027 at age 67, creating ambiguity
      - Report quotes state pension as £230/week for both; Fact Find lists £230/month for basic income, not weekly
      - Report states financial impact of client retirement as -£300/week net loss of HGV income; Fact Find shows employment income £1,200/month from CDH Recruitment which is approximately £276/week, not £300/week
      - Report states Kim's retirement financial impact -£1,100/month net loss of employment income; this aligns with Fact Find showing Kim's income contribution £1,100/month
      - Report states Kim will receive £1,000/month net from state pension; State Pension £230/week = approximately £1,000/month, but actual figure stated in Fact Find as £230/month (not weekly), creating unit ambiguity
    The comparison can be performed. Several discrepancies exist but require careful interpretation: (1) Client age: Report extraction states 69 at report completion; Fact Find DOB 07/06/1956 with fact find dated 19/09/2025 indicates age 69 at fact find (not yet reached 70th birthday in June as extract shows current date in September is after birthdate). This is internally consistent. (2) Retirement timing: Report states 'within two years' from report perspective; Intelliflo shows 2027 retirement aligning with 'within two years' if report dated 2025. The discrepancy between Intelliflo baseline showing age 71 in 2027 and report showing October 2027 retirement reflects different planning scenarios (baseline vs proposed). Not a material contradiction. (3) Kim's retirement: Report states age 66 in 2027; Intelliflo shows 2026 retire at age 66. Kim DOB 07/05/1960 means she reaches 66 on 07/05/2026, not 2027. This is a discrepancy: report states 2027-01-01 but evidence shows she reaches 66 in 2026. However, the report separately in narrative (P6) states 'Kim intends to continue working until she reaches 66', which is consistent with 2026. The canonical model extraction showing 2027-01-01 appears incorrect; the narrative supports 2026. (4) State Pension amount: Fact Find shows £230/month for 'Basic Income' from State Pension category. Report quotes £230/week. This is a unit error in either report or fact find. P10 suitability report states '£11,973 per annum' which equals approximately £230/week (£11,973÷52≈£230/week) or £998/month. The Fact Find entry of £230/month appears to be a data entry error for what should be weekly amounts. Report's £230/week is consistent with the annual figures stated. (5) HGV income: Report states £300/week net; Fact Find shows Employment £1,200/month ≈ £276/week. This is a discrepancy of £24/week or £100/month. (6) Kim's employment income: Report states £1,100/month; Fact Find confirms approximately £1,100/month from employment income. Consistent. (7) Household expenditure: Report states £1,700/month in current situation and £1,700/month in retirement; Fact Find calculates £1,718/month total monthly household expenditure. Difference of £18/month is immaterial rounding. (8) Inheritance Tax change: This is a legislative change effective 6 April 2027 affecting all pension holders, not a client-specific foreseeable change. The guard notes legislative changes affecting everyone are not client-specific foreseeable changes, so this should not be counted as a foreseeable life change in the sense required. The report has included a regulatory event rather than a personal life event. All stated objectives in the report (retire within 2 years with £1,700/month household income, consolidate pensions) are supported by fact find evidence showing retirement planning intention and need for £1,700/month income. The planning models (Intelliflo) support the retirement within 2-year timeframe and the income target. No vulnerability factors identified in evidence that would materially affect this assessment.

  [G2.5] Conflicting goals
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states three objectives with priority ranks: (1) 'You want to ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime'; (2) 'You want to retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month'; (3) 'You would like a clearer understanding of your pension arrangements and to ensure they are appropriately structured for your retirement. You also prefer a more streamlined approach to managing your pensions, rather than maintaining five separate plans with different providers.' The report asserts a target date of 2027-10-14 for one objective. The investment recommendation is based on 'a 2-year investment horizon to retirement' with retirement age 75 used for projections. The report states the client will 'retire in 2 years time' and includes cashflow modelling showing 'Retire (Kim)' in 2026 and 'Retire (John)' in 2027.
    File:     The Fact Find [P21] confirms 'I/We the undersigned confirm my/our financial planning objectives are those identified and prioritised in this document.' The intelliflo planning baseline scenario [P9] and proposed scenario [P11] model retirement dates: 'Retire' age 66 in 2026 for one spouse and 'Retire' age 71 in 2027 for the other. The cashflow analysis [P9] shows income 2026 total £40,703 (which includes employment income of £16,640 plus state pension of £12,173), dropping in 2027 to £24,181 (state pension only, employment income ceasing). Current household expenditure is stated as approximately £1,700 net per month [P4]. The planning documents model liquid assets and accessible pensions remaining stable at approximately £122,000 through the projection period [P5], [P9]. The objectives around lifestyle affordability and pension consolidation are supported by the fact-finding process and cashflow modelling showing the client can sustain £1,700 monthly expenditure throughout retirement. No evidence contradicts the stated objectives; the planning confirms both spouses retire within the 2-year window (one in 2026, one in 2027).
    The report's three stated objectives are all evidenced and reconciled with supporting documentation. Objective 1 (lifestyle affordability through lifetime) is supported by cashflow projections [P5], [P9] demonstrating sustainable drawdown with no deficits. Objective 2 (retire in 2 years with £1,700 net monthly household income) is confirmed by the timeline in [P11] showing retirement in 2026 and 2027, and by the first-year expenditure statement [P16] showing projected household expenses of £24,001 annually (approximately £2,000 monthly), with the stated target of £1,700 appearing as an achievable net figure after tax. Objective 3 (pension consolidation and streamlining) is evidenced by the recommendation to consolidate five separate pension plans into a single Aviva Platform arrangement [P3], [P4], [P11]. The 2-year investment horizon stated in the report aligns with the 2027-10-14 target date extracted, falling within the 2026-2027 retirement window modelled in the planning documents. The report's investment recommendation of balanced funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 50%, CT Universal MAP Balanced 50%) with combined annual charges of 0.47% (0.18% + 0.29%) plus platform and advice charges is positioned as appropriate for a medium-term horizon to retirement. No structural conflict is apparent: the three objectives do not compete for the same capital; the investment term (to age 75) extends beyond the initial access date (retirement at 66/71) but this is standard practice for ongoing growth through drawdown and is justified by the client's lifetime income need. The vulnerability overlay does not apply here—no actual or potential vulnerability drivers identified in the evidence materially affect the consistency of objectives and time horizons.

  [G2.6] Recommendations are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states five recommendations linked to objectives: four recommendations linked to OBJ3 (encash Scottish Widows £211.32, switch Aviva £3,744.36, switch Zurich £103,439.24, switch Standard Life £3,002.00) and one linked to OBJ1 (transfer to Aviva Pension Portfolio £110,185). The report specifies a retirement age of 75 and identifies a retirement need of £1,700 net per month in retirement. The objectives have time horizons stated as: OBJ1 LongTerm, OBJ2 MediumTerm, OBJ3 MediumTerm. The report states 'In retirement, you expect your annual expenditure to be approximately £1,700' and projects cashflow to age 100 with no shortfall under mid-growth assumptions. The client is stated to understand pension freedoms and prefer Flexi Access Drawdown flexibility from age 55 (rising to 57 from 6 April 2028). Current age is approximately 69 (born 7 June 1956, planning date 2025).
    File:     Evidence shows the client's stated retirement income need from the fact find and planning data: 'Needs £1,700 net per month income in retirement' (P1). The existing arrangements include five pension plans with combined values of £116,998.47 (People's Pension £6,601.55, Scottish Widows £211.32, Aviva £3,744.36, Zurich £103,439.24, Standard Life £3,002.00). Employment income is stated as '£300 per week as an HGV driver' (P10). State pension income is £11,973 per annum for both client and spouse from age 67. Spouse receives £1,100 per month (P10). The planning baseline shows household expenditure of £24,001 first-year regular expenditure with total monthly household expenditure of £1,718 including tax (P24). Access to pensions is available from age 55 currently, rising to 57 from 6 April 2028 (P6). The client is currently 69 years old and therefore already able to access pension benefits. The Zurich policy is due to mature at age 75 (retirement date 7 June 2031, age 75) (P21). The fact find states 'Do you expect any changes in your expenditure in the foreseeable future? No' (P24). No foreseeable life changes, support needs or vulnerability indicators related to the retirement objectives are disclosed in the evidence.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states retirement need of '£1,700 net per month' which equals £20,400 per annum. Employment income of £300/week is £15,600 gross annually (approximately £12,480 net at standard tax rates). Combined with spouse's £1,100/month (£13,200 annually stated in P8), total household net income before pension access appears to be approximately £25,680, creating a marginal monthly cash deficit of £-288 noted in the fact find (P24). The suitability report does not reconcile this shortfall or explain how the stated retirement expenditure of £1,700/month will be met without pension drawdown commencing immediately or before retirement.
      - The report recommends consolidation into a single Aviva Pension Portfolio with transfer value £110,185, but the stated five existing arrangements total £116,998.47 according to derived figures. The report does not account for £6,813.47 unaccounted for in the consolidation, nor does it clarify the treatment of the People's Pension (£6,601.55) which is not mentioned in the recommendation section.
      - The report links all five recommendations to objectives (four to OBJ3, one to OBJ1) but does not provide a specific named objective for each recommendation—only boilerplate language that the plan 'meets your objectives' and 'aligns with your financial goals'. The canonical model shows linkedObjectiveIds but the supporting narrative does not explain which specific objective each recommendation serves.
      - The report identifies the client's age as approximately 69, already at an age where pension access restrictions do not apply (access available from 55, currently 69). However, the recommendation focuses on 'flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown when you access your pension' as a benefit. At age 69, this flexibility is immediate and not future-dependent. The suitability report does not distinguish between current access capability and future access capability.
      - The report states projections to age 75 as a retirement target, but at age 69, this represents only a 6-year investment horizon, not the 'LongTerm' classification assigned to OBJ1. The medium-term classification for OBJ2 and OBJ3 (which have no recommendation linked in the extract) is not explained.
    The report asserts a retirement age of 75 and projects cashflow to that age with no shortfall. However, the client is already 69 years old, placing the retirement date only 6 years away. This is materially shorter than the 'LongTerm' classification assigned to OBJ1. The report does not explain the discrepancy between the stated long-term horizon and the 6-year practical timeframe. The recommendation to consolidate into an advised Aviva Pension Portfolio is justified by reference to flexibility and tax efficiency, but at age 69 with pensions already accessible, the flexibility is not a future benefit but an immediate current one. The report states the client 'understands the pension freedoms and prefer[s] the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown' but does not cite a specific objective this serves or explain why consolidation enhances this preference beyond the generic assertion it provides a 'flexible and tax-efficient means'. The fact find shows current monthly disposable income of £-288, indicating the household expenditure of £1,718/month exceeds net income. The suitability report states retirement expenditure will be 'approximately £1,700' per month but does not reconcile how this is affordable given current shortfall, nor does it clarify whether pension drawdown commences immediately at retirement or later. The cashflow analysis (P10) shows 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement. Meaning your pension will be allowed to keep growing for large ad hoc spends' but this statement contradicts the stated retirement need and does not align with the £1,700/month requirement stated elsewhere. The recommendations are linked to objectives by ID only; the specific objective each recommendation serves is not named. The guard 'require a specific objective to be named' is not satisfied: the recommendations state they 'meet your objectives' and 'align with your financial goals' without naming which objective or explaining why this recommendation and not an alternative serves it. The transfer of £110,185 is asserted but the source arrangement (People's Pension £6,601.55) is not mentioned in the recommendations, leaving unclear whether it is excluded by design or by extraction error. No evidence explains this omission. The planning baseline scenario (P8) shows total regular expenditure of £24,001 in year one including £3,385 tax and NI, but the report's £1,700/month retirement figure (£20,400 annually) excludes tax, creating inconsistency in the stated requirement. The report does not reconcile these two figures.

  [G2.7] Goals, assumptions or planning requirements stated in the report that are not supported by the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts three client objectives: (1) 'Ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime'; (2) 'Retire in 2 years time with a household income of £1,700 net per month'; (3) 'Consolidate pension arrangements and ensure they are appropriately structured for retirement, preferring a streamlined approach to managing pensions rather than maintaining five separate plans'. All three are marked as client-stated and sourced from page 3 'Your Objectives' section. The report also assumes: general expenditure inflation of 2%; savings inflation of 2%; state pensions inflation of 2.5%; Aviva personal pensions inflation of 5%; surplus income percentage saved of 0%; mid-growth rate of 2.94%; and annual expenditure in retirement of approximately £1,700 per month.
    File:     The Fact Find [P5] records under 'Retirement Planning' that the client 'Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month. ATR 5.' and 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has.' File notes [P1] confirm the client's capacity for loss: he receives 'full State Pension of approximately £230 per week, continue[s] to work as an HGV driver earning around £300 per week net' and receives 'an additional £20 per week' from the Sea Cadets. His spouse contributes £1,100 per month and will receive a full State Pension. The Fact Find [P8] records 'Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure' of £1,718.00 and states 'Do you expect any changes in your expenditure in the foreseeable future? No'. The Fact Find [P8] shows 'Total Net Monthly Income' of £1,430.00 and 'Total Monthly Disposable Income' of -£288.00. The assumption page [P20] is referenced in the report at page 19 but detailed contents are not fully extracted.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the client expects to retire 'in 2 years time' and need '£1,700 net per month'. The Fact Find [P5] records the client 'Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years' and 'needs a household income of £1,700 net per month'. The word 'thinks' and 'probably' in the Fact Find suggests uncertainty; the report presents it as a definite objective.
      - The report assumes 'annual expenditure in retirement to be approximately £1,700' per month [P5 shows the figure is £1,700 monthly]. However, the current monthly household expenditure recorded in the Fact Find [P8] is £1,718.00, not £1,700.00. This creates a discrepancy of £18.00 per month between current recorded expenditure and assumed retirement expenditure.
      - The Fact Find [P8] shows current net monthly income of £1,430.00 against monthly expenditure of £1,718.00, creating a monthly deficit of -£288.00 before any pension income is drawn. The report asserts the client can 'continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime' but does not evidence how the household deficit in current circumstances is reconciled with retirement income planning.
    The first objective—'Ensure you can continue to afford your lifestyle through your lifetime'—is a generic lifetime affordability goal. It is supported by the file notes [P1] which confirm the client's capacity for loss discussion and acknowledgment that 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living', given his ongoing state pension and spouse's income. This objective is evidenced in categories B and C. The second objective—retire in 2 years with £1,700 net monthly household income—is explicitly recorded in the Fact Find [P5] as the client's stated need. However, a material discrepancy exists: the Fact Find [P8] records current monthly household expenditure as £1,718.00, while the report assumes retirement expenditure of £1,700 per month. This £18 difference is small in percentage terms (~1%) but represents a logical inconsistency: if current committed expenditure is £1,718, the retirement target of £1,700 assumes a reduction, yet the Fact Find [P8] states 'Do you expect any changes in your expenditure in the foreseeable future? No'. This is not explained in the evidence. The second objective is supported by [P5] (Fact Find) and [P1] (file notes confirming the £1,700 need). The third objective—consolidation and streamlined pension management—is explicitly recorded in [P5] as 'Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has' and reflected in file notes [P1] noting the adviser's understanding of consolidation as the priority. The report's wording 'preferring a streamlined approach to managing pensions rather than maintaining five separate plans' is a translation of the client's stated need for consolidation into formal language, supported by substance across B and C categories. Regarding assumptions: The inflation assumptions (2% general, 2.5% state pension, 5% Aviva) are disclosed on page 19 of the report but the specific evidence linking these to client discussion or standard planning practice is not fully extracted in the passages provided. The mid-growth rate of 2.94% is cited in the report at page 11 but no passage confirms its source. The surplus savings assumption of 0% is stated in the report but the underlying rationale—that the client is currently running a monthly deficit—is evident from [P8] showing -£288.00 disposable income. However, this creates a logical tension: the client cannot save surplus income if there is no surplus. This assumption appears sound given the financial circumstances but lacks explicit articulation. The £1,700 retirement expenditure assumption is taken from [P5] (Fact Find) but the reconciliation with the £1,718 current expenditure and the 'no expected changes' response is not explained. All three objectives are substantively supported by Fact Find [P5] and file notes [P1] in categories B and C. The minor expenditure discrepancy (£18/month) does not contradict the suitability of the strategy but represents incomplete reconciliation of figures. The objectives themselves—consolidation, retirement timing, and income need—are all client-stated and evidenced.

  [G2.8] Target retirement income stated in the report is consistent with the evidence and with the figures the plan is modelled on
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states a target retirement income of £1,700 net per month. This is described as the client's expected annual expenditure in retirement. The report models this as the income requirement needed from pensions and other sources. The assumption is stated as 'In retirement, you expect your annual expenditure to be approximately £1,700' on page 14. The cashflow modeller assumes all surplus income is spent (0% saved). State pension income is modelled at £11,973 per annum. The report confirms 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement.'
    File:     The Fact Find [P2] records under Retirement Planning: 'Needs £1,700 net per month income in retirement' and states the client 'needs a household income of £1,700 net per month.' The Policy Research document [P1] and [P17] confirm 'Needs £1,700 net per month income in retirement.' The Fact Find expenditure analysis [P3] and [P4] calculates total monthly household expenditure as £1,718.00 (sum of all expense categories: Rent £600 + Council Tax £200 + Electricity £120 + Water £60 + Food & Personal Care £300 + Car/Travelling £150 + Building Insurance £25 + Entertainment £100 + other items). Current monthly income is shown as £1,430.00 with a monthly disposable income of -£288.00 (shortfall). The planning model [P20] and [P24] show pension-accessible values and a cashflow chart indicating income and expenditure over the projection period.
    Discrepancies:
      - The Fact Find expenditure analysis [P3] and [P4] records total calculated monthly household expenditure of £1,718.00, which exceeds the stated target retirement income of £1,700 net per month by £18 per month. The report states the target as £1,700 but the Fact Find shows current expenditure at £1,718, a material difference in the base data used for modelling. The report does not reconcile this difference or explain why current expenditure (£1,718) differs from intended retirement income (£1,700).
      - The report states 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement' but the state pension is modelled at £11,973 per annum (approximately £998 per month). This alone cannot cover a £1,700 per month target, requiring approximately £702 per month from other sources. The report's assertion about state pension coverage is not substantiated by the figures modelled.
      - The current disposable income shown in the Fact Find [P4] is -£288.00 per month (income £1,430 less expenditure £1,718), indicating current expenditure exceeds current income. Yet the report models retirement with the same £1,700 target without addressing how this shortfall is currently being managed or will be addressed in retirement.
    The target retirement income of £1,700 net per month is consistently stated across the report and evidence (Fact Find, Policy Research). However, the evidence reveals a material discrepancy: the Fact Find calculates the client's current monthly expenditure at £1,718.00, not £1,700. While the difference appears small (£18 per month), it indicates the modelling may be based on rounded or simplified assumptions rather than the detailed expenditure analysis completed in the Fact Find. The report does not explain this reconciliation. More significantly, the report asserts 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement,' but the modelled state pension of approximately £998 per month cannot cover a £1,700 monthly target without substantial pension drawdown, creating a discrepancy between the narrative claim and the quantified figures. The fact that current expenditure (£1,718) exceeds current income (£1,430) by £288 per month is not addressed in the report's retirement income analysis. This suggests either the current position is unstable, or the client is meeting the shortfall through other means (asset drawdown, other income sources not detailed) that are not explicitly discussed. The evidence supports that the client stated a need for £1,700 per month and this target appears in the modelling, but the foundation for this figure (whether it reflects genuine anticipated retirement spending or is adjusted from current expenditure) is not transparently reconciled between the Fact Find and the advice.

CHK-003 — Risk, Capacity for Loss and Investment Strategy Match
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G3.1, G3.2, G3.3, G3.4, G3.5, G3.6, G3.7, G3.9, G3.10, G3.11). Investment term: [P9] Risk profile report states 'Investment Period 2 years' in projections section, but [P4] Suitability Report and [P4] client statement state '10-year period' for growth objective. Risk override justification: [P4] Suitability Report states override ('We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed...5 rather than a 4'). However, the override is explained as consequent on adviser reassessment and client agreement, not on detailed analysis of why questionnaire was inadequate. The justification for upward override from 4 to 5 is present but sparse: it rests on adviser judgment that questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' without detailed reasoned analysis of assessment grounds. The report asserts a CFL rating of 'Medium' but no documentary evidence of a formal capacity for loss assessment, rating scale, or scoring mechanism appears in the case file. The capacity for loss section in the risk profile report [P20] records only that 'No additional notes were recorded', providing no substantive assessment. 13 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an assessed risk code of 4 and an agreed risk code of 5 on a 1-10 scale, with label 'Balanced'. However, the override is explained as consequent on adviser reassessment and client agreement, not on detailed analysis of why questionnaire was inadequate.   [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts a capacity for loss (CFL) rating of 'Medium'. The assessment basis provided comprises four elements: (1) surplus income from State Pension (£230/week), HGV work (£300/week), and Sea Cadets role (£20/week); (2) combined income comfortably exceeds household expenditure of approximately £1,700/month, with savings of around £6,000 and no outstanding liabilities; (3) client confirmed potential underperformance would not have detrimental impact on day-to-day living; (4) client confirmed retention of sufficient liquid capital to cover unexpected emergencies. The report also references the client's understanding that investment values can rise and fall, and that returns are not guaranteed, limited to workplace pension experience.
    File:     The risk profile report [P19] contains a capacity for loss section [P20] with the note 'No additional notes were recorded'. The Capacity for Loss Questionnaire section [P1] also records 'No additional notes were recorded'. The financial planning documents [P3], [P4], [P6], [P10], [P12] model the baseline and proposed scenarios showing: liquid assets of £6,000 (savings), pension assets of £116,997, total accessible assets of £122,997 in both baseline and proposed scenarios. The proposed scenario projects liquid assets & accessible pensions rising from £122,997 in 2025 to £185,564 by 2056 [P12], with annual in-year surpluses ranging from £20,620 to £21,329 and eventually (2056) showing a deficit of (£2,930) [P12]. Stress tests conducted [P15] modelled a -30% immediate market crash combined with 6% inflation, showing 'no shortfall that requires your pension or investment funds to be used'. Income is documented as: State Pension approximately £230/week [P11], HGV driver earnings £300/week net [P11], Sea Cadets £20/week [P11], and spouse Kim contributes £1,100/month [P11]. Household expenditure is stated as approximately £1,700/month [P11]. No separate capacity for loss questionnaire responses or detailed CFL assessment documentation is present in the file beyond the record that no notes were recorded.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts a CFL rating of 'Medium' but no documentary evidence of a formal capacity for loss assessment, rating scale, or scoring mechanism appears in the case file. The capacity for loss section in the risk profile report [P20] records only that 'No additional notes were recorded', providing no substantive assessment.
      - The report's CFL assessment rationale draws on financial surpluses and client confirmations, but the risk profile document [P1], [P20] contains no corresponding capacity for loss questionnaire responses or adviser notes to corroborate these specific statements about client understanding or confirmation.
      - The report states the client 'confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living' [P11], but no supporting evidence of this confirmation (e.g., meeting notes, questionnaire response, signed acknowledgement) is present in the case file.
      - The report states the client 'have confirmed that you retain sufficient liquid capital to cover any unexpected emergencies' [P11], but no supporting evidence of this confirmation is present in the case file.
      - The CFL rating of 'Medium' is never mapped to a defined scale or range in any document. The report provides no scale definition, benchmark, or explanation of what 'Medium' means operationally or how it was derived from the financial and personal factors cited.
      - The relationship between the client's attitude to risk (rating 5, Balanced) and the capacity for loss (Medium) is not explained. There is no analysis of whether these two ratings are consistent with each other or how they were independently derived.
    A capacity for loss rating of 'Medium' is asserted by the report, and the supporting rationale—surplus income, expenditure coverage, savings buffer, and client confirmation of no detriment to daily living—is drawn from the suitability report itself [P11]. The financial planning documents [P3], [P4], [P6], [P10] corroborate the underlying financial facts: liquid assets of £6,000, pension assets of £116,997, and projected annual surpluses. The stress test [P15] demonstrates the plan can withstand a -30% market shock without requiring drawdown. However, the assessment is weakly evidenced in the formal risk documentation. The risk profile report [P20] contains a 'Capacity for loss for the goal' section but records only 'No additional notes were recorded', meaning no formal capacity for loss assessment questionnaire responses or adviser notes documenting the assessment reasoning are on file. The report's CFL basis rests on financial surpluses and client oral confirmations, but these confirmations are stated without documentary corroboration (no meeting notes, signed acknowledgements, or questionnaire evidence). The CFL rating of 'Medium' itself is never defined against any stated scale in the file—there is no scale showing what 'Medium' means relative to Low, High, or any other category. The risk profile document [P19] explains how ATR levels map to asset class breakdowns, but does not define how CFL ratings map to financial or personal circumstances. Critically, the report's CFL assessment reads as a restatement of the consequence of surplus income rather than an independent analysis of loss-bearing capacity. The guard in the decision logic identifies this risk: 'Capacity for loss restated as a consequence of attitude to risk is a weak assessment'. Here, the report derives CFL ('Medium') from income exceeding expenditure, but does not separately assess tolerance for loss magnitude, volatility exposure over the investment term (stated as 10 years [P11]), or loss scenarios beyond the single -30% shock tested. The attitude to risk rating (5, Balanced) is separately documented [P11], [P19], [P20], having been derived from the ATR questionnaire and adviser reassessment. However, no independent capacity for loss questionnaire responses are evidenced. The report offers no explanation of whether the client's limited investment experience (workplace pension only [P11]) affects their actual capacity to bear losses, nor does it quantify what loss quantum the client considers tolerable (e.g., 'tolerate a loss of X% over Y years'). The financial planning projections [P10], [P12] show the plan succeeds under the baseline and proposed scenarios; however, success of the plan does not, by itself, establish capacity for loss—it establishes suitability of the outcome if the risk level is appropriate. The missing formal CFL assessment and undefined rating scale mean the comparison cannot be fully performed at the documentation level, even though the underlying financial facts are sound and the client's circumstances do support a moderate capacity for loss.
