  [G3.3] Tolerance for loss and need-level risk
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report (P18) asserts: 'John, you are aware that investment values can rise as well as fall, and although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed. You confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living.' The report states the client is in receipt of full State Pension (~£230/week), works as HGV driver earning ~£300/week net, receives £20/week from Sea Cadets role, and spouse contributes £1,100/month from employment. The report asserts an agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced) with a 2-year investment term of £106,000 and recommended funds: CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50%). The report does not quantify a numerical tolerance for loss or model a specific large-loss scenario with defined recovery parameters.
    File:     The Risk profile report (P14, P19) describes the agreed risk level 5 as 'Balanced' investor with definition: 'you do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person and have no strong positive or negative associations with the notion of taking risk.' The questionnaire response section (P11) shows capacity for loss responses but no quantified tolerance for loss value. The capacity for loss questionnaire section notes 'No additional notes were recorded' (P24). The stress test section (P23) models a -30% immediate market crash combined with 6% inflation, concluding 'there would be no shortfall that requires your pension or investment funds to be used.' Financial planning projections (P10, P15) show asset trajectories through retirement but do not isolate or quantify a specific tolerance for loss threshold. The extraction report flags that stress tests were marked as 'NotPresentInDocument' for the path '/riskAssessment/capacityForLoss/stressTestsApplied', yet P23 does contain stress test analysis.
    Discrepancies:
      - The canonical model contains no value for '/riskAssessment/perClient[]/toleranceForLoss' or '/objectives[]/needLevelRisk', indicating these fields were not extracted from the report despite P18 and related passages discussing capacity for loss qualitatively.
      - The extraction metadata flags '/riskAssessment/capacityForLoss/stressTestsApplied' as 'NotPresentInDocument', but P23 explicitly documents stress tests including a -30% market crash scenario and 6% inflation scenario.
      - The report states the agreed risk level is 5 (described as 'Balanced' in P5, P14), but does not explicitly quantify a numerical tolerance for loss (e.g., 'client can tolerate loss up to X%') separate from the attitude to risk descriptor.
      - P18 describes capacity for loss qualitatively ('any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living') without a quantified threshold or modelled loss amount that triggers a rebalancing or withdrawal event.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Explicit quantified tolerance for loss value (e.g., 'client can tolerate 15% loss' or similar numeric threshold), Need-level risk rating or requirement (the canonical model path '/objectives[]/needLevelRisk' is absent), Documented reconciliation between the questionnaire-derived natural risk level (4, per P19) and the agreed risk level (5, per P18) with client written acknowledgement of the increase
    The requirement seeks consistency between the report's assertions on tolerance for loss, need-level risk, attitude to risk, capacity for loss, investment term and recommended strategy, supported by evidence from categories D, B, G or H. The canonical model extraction identified no value at the tolerance-for-loss path, triggering comparisonPerformed=false. While the suitability report (P18, category I) asserts capacity for loss qualitatively—that underperformance would not impact day-to-day living, supported by income and spousal support details—it does not quantify tolerance for loss as a numeric percentage or loss-recovery timeline. The Risk Profile report (P14, P19, category D/G) and Capacity for Loss Questionnaire (P11, category D) provide supporting questionnaire responses but no separate quantified tolerance for loss figure. The extraction metadata contradiction (stress tests marked absent but present in P23) indicates potential capture failures. The report does model scenarios (P23: -30% crash, 6% inflation) but does not frame these as tests of the client's stated tolerance, nor does it define a tolerance threshold that would trigger concern. The override from natural risk level 4 to agreed risk level 5 (P18) is documented with client confirmation ('You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 5') but the phrase 'higher than your agreed risk rating' in P18 introduces ambiguity about which rating was previously 'agreed'. The evidence shows capacity for loss qualitatively assessed and stress-tested, but tolerance for loss is not quantified separately. This is a documentation gap: the requirement can be partially evaluated (capacity for loss is supported), but tolerance for loss cannot be compared because it is not numerically stated in either the report or the evidence.

  [G3.4] Time horizon used to support the risk profile
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The canonical model asserts an investment term of 10 years (/riskAssessment/perClient[]/investmentTermYears: [10]). The objectives recorded include three time horizons: 'LongTerm', 'MediumTerm', and 'MediumTerm' (/objectives[]/timeHorizon: ['LongTerm', 'MediumTerm', 'MediumTerm']).
    File:     [P1] states the natural risk level is 'based on the answers given in the Attitude to Risk Questionnaire which was completed on 18/09/2025' and that projections are produced 'based initially on your natural risk level, investment amount, target and desired time horizon.' [P3] shows an 'Investment Period' of '2 years' in the risk level justification table. [P5] shows the timeline as 'First Year Of Plan' in 2025, 'Retire (Kim)' in 2026 (one year), and 'Retire (John)' in 2027 (two years). The ages at retirement are shown as approximately 70 and 71 respectively (born 1956 and 1960, retiring 2026 and 2027). [P9] and [P10] reference 'agreed risk level' as 5 and confirm it 'considered your natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return for your goal.' [P12] states for the Standard Life illustration: 'Time to retirement: 5 years and 6 months' and 'Retirement age: 75'. [P4], [P6], [P7], [P8] show cashflow projections extending from 2025 through 2060 (35 years), with ages ranging from 69/65 at the start to 104/100 at the end.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts an investment term of 10 years, but [P3] explicitly states 'Investment Period: 2 years' in the risk level justification for the agreed risk level 5 (Balanced).
      - The objectives include both 'LongTerm' and 'MediumTerm' horizons, which are conceptually different; the latter are more consistent with the 2-year and 5.5-year horizons evidenced in the documents.
      - [P5] shows retirement occurring in 2026 (1 year from 2025) and 2027 (2 years from 2025), not 10 years later.
      - [P12] shows time to retirement for the Standard Life illustration as 5 years and 6 months, which differs from both the 10-year assertion and the 2-year investment period.
    The report asserts a 10-year investment term, but multiple passages directly contradict this. [P3] documents an 'Investment Period' of '2 years' in the very table titled 'Risk level justification' where the agreed risk level 5 (Balanced) is calibrated. This 2-year period appears to be the planning horizon for the investment itself. [P5] confirms clients are retiring in 2026 and 2027, which are 1-2 years from the plan start in 2025, not 10 years. The cashflow projections in [P4], [P6], [P7], [P8] extend 35 years into the future (to 2060, ages 104/100), but this is the projection horizon for financial planning post-retirement, not the investment term used to justify the risk profile. [P12] introduces a different figure: 5 years and 6 months to retirement for one illustration, suggesting a different time horizon was used in part of the analysis. The objectives recorded as 'LongTerm', 'MediumTerm', 'MediumTerm' are qualitative descriptors that do not reconcile clearly with any single numerical horizon. The guard allows that money left invested through retirement can have a longer horizon than the retirement date—the 35-year projection supports this interpretation—but the report must explicitly state this if it is the justification. The report does not state that the 10-year term accounts for post-retirement growth; instead, [P3] directly specifies a 2-year investment period for the risk justification. This is a material inconsistency: the time horizon explicitly used to calibrate the agreed risk level (2 years) contradicts the 10-year term asserted in the canonical model.

  [G3.5] Investment strategy and asset allocation
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report recommends an investment strategy with a target risk rating of 5. The recommended portfolio comprises two funds with equal 50% allocations: (1) HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Accumulation with a risk rating of 5 and ongoing charge of 0.18%, and (2) CT Universal MAP Balanced C with a risk rating of 5 and ongoing charge of 0.29%. The strategy is described as a Model Portfolio approach (CIP - Centralised Investment Proposition) designed to align with the client's risk profile, provide diversification, and balance active and passive investments while keeping costs low. The report states the funds reflect the client's risk profile both individually and combined.
    File:     The evidence confirms the same two funds are recommended with identical 50% allocations and the same risk rating of 5 for both funds [P8, P11]. The ongoing charges are corroborated as 0.18% for HSBC and 0.29% for CT Universal MAP [P8, P11]. The SIPP research report [P10] confirms the agreed risk level is 5 and states 'The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 5 which matches the client's agreed risk level' [P10, P16]. Individual fund factsheets confirm the HSBC fund is rated as risk level 3 in its own objective [P12], but the risk profile documentation [P22, P10] confirms the client's agreed risk level following discussion and reassessment is 5. The CT Universal MAP fund KIID [P2] indicates the fund is classified in risk category 4 based on historical price fluctuations, though it describes asset allocation ranges (Equities 45-70%, Bonds 20-55%, Other 0-25%) appropriate for a balanced strategy.
    Discrepancies:
      - The HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio's own fund objective states its risk profile is rated as 3, where 1 is lower risk and 5 is higher risk [P12: 'The Fund's risk profile is rated as 3 where 1 is a lower level of risk and 5 is a higher level of risk'], but the report and supporting research assign it a risk rating of 5 for portfolio construction purposes [P8, P11]. This appears to reflect different risk rating scales: the fund's internal scale (1-5 with 3 as assigned) versus the firm's client risk rating scale (1-5 with 5 as agreed). The CT Universal MAP fund KIID indicates risk category 4 based on historical volatility [P2: 'This Fund is classified in category 4 because its price or simulated data has shown medium fluctuations historically'], not category 5. However, the research report states both funds are risk-rated as 5 in the recommended portfolio [P8, P10].
    The discrepancies identified reflect the interaction of two different risk rating frameworks. The guard provided states: 'Fund-level risk ratings from a provider are not necessarily on the firm's client risk scale.' This applies here. The HSBC fund's own risk profile rating of 3 is its internal fund-level classification on HSBC's 1-5 scale. The report's assignment of risk rating 5 reflects the firm's client risk rating scale, used to construct portfolios aligned with the client's agreed risk tolerance. The SIPP research report [P10, P16] explicitly states the agreed risk level is 5 and confirms 'The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 5 which matches the client's agreed risk level', establishing that a combined portfolio of these two funds targets and meets risk rating 5 on the firm's scale. The guard further states: 'A blend of two funds can target a rating neither fund carries individually — check for a stated combined rating before flagging.' The evidence confirms this: the combined portfolio is explicitly stated to be risk-rated 5 on the client risk scale [P10, P16], matching the client's agreed rating. The CT Universal MAP's KIID category 4 classification is similarly a fund-level risk indicator distinct from the portfolio risk rating. Both funds are confirmed in the research documentation as appropriate selections for risk level 5 portfolios. The allocations (50% HSBC, 50% CT Universal MAP) sum to 100%, and the charges (0.18% and 0.29%) are corroborated. The report's statement that the funds 'reflect your risk profile both individually and combined' is supported by the explicit confirmation that the combined portfolio risk mapping equals the agreed risk level of 5. No override is stated; rather, the funds were selected through a documented research and comparison process [P8, P22]. The investment strategy is consistent with the evidence, properly grounded in the client's agreed risk level following reassessment from an initial questionnaire rating of 4 to an agreed rating of 5 [P22].

  [G3.6] Risk override justification where the agreed rating differs from the questionnaire
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts that the client completed an Attitude to Risk questionnaire rating 4, but the adviser felt this did not fully reflect the client's risk appetite. The agreed risk rating was increased to 5 (Balanced investor). The report states: 'You completed an Attitude to Risk questionnaire. We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire.' The justification offered includes: (1) the client's awareness of volatility ('You are aware that your funds may go up as well as down, and the higher risk you take with your funds, potential there is for larger drops in value'); (2) client confirmation of the increase ('You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5- Balanced investor, you are aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating and are happy to take on this risk with your pension'); and (3) client acknowledgement of objectives ('You have confirmed that you are not reliant upon your pension funds and wish for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period and feel a balanced approach to this investment would achieve this goal').
    File:     The Risk profile report [P2] documents the natural risk level based on the Attitude to Risk questionnaire completed on 18/09/2025 as rating 4 (Cautious Balanced). [P14] provides the questionnaire responses confirming rating 4 across multiple answers (Cautious investment approach, More than £4,000 anxiety threshold on £20,000 investment, association of investment risk with caution, more cautious self-description). The Risk profile report states: 'By using these projections and discussions, we came to an agreed risk level for your specific investment objective' and documents the agreed risk level as 5 (Balanced). [P2] shows that the process involved discussing projections 'based initially on your natural risk level, investment amount, target and desired time horizon. We then discussed this projection and altered any factor, including the risk level, until we agreed that the right balance had been achieved.' However, [P1] and [P3] record 'No additional notes were recorded' under 'Agreed risk level' and 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' sections respectively, providing no documentary evidence of the reasoning, discussion content, or explicit client confirmation during that conversation. [P6] describes the risk level 5 (Balanced) characteristics but provides no specific justification for why the override from 4 to 5 was necessary or appropriate for this client.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the override was justified by the client's awareness of volatility and confirmation, but the Risk profile report's notes sections record 'No additional notes were recorded' under both 'Agreed risk level' and 'Capacity for loss questionnaire', creating a gap between the reported justification content and documented evidence of that discussion.
      - The report states the adviser 'felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite', but no file evidence explains what specific aspects of the questionnaire result were deemed insufficient or what client-specific factors prompted the upward reassessment.
    The guard states: 'Where assessed and agreed ratings match, this group is not applicable — do not report a missing justification for an override that did not occur.' Here, assessed and agreed ratings do NOT match: questionnaire result is 4, agreed rating is 5. An override has occurred and justification is required. The suitability report [P4] and [P8] assert client awareness of volatility and client confirmation of the increase, but these are internal consistency claims (the report quoting itself about what the client said). [P2] and [P6] confirm that a risk level 5 was agreed and that discussions occurred, but the specific notes sections in [P1] and [P3] record no additional documentation of those discussions. The Risk profile report [P2] describes the general process ('We then discussed this projection and altered any factor, including the risk level, until we agreed that the right balance had been achieved') but does not contain contemporaneous notes explaining why, for this specific client, the override from 4 to 5 was warranted. The file evidence shows: (1) questionnaire completed 18/09/2025 rating 4; (2) agreed risk level documented as 5; (3) the process framework for discussing and agreeing risk levels is described; but (4) the specific reasoning for this client's override and the supporting discussion content are not recorded in the file. The report's justifications rest on client statements (awareness, confirmation, objectives) that are recorded only within the report itself [P4], not independently documented in the supporting files. This constitutes a missing component: the file does not record the substance of the discussion that led to the override, only that an override occurred and a risk level was agreed.

  [G3.7] Recommendation or strategy exceeds risk tolerance, taking account of capacity for loss
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an agreed risk rating of 5 (Balanced investor). The client completed an Attitude to Risk questionnaire initially scoring 4, which the adviser felt did not fully reflect the client's risk appetite. The report states: 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire.' The client confirmed agreement with rating 5. The recommended solution is an Aviva Pension Portfolio with target risk rating 5. Capacity for loss is assessed as 'Medium'. The report notes: 'You confirmed that any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living.' Investment term is 10 years. The existing arrangements hold five pensions with stated risk ratings of 9, 4, 2, 7, and 4 (Standard Life rated).
    File:     The Risk profile report [P3] documents a natural risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) from the Attitude to Risk questionnaire completed on 18/09/2025. The questionnaire responses show the client selected 'Cautious' for investment approach, 'Caution' for association with investment risk, and 'More cautious' compared to others [P24]. The SIPP research report [P1] and [P2] confirm an agreed risk level of 5 following discussion, noting 'the natural risk level, which is ascertained from the psychometric attitude to risk questionnaire score, is used as a starting point to discuss the actual level of risk you are willing and able to take.' The same documents state the client is a 'Balanced investor' at risk level 5. Capacity for loss is not quantified in the retrieved passages beyond the general statement in the suitability report itself [P5]. The recommended funds—CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50%)—are described as 'Balanced' investments [P1]. The Pension Switch report [P7] and [P18] confirm 'the client's agreed risk level is 5' for both Standard Life and Zurich arrangements.
    Discrepancies:
      - The questionnaire natural risk rating (4) differs materially from the agreed rating (5), representing a one-level upward override
      - The existing Zurich arrangement is described in the arrangements table with risk rating 7, which exceeds the agreed risk rating of 5
      - The existing Aviva arrangement is listed with risk rating 9, which substantially exceeds the agreed rating of 5 and the client's capacity for loss of Medium
      - The existing People's Pension is listed with risk rating 4, which is below the agreed rating of 5
      - The existing Scottish Widows arrangement is listed with risk rating 2, which is substantially below the agreed rating of 5
      - The risk ratings of existing arrangements span 2 to 9 on what appears to be a different scale than the 1-5 scale used for the new recommendation
      - No mapping between the 1-5 scale (questionnaire, agreed, recommended) and the 1-9 scale (existing arrangements) is provided
      - Capacity for loss is stated as 'Medium' in the canonical model but is not explicitly quantified or related to a loss scenario in any retrieved passage
    The comparison can be performed because the agreed risk rating (5), recommended strategy risk rating (5), and capacity for loss classification (Medium) are all stated. However, material issues emerge. First, the questionnaire produced a natural risk rating of 4 (Cautious Balanced), evidenced in [P3] and [P24]. The suitability report overrides this upward to 5, stating 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite' [P5]. The override is explained but not robustly evidenced: the report provides no stochastic projection, stress test, or detailed financial rationale showing why the override is justified given the client's circumstances. The report does document client acknowledgement ('You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 5'), but this is a statement of agreement, not evidence of capacity. Second, the existing five pension arrangements are rated 9, 4, 2, 7, and 4 respectively. These appear to be on a different scale (1-9) than the new recommendation (1-5 scale). The largest existing arrangement, Zurich at £103,439.24, is rated 7—which exceeds the agreed rating of 5. The second-largest, Aviva at £3,744.36, is rated 9, far exceeding the agreed profile. No mapping between scales is provided, making it impossible to determine whether the client's existing portfolio risk already exceeded their capacity for loss before this recommendation. Third, capacity for loss is classified as 'Medium' but never quantified. The report states income sources (State Pension £230/week, employment £300/week HGV driving, spousal contribution £1,100/month), household expenditure approximately £1,700/month, and that 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living.' However, no stress test, scenario analysis or maximum loss tolerance (e.g., 'can tolerate losses up to £X or Y%') is documented. The suitability report itself states the questionnaire responses are in the appendix of the Risk profile report [P3], but the individual question on loss tolerance—'If you invested £20,000 and the value fell temporarily, how much of a fall would make you feel anxious?'—shows the response was 'More than £4,000,' indicating tolerance for a 20% fall on that example. This is consistent with a Balanced profile but does not constitute a quantified capacity for loss assessment. The vulnerability overlay (CHK-002 instruction) asks whether any actual or potential drivers of vulnerability affect the suitability assessment. The case file does not identify any recorded vulnerabilities, support needs, or communication requirements that would materially affect this check. The alignment between agreed risk (5), recommended strategy (5 Balanced), and asset allocation (50/50 Balanced funds) is internally consistent. The report's statement that the strategy 'aligns' with the risk profile is supported by the fact both use the same rating. However, the basis for the upward override and the lack of quantified capacity for loss assessment are material gaps. The existing arrangements' risk ratings on an incomparable scale, combined with the Zurich plan (7) and Aviva plan (9) exceeding the agreed rating, raise a concern about whether the portfolio as a whole—including retained existing plans—remains aligned with the client's risk tolerance.

  [G3.9] Recommended funds and portfolio align with the client's attitude to risk, capacity for loss and investment term
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report recommends a 50/50 portfolio of HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (risk rating 5, 0.18% charge) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (risk rating 5, 0.29% charge). Both funds are stated to have a risk rating of 5 on a Balanced scale. The report asserts the client has an agreed risk rating of 5 (overridden from an initial questionnaire score of 4), a medium capacity for loss, and a 10-year investment term. The report states: 'The funds reflect your risk profile both individually and combined. This means that should there be any portfolio drift, the risk being taken remains the same.' The portfolio is described as providing diversification across asset classes, a blend of active and passive investments, and alignment with the client's financial goals and risk profile.
    File:     The SIPP research report [P12] confirms the agreed risk level is 5 Balanced and lists the same two funds at 50/50 allocation. The research report [P6] explains that risk levels are mapped to asset class collections and that projections are produced using Hymans Robertson modelling. The risk profile report [P15] documents the natural risk level as 4 Cautious Balanced from the questionnaire, with an agreed level following discussion. Fund factsheets [P1], [P9] show HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio is internally rated as a 3 on a 1-5 scale (where 5 is higher risk), not a 5. The KIID [P9] states: 'This is one of a range of actively managed Global Strategy Portfolios offered at five different risk levels. The Fund is rated as 3, where 1 is a lower level of risk and 5 is a higher level of risk.' Performance data [P4] shows combined performance and individual fund performance for both recommended funds. The research report [P12] shows the funds are described as mapped to risk level 5 Balanced for this client's portfolio, but no independent validation of the CT Universal MAP Balanced C risk rating is provided in the passages. The investment term of 10 years is inferred from the projections [18] but not explicitly stated in the evidence pack passages.
    Discrepancies:
      - The HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C is rated as 3 on its own Risk and Reward Indicator scale (1-5 where 5 is higher risk), per [P9], but the report asserts it has a risk rating of 5. These are on different scales: the fund's internal SRRI is 3, whilst the profiler's agreed risk level is 5 Balanced.
      - The report does not disclose or explain that the fund's own Risk and Reward Indicator (3 on a 1-5 scale) differs from the agreed client risk level (5 on a different Balanced scale). This scale difference is not made explicit in the suitability report.
      - No evidence is provided in the pack for the CT Universal MAP Balanced C fund's own Risk and Reward Indicator or SRRI rating to enable comparison with the agreed risk level of 5.
    The comparison can be performed on the HSBC fund because its KIID is in the pack. The guard states: 'A fund's SRRI or risk-and-reward indicator runs on its own scale and does not map onto the profiler's; use it as corroboration, never as a value match.' The HSBC fund's Risk and Reward Indicator of 3 (on its 1-5 scale) and the agreed risk level of 5 Balanced (on a different profiler scale) are not contradictory when this guard is applied—they operate on different scales and the lower SRRI does not invalidate the suitability if the profiler's risk mapping is sound. However, the report does not explicitly acknowledge or explain this distinction to the reader, which leaves the apparent mismatch unexplained in the advisory narrative itself. The report asserts both funds have 'risk rating 5' without clarifying that the HSBC fund's own SRRI is 3. The guard protects this from being a false-positive mismatch on the numbers, but the lack of transparency about scale differences in the report's own statements is a disclosure shortfall. For CT Universal MAP Balanced C, no KIID or risk factsheet is provided in the evidence, so alignment cannot be corroborated. The capacity for loss is assessed as medium, the investment term is 10 years, and the recommended portfolio is a multi-asset model at risk level 5 Balanced per [P12] and [P6], which is consistent with the agreed rating. The projections at [18] show three scenarios (Low 0%, Mid 2.95%, High 5.88%) with outcomes ranging from £97,600 to £135,000 at age 75, which are based on the agreed risk level. The funds' combined performance [P4] shows discrete returns over 2021-2024 and YTD figures, corroborating that they have performed and are available. The report's rationale links the strategy to the risk profile, diversification, cost efficiency and simplicity, which are documented. The investment term of 10 years aligns with a Balanced portfolio and multi-asset structure appropriate for medium-term retirement planning.

  [G3.10] Unsupported, contradictory or unexplained statements about risk, loss capacity, term or strategy
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) Attitude to Risk: questionnaire result was 4 (Cautious Balanced); adviser assessed as 5; client agreed to 5 ('We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire'); later states 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5... you are aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating'. (2) Capacity for Loss: rated as 'Medium', based on surplus income (State Pension £230/week + HGV work £300/week + Sea Cadets £20/week, plus spouse income £1,100/month), household expenditure £1,700/month, savings £6,000, no liabilities, confirmed underperformance would not impact day-to-day living, liquid capital retained for emergencies. Report states 'We therefore agreed that you have a medium capacity for loss, consistent with your agreed Attitude to Risk level of 5'. (3) Investment Term: 10 years ('best growth potential over a 10-year period'). (4) Recommended Strategy: 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (risk rating 5, 0.18% charge) + 50% CT Universal MAP Balanced C (risk rating 5, 0.29% charge). Report states funds 'reflect your risk profile both individually and combined' and are 'risk-profiled solutions, meaning they are specifically designed to remain within your agreed level of risk'.
    File:     Evidence from supporting documents: (1) Risk Profile Report [P1, P8] shows 'Natural risk level' assessed as 4 (Cautious Balanced) from questionnaire completed 18/09/2025. Same document [P8] shows Agreed Risk Level as 5 (Balanced) with notation that this resulted from discussion where 'we altered any factor, including the risk level, until we agreed that the right balance had been achieved'. [P8] projects 2-year investment period with £106,000 investment and notes 1.5% estimated total charge. (2) SIPP Research Report [P2, P5] confirms agreed risk level 5 (Balanced) after 'interactive discussion' considering 'natural attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return'. [P5] lists same two funds: CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc 50%, HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C 50%. (3) File notes [P7] record 'Aviva current risk – 2', 'Zurich current risk – 7', 'Standard Life current risk – 4', with no notes on capacity for loss assessment methodology. (4) Risk Profile Report [P4] states under 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' section: 'No additional notes were recorded'. [P3] states under 'Agreed risk level' section: 'No additional notes were recorded'. (5) Both funds are confirmed as risk rating 5 in the research documents [P5, P18]. (6) Capacity for Loss questionnaire responses [P15] show client answered questions but specific responses are truncated in evidence. No stress testing or loss scenario modelling appears in retrieved passages.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states investment term is 10 years ('best growth potential over a 10-year period'), but the Risk Profile Report [P8] models a 2-year investment period ('Investment Period | 2 years'). These are materially inconsistent.
      - The report's phrasing 'this is higher than your agreed risk rating' in the client confirmation is ambiguous. The questionnaire result was 4, the agreed rating is 5. The phrase could refer to 5 being higher than 4 (questionnaire result) or higher than some prior agreement, creating internal inconsistency about what was 'agreed' beforehand.
      - The Risk Profile Report [P8] notes that projections 'take account of an estimated annual total charge of 1.5%' but the recommended solution in the Suitability Report [P18] lists fund charges of 0.18% + 0.29% = 0.47%, far below 1.5%. The basis for the 1.5% estimate is not explained and no reconciliation to actual charges is provided.
    1. Investment Term Discrepancy: The most material inconsistency is the investment period. The report asserts the client 'wish[es] for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period', but the Risk Profile Report [P8]—which is a supporting document from the same adviser—explicitly models 'Investment Period | 2 years'. This is not a matter of rounding or presentation; 2 years and 10 years are fundamentally different time horizons that would materially affect both the risk assessment and the suitability of a Balanced strategy. The 2-year period would suggest lower capacity for recovery from losses. This is an unsupported contradiction between the report and its own evidence base. 2. Attitude to Risk Override: The override from rating 4 to rating 5 is documented and the client's acknowledgement is recorded. The phrasing 'higher than your agreed risk rating' is ambiguous—it may refer to 5 being higher than the initial questionnaire result (4), but the word 'agreed' appears to conflate two different prior states. However, the evidence shows the adviser discussed the mismatch, the client confirmed awareness, and client acknowledgement is recorded in both the report and supporting documents. This is internally consistent despite the awkward phrasing; the override is explained and evidenced. 3. Capacity for Loss: The report rates this as 'Medium' and supports it with income, expenditure, savings and client confirmation of no detriment to day-to-day living. The methodology is sound and supported by [P1], [P8] and [P17]. No stress testing or quantified loss scenario is present in the file, but this is not contradicted by any evidence; it is an absence rather than a contradiction. The claim that CFL is 'consistent with your agreed Attitude to Risk level of 5' is supported by the evidence base showing both were agreed through discussion. 4. Investment Strategy and Funds: Both funds are confirmed as risk rating 5 in the supporting documents [P5, P18]. The report states they 'reflect your risk profile both individually and combined', which aligns with the fund documentation and research reports. No contradiction found. 5. Charge Projection: The Risk Profile Report projects 1.5% total charge for modelling purposes, but the actual recommended charges are 0.47%. This is not a contradiction of the recommendation itself but a gap in explanation of the variance. The 1.5% appears to be a standard assumption for illustration, not a claim about the actual cost. No statement in the report asserts the total charge will be 1.5%, so this is not an unsupported statement. 6. The 2-year vs 10-year term discrepancy stands unresolved and is material to the suitability assessment. A Balanced strategy (risk 5) suited for a 2-year horizon differs significantly from one suited for a 10-year horizon in terms of expected volatility and recovery time. This is an unsupported contradiction.

  [G3.11] Funds inside a retained arrangement align with the agreed risk rating
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts five existing arrangements with the following actions and fund risk ratings: (1) People's Pension - Retain, risk rating 9, holding 'Global Investments (up to 85% shares)'; (2) Scottish Widows - Encash, risk rating 4, holding 'SW Pension Portfolio Four CS1 & SW Pension Portfolio Three CS1'; (3) Aviva - Switch, risk rating 2, holding 'Aviva pension my future focus consolidation'; (4) Zurich - Switch, risk rating 7, holding 'Managed AP'; (5) Standard Life - Switch, risk rating 4 (Standard life rated scale), holding 'SL at retirement universal (PP 10 year) pension fund'. The report's agreed risk level for the client is 5 (Balanced investor). The report retains only the People's Pension arrangement, stating 'This is your current workplace scheme, and you and your employer are still contributing to this and will continue to until your retirement. If you were to transfer away, you would miss out on the valuable benefit of your employer's contributions.'
    File:     Evidence from the case file establishes: (1) The client's agreed risk level is 5, described in [P10] as 'Balanced investor' who 'do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person and have no strong positive or negative associations with the notion of taking risk' and in [P4] as part of an agreed process mapping risk levels to asset class collections. (2) The People's Pension arrangement is retained. (3) Risk ratings appear on multiple scales: [P9] and [P10] reference risk level 5 on an unspecified scale; [P1] shows funds rated 4, 5, 6, 7 on a 1-7 Risk and Reward Indicator scale; [P11] references Aviva fund at risk rating 4 and Zurich blend of 7 and 2 to create 5, with no multi-asset funds available in the client's risk range at Zurich; [P12] confirms agreed risk level is 5 for Standard Life comparison. The retained People's Pension holds 'Global Investments (up to 85% shares)' with no corroborating fund-level risk rating in the evidence. No KIID, factsheet or fund documentation for the People's Pension Global Investments fund is provided in the case file.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the People's Pension (retained arrangement) holds a fund rated 9 on an unspecified scale, but no evidence in the case file corroborates this rating or maps it to the client's agreed risk level 5.
      - The report does not disclose what scale the rating 9 represents (e.g., whether it is a 1-10 scale, a 1-7 scale like the KIIDs, or a provider-specific scale).
      - The People's Pension fund 'Global Investments (up to 85% shares)' is described by asset allocation range only; no risk rating document (KIID, factsheet or fund literature) is provided to verify the claimed rating 9.
      - The agreed risk level 5 (Balanced investor) is documented in [P10] and [P4], but the scale and methodology for rating 9 are never specified in the report or evidence, making direct comparison impossible.
      - The report retains the People's Pension with rationale focused on employer contributions and scheme continuity, but does not explicitly address whether the retained fund's risk profile aligns with the agreed rating 5, leaving an implicit alignment claim unsupported.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Risk rating scale definition for the People's Pension fund rating of 9 (unspecified whether 1-10, 1-9, or another scale)., Fund-level risk documentation (KIID, factsheet, Key Information Document or equivalent) for the People's Pension 'Global Investments (up to 85% shares)' fund showing its actual risk rating or asset allocation profile., Explicit statement from the report affirming or denying that the retained People's Pension fund aligns with the client's agreed risk level 5, or explanation of any override.
    The requirement asks whether funds in retained arrangements align with the agreed risk rating. The People's Pension is the only arrangement retained on advice. The report asserts it holds a fund rated 9, and the client's agreed risk level is 5 (Balanced investor, established in [P10] and [P4]). However, three critical issues prevent the comparison from being made: First, the report never defines what scale the rating 9 sits on. The evidence shows multiple scales in use: [P1] references a 1-7 Risk and Reward Indicator, [P9] and [P10] reference level 5 without specifying scale boundaries, and [P11] references Aviva rating 4 and Zurich blend of 7 and 2. Without a declared scale for the 9, no reliable comparison to level 5 can be made. Second, no fund-level evidence for the People's Pension Global Investments fund exists in the case file. The evidence contains KIIDs and factsheets for other funds (e.g., [P1], [P3], [P5], [P8]) but none for the retained fund. A 1-7 KIID rating, an OCF breakdown, or asset allocation confirmation would clarify the fund's actual risk profile. Third, the report does not explicitly state whether it is claiming the retained fund aligns with level 5 or explaining any override. The rationale given (employer contributions, scheme continuity) explains why the fund is retained but does not address risk alignment. Per the guard 'a retained plan carries the same alignment duty as a recommended one' and 'where the report gives one and says nothing about the fund's risk level, that is the finding.' The report asserts a rating 9 but never maps it to the agreed 5, never discloses the scale, and provides no fund evidence to support the rating. This is an unsupported assertion of the fund's risk profile, compounded by the absence of a documented comparison or override acknowledgement. No evidence corroborates that the People's Pension holding is appropriate for a Balanced (level 5) investor with the agreed risk profile.

CHK-004 — Knowledge, Experience and Product Complexity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G4.1, G4.2, G4.3, G4.4, G4.5, G4.6, G4.7). Report describes client as having 'Moderate' knowledge and suitable for a Balanced (risk 5) portfolio 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters'. Questionnaire records direct answer 'No understanding / knowledge' to 'When it comes to your experience and understanding of investing, how would you describe yourself?'. Report implies client has investment experience and understanding. Questionnaire shows no investment in stocks and shares, unit trusts, or pension products in the last 5 years — only basic cash/savings account experience. Report states client 'understand[s] that returns are not guaranteed' and frames comprehension. Questionnaire shows 'No understanding / knowledge' to understanding how stocks and shares work, unit trusts work, and pension planning works — the core product types and risks relevant to the recommended balanced investment. 21 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G4.1] Client's stated product and investment knowledge level
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states the client has 'Moderate' knowledge level. It asserts: 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach to investing.' The report describes the client as comfortable proceeding with risk rating 5 and states 'you understand that returns are not guaranteed' and frames the client as capable of managing a balanced investment portfolio.
    File:     The Risk Profile questionnaire (P1, P10) documents the client's responses to direct questions about investment knowledge and experience: 'When it comes to your experience and understanding of investing, how would you describe yourself? — No understanding / knowledge'. On specific product types: 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work and do you know the risks associated with them? — No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them? — No understanding / knowledge'; 'Do you understand how Unit Trusts and General Investment Accounts work and do you know the risks associated with them? — No understanding / knowledge'. On experience: 'Have you invested in stocks and shares within the last 5 years? — No'; 'Have you invested in Unit Trusts and General Investment Accounts within the last 5 years? — No'; 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years? — No'. The only investment experience recorded is 'Have you invested in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings and Investments within the last 5 years? — Yes, by myself'. The Fact Find (P7) contains no narrative assessment of the client's knowledge or experience level. File notes (P3, P4) record 'No additional notes were recorded' under both 'Investor Experience' and 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' sections, indicating no verbal elaboration or override was documented.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report describes client as having 'Moderate' knowledge and suitable for a Balanced (risk 5) portfolio 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters'. Questionnaire records direct answer 'No understanding / knowledge' to 'When it comes to your experience and understanding of investing, how would you describe yourself?'
      - Report implies client has investment experience and understanding. Questionnaire shows no investment in stocks and shares, unit trusts, or pension products in the last 5 years — only basic cash/savings account experience.
      - Report states client 'understand[s] that returns are not guaranteed' and frames comprehension. Questionnaire shows 'No understanding / knowledge' to understanding how stocks and shares work, unit trusts work, and pension planning works — the core product types and risks relevant to the recommended balanced investment.
      - Report recommends complex balanced portfolio (CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio) described in research as suitable where client has 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' (P12). Questionnaire evidence shows no such experience or understanding.
    The report's assertion of 'Moderate' knowledge is directly contradicted by contemporaneous questionnaire evidence. The questionnaire (completed 18/09/2025, same date as the meeting per P7) is a structured assessment tool with direct yes/no responses to specific knowledge questions. The client answered 'No understanding / knowledge' to the general question about investment understanding and to all product-specific knowledge questions (stocks/shares, unit trusts, pension planning). This is not a self-deprecating comment or a reserved position: it is a direct answer to a specific question in the investor experience section. The guards note that self-assessed comfort differs from assessed knowledge, and that questionnaire wording recording 'No understanding / knowledge' is an assessed answer, not a side remark. The report's description of the client as suitable for a Balanced approach 'If you have some experience of investments and a degree of understanding' directly invokes the very criteria the questionnaire shows the client does not meet. The only relevant investment experience on file is basic cash/savings products. The file notes show no override, clarification, or verbal reassessment of the questionnaire answers — sections for investor experience and capacity for loss both record 'No additional notes were recorded', indicating the questionnaire responses were accepted without elaboration. The report's narrative that the client 'understand[s] that returns are not guaranteed' is corroborated only by general capacity-for-loss discussion (P15), not by knowledge of how investments work. Recommending a complex balanced portfolio (two actively managed multi-asset funds with multiple underlying holdings, as shown in P12 and P13) to a client assessed as having 'no understanding / knowledge' of stocks and shares, unit trusts, and pension products represents a material mismatch between stated knowledge level and recommended product complexity. No evidence of comprehension checks or product-specific explanation is cited in category C documents.

  [G4.2] Previous investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the client: (1) has previously taken out cash savings products [confirmed]; (2) has NOT taken out stocks & shares investments by himself [stated]; (3) has held group pensions for some time and feels comfortable with how investments work and has reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards [stated]. The report recommends consolidating five pension arrangements into new solutions, including a balanced portfolio (risk rating 5).
    File:     The Risk Profile Report [P1] records that the client has: (1) 'No understanding / knowledge' of how stocks and shares work and risks associated; (2) 'No understanding / knowledge' of how pension planning and income in retirement works and risks associated; (3) 'Yes, by myself' for Current Accounts and Savings/Deposit Accounts investments in last 5 years; (4) 'No' for stocks and shares in last 5 years; (5) 'No' for pension planning and income in retirement products in last 5 years. The Risk Profile Report also confirms the client has 'No additional notes recorded' regarding investor experience and capacity for loss discussions [P7, P16, P20].     Discrepancies:
      - Report states client 'feel[s] that you are comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' but Risk Profile Report records client answered 'No understanding / knowledge' to 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them?'
      - Report asserts client held group pensions and this evidences comfort with investments and understanding of risks, but Risk Profile Report records 'No' response to 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?'
      - Report describes client knowledge level as enabling suitability for a Balanced (risk rating 5) portfolio, but Risk Profile Report records 'No understanding / knowledge' to multiple key investment knowledge questions including stocks and shares and pension planning
    The report's assertion that the client 'feel[s] that you are comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' based on holding group pensions conflicts materially with the Risk Profile Report evidence. It explicitly records the client's answers as 'No understanding / knowledge' for both (1) stocks and shares and (2) pension planning and income in retirement—the precise domains the report invokes to support comfort and understanding. The guard states that 'holding a workplace pension is passive experience; a report distinguishing it from self-directed investing is being accurate, not inconsistent.' However, the report does not distinguish passive from active experience; it asserts the client has 'reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' based on tenure alone, a claim the questionnaire contradicts. The report also asserts the client's knowledge is consistent with a risk rating 5 (Balanced) portfolio, but the Risk Profile Report [P1] records the client's natural risk level as 4 (Cautious Balanced) initially, and [P11] describes that risk level 5 suits investors 'if you have some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters.' The evidence shows the client records no understanding of pension planning—a material contradiction where the entire recommendation centres on pension consolidation and the client is being asked to transfer five existing arrangements. The Risk Profile Report [P7, P16, P20] also records 'No additional notes were recorded' in sections for investor experience discussion, agreed risk level discussion, and capacity for loss discussion—meaning no documented rationale for how the client's limited recorded knowledge was reconciled with the Risk rating 5 agreement or the complex pension transfers recommended. Quote from report [I]: 'You have however held your group pensions for some time and feel that you are comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards.' This directly contradicts [P1] where client stated 'No understanding / knowledge' to pension planning. The recommendation of multiple pension transfers to a client recording zero understanding of pension planning and income in retirement represents a material gap between stated knowledge and recorded capability.

  [G4.3] Complexity of recommended products and investments, including risks and features
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the recommended Aviva Personal Pension Platform is a Complex product. The complexity drivers identified are: (1) Flexible drawdown access; (2) Wide range of investment funds (6,900+ available); (3) Multi-asset fund selection; (4) Tax planning considerations; (5) Death benefit options; (6) Inheritance Tax implications from 6 April 2027. The report states the client 'understand[s] the pension freedoms and prefer[s] the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown' and describes the product as offering 'flexibility [that] provides greater scope to tailor the pension plan to the individual's needs and circumstances.' The report indicates ongoing adviser relationship and discusses comprehension of tax treatment, benefit options, and drawdown mechanics.
    File:     The evidence pack contains extensive product literature describing the Aviva Pension Portfolio's features and options. P1 documents flexi-access drawdown mechanics including single drawdown, phased drawdown, and capped drawdown, with explanations of crystallisation, tax-free lump sums (25%), and taxable withdrawal mechanics. P4 (the suitability report itself) restates these features and explains lifetime allowance replacement, annual allowance (£60,000), and the tax-efficient characteristics of flexi-access drawdown. P20 and P21 confirm the Key Features and charges. P19 shows two balanced multi-asset funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund) forming the investment basis. P8 describes the CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund as actively managed with exposure to equities 30%-70%, fixed income, collective investment schemes, REITs and derivatives. The terms and conditions (P2, P3, P5, P7, P10, P11, P13, P14, P15) detail administrative, charge, and investment rules. The client is described in the canonical model as having 'moderate experience' but the file contains no fact-find, prior investment history, knowledge assessment, risk questionnaire, or evidence of prior pension or investment holdings beyond the five arrangements being transferred. There is no record of client questions, explanations, or demonstration of comprehension of the complex features being transferred into.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts product complexity ('Complex') driven by 6,900+ fund options and flexibility, but no evidence in file demonstrates the client's prior investment knowledge or experience that would support understanding of such complexity.
      - Report states client 'understand[s] the pension freedoms' but file contains no knowledge assessment, questionnaire, or documentation of how this understanding was established or tested.
      - Report emphasizes tax planning and Inheritance Tax implications as complexity drivers, but no evidence shows whether client was offered or received explanations of these features or demonstrated understanding of them.
      - Report describes client preference for 'flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown' but file contains no signed agreement, questionnaire response, or corroborating statement confirming this preference was elicited and understood.
      - Product literature (P1, P20, P21) details multiple drawdown options (single, phased, capped) and their different tax mechanics, but no evidence shows which option client selected or why, or that client understood the differences.
      - Canonical model indicates client has 'moderate experience' but file provides no fact-find, investment history, existing holdings documentation, or prior pension experience to support this characterization.
      - Report recommends transfer of five existing pension arrangements totalling £116,998.47 into a single platform with 6,900+ fund options but file contains no evidence of client questions about consolidation risks, complexity of managing multiple funds, or comprehension of the new platform's scope.
    The requirement is to establish whether the recommended product's complexity is consistent with the client's demonstrated knowledge and experience, and whether comprehension is corroborated where the product is complex. The report correctly identifies the Aviva Personal Pension Platform as a complex product: flexi-access drawdown with 6,900+ investment options, multi-asset funds with derivatives exposure, flexible crystallisation and taxation rules, and inheritance tax planning features are inherently complex features requiring investment knowledge and ongoing engagement. The report asserts the client understands pension freedoms and prefers drawdown flexibility. However, the file does not corroborate this assertion with any fact-find, knowledge assessment, or prior investment history. The canonical model states the client has 'moderate experience,' but no supporting evidence appears: no prior pension statements, no prior investment holdings, no knowledge questionnaire, no adviser interview notes. The product literature thoroughly documents the features and risks (P1, P4, P8, P20, P21, P22, P23, P24), but there is no evidence that the client was taken through these features, asked whether they understood them, or confirmed comprehension. The report does not cite a knowledge assessment. The vulnerability overlay requires consideration of whether support needs or communication requirements affect the assessment: the file provides no evidence of vulnerability screening, support needs identification, or communication adjustments. The comparison cannot definitively establish a mismatch between the product's complexity and the client's capability because the file simply does not document the client's knowledge, experience, or comprehension. The report asserts understanding and preference but offers no corroboration. This is not a claim that can be checked against the file; it is an absence of evidence where evidence is required. A complex product recommended to a client whose knowledge level is not established in the file, and for whom no record of comprehension exists, presents a material gap in the suitability assessment. The standard is that 'comprehension is corroborated in category C where the product is complex' (decision logic); no such corroboration appears. This falls within the Potential Concern band: material clarification or additional evidence required. The severity is Moderate because the file contains no record of the client questioning or understanding complex features where the product warrants it, not because the file contradicts the report's assertion—it simply provides no evidence to support it.

  [G4.4] Recommended product is too complex for the stated level of knowledge, or the knowledge level has been misinterpreted
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states the client has 'Moderate' knowledge and experience [/knowledgeAndExperience/perClient[]/statedLevel]. The recommended products are described as 'Complex' [/knowledgeAndExperience/recommendedProductComplexity[]/complexity]. The report states: 'In addition to your attitude to investment risk and capacity for loss, I have also considered your investment objectives as well as your knowledge and experience, and the length of time you wish to invest for' [P5]. The recommended funds are HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%), both risk-rated at level 5. The report justifies complexity by stating 'You want to keep the investment strategy of your pension fairly simple' and notes 'The CIP aligns with your long-term goals and offers a more robust and flexible framework compared to a single multi-asset fund' [P8]. The report asserts the client 'have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' [P7].
    File:     The Risk Profile questionnaire responses [P9] show the client answered 'No understanding / knowledge' to three critical questions: (1) 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work and do you know the risks associated with them?'; (2) 'Do you understand how Enterprise Investment Schemes and Venture Capital Trusts work and do you know the risks associated with them?'; (3) 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them?'. For investment experience in the last 5 years, the client answered 'Yes, by myself' only for Current Accounts/Savings/Deposit Accounts/Cash ISA/National Savings and Investments. The client answered 'No' to Unit Trusts, stocks and shares, Enterprise Investment Schemes/Venture Capital Trusts, and pension planning products. However, the client does check valuation 'Yes, by myself' annually [P9]. The questionnaire notes section records 'No additional notes were recorded' for both Investor Experience and Capacity for Loss discussions [P10]. The Natural Risk Level from the questionnaire was 4 (Cautious Balanced), which was then moved to 5 (Balanced) [P6, P7]. The Risk Profile document states the Balanced investor description includes 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters then you may be suited to a Balanced approach' [P1, P6, P19], which is presented as a conditional precondition, not a confirmed fact about this client.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the client has 'Moderate' knowledge, but the questionnaire responses show 'No understanding / knowledge' on stocks and shares, enterprise schemes, and pension planning
      - The report states the client 'have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' [P7], but the questionnaire shows the client has had no investment experience in Unit Trusts, stocks and shares, or pension products in the last 5 years [P9]
      - The report recommends a 'Complex' product, but the client's stated knowledge is 'Moderate' and the questionnaire evidence indicates limited knowledge
      - The risk profile description reproduced in the report ('If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters then you may be suited to a Balanced approach') [P1, P7] presents this as a precondition or guard, not an established fact; the client's questionnaire responses contradict the precondition
      - The report states 'You want to keep the investment strategy of your pension fairly simple' [P5], yet recommends a complex multi-asset solution with ongoing rebalancing and adviser management required
      - No documented discussion of the client's limited knowledge of stocks and shares, pensions or enterprise schemes appears in the file; the investor experience notes section shows 'No additional notes were recorded' [P10]
    The requirement asks whether the client's knowledge, experience and investment understanding are accurately reflected, whether recommended product complexity is consistent with demonstrated knowledge and experience, and whether rationale is adequately justified.
    
    On reflection of the facts: The canonical model records the client's stated level as 'Moderate' and the recommended products as 'Complex'. The questionnaire completed at [P9] is the primary evidence of the client's actual knowledge and experience. It records 'No understanding / knowledge' on three critical investment and pension topics. The client has no recent personal experience with stocks and shares, unit trusts, pensions or enterprise schemes—only with basic savings and cash accounts.
    
    The report reproduces a description of a Balanced investor (Risk Rating 5) that begins with a conditional: 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters then you may be suited to a Balanced approach' [P1, P6, P7, P19]. This conditional is restated in the suitability report at [P7]: 'If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach to investing.' The precondition is 'some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters'. The questionnaire evidence contradicts this precondition: the client reports no understanding of stocks and shares or pensions, and no recent experience with investment products beyond cash.
    
    The guard at the comparison method level states: 'A risk-profile description carries preconditions about the client's experience. Where the report reproduces one whose preconditions the file contradicts, the description is being used as evidence of knowledge it does not establish—which is in scope here even though risk profiling itself belongs to CHK-003.' This guard applies. The report uses the Balanced investor description (which requires 'some experience in investments and a degree of understanding') to establish the client's knowledge level, but the questionnaire contradicts the precondition.
    
    The report states 'You want to keep the investment strategy of your pension fairly simple' [P5], which suggests simple investment design, yet recommends a two-fund portfolio requiring ongoing adviser management and rebalancing via a CIP (Centralised Investment Proposition). The rationale is offered at [P8]: 'The CIP aligns with your long-term goals and offers a more robust and flexible framework compared to a single multi-asset fund', but this addresses suitability of the structure, not suitability relative to the client's stated or demonstrated knowledge level.
    
    No evidence appears in the file of a recorded discussion about the client's limited knowledge of stocks and shares, pensions, or how the recommended products work. The investor experience discussion notes show 'No additional notes were recorded' [P10]. The discrepancy between the report's assertion of 'Moderate' knowledge and the file's questionnaire evidence of 'No understanding' on critical topics represents a material misalignment. The report fails to explain how a client with no stated understanding of stocks and shares or pensions is suited to a complex two-fund balanced portfolio, and the risk-profile description it invokes as justification carries a precondition the questionnaire contradicts.
    
    The complexity of the recommended solution (two actively managed multi-asset funds requiring ongoing rebalancing and adviser discretion) is not proportionate to the client's demonstrated knowledge level (no understanding of stocks and shares, pensions or investment schemes, experience limited to cash accounts). The rationale offered focuses on why the CIP structure is better than an investment pathway, not on why it is appropriate for a client with the client's limited knowledge.
    
    The vulnerability overlay does not apply here; no vulnerabilities affecting knowledge or comprehension are recorded in the evidence.
