  [G4.5] Evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product, based on their questions and comprehension
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states: 'You feel that you are comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards.' [P1] This is asserted as the client's understanding of investment comprehension.
    File:     The file records limited corroboration of this claimed understanding. The Fact Find [P18] and [P21] contain no recorded notes on the client's investment experience or questions. The Risk Profile Report [P14] and [P16] state 'No additional notes were recorded' under both 'Investor Experience' and 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' sections. The file notes [P2] record that the client's 'experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements' and that the client 'understand[s] that returns are not guaranteed.' There is no record of client questions, explanations, or demonstration of comprehension regarding the complexity of the recommended Collated Investment Portfolio (CIP) approach, which involves multiple asset classes, active oversight, and layered fund structures.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the client feels 'comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards,' but the file describes the client's experience as 'limited to workplace pension arrangements' — a characterisation that contradicts comfort with general investment mechanisms.
      - The report presents this as client understanding, but the file records 'No additional notes' on investor experience or capacity for loss discussions, creating a gap between the assertion and documented evidence of the client's reasoning or questions.
      - The recommended product (CIP with multiple coordinated funds and active governance) is described as complex in the report [P7], yet there is no recorded evidence of the client comprehending, questioning, or engaging with this complexity.
    The comparison can be made because the assertion appears in the canonical model and passages in the file directly address investor experience and recorded comprehension. The report asserts that the client 'feel[s] comfortable' and has 'reasonable understanding' of risks and rewards. This is presented as a subjective statement of the client's own perception. The file, however, records the client's experience as 'limited to workplace pension arrangements' [P2], which is an objective characterisation that undermines the breadth of understanding claimed. The file notes no documented discussion capturing the client's questions, explanations, or comprehension of the CIP structure itself. The requirement under [G4.5] calls for evidence that the client 'can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product, based on their questions and comprehension.' The standard is not merely that the client feels comfortable, but that comprehension is evidenced through questions, discussion notes, or explanations. The Risk Profile Report [P14] and [P16] explicitly record that 'No additional notes were recorded' under Investor Experience and agreed risk level sections. This absence is material where the recommended product is acknowledged as complex (requiring more than a generic investment pathway due to tailoring and active oversight [P7]). The guard note indicates that absence of recorded questions is not proof the client did not understand, but it is an evidence gap weighted by product complexity. Here, the product IS complex, and the gap is not merely silence but contradicts the stated level of comfort by positioning experience as 'limited.' The client's understanding is stated in the report but not evidenced in the file through questions, explanations, or structured comprehension checks. The discrepancy between 'comfortable understanding' and 'limited experience' indicates weak justification for the knowledge assertion.
      · I/Suitability Report Test 1.md [I_RecommendationsAndAdvice] (P1)
        "feel that you are comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards"
      · C/File notes notes - updated.md [C] (P2)
        "although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed"
      · D/Risk profile report.md [D] (P14)
        "No additional notes were recorded"
      · D/Risk profile report.md [D] (P16)
        "No additional notes were recorded"
      · I/Suitability Report Test 1.md [I_RecommendationsAndAdvice] (P7)
        "You require a more tailored solution than any of the four generic solutions offered by the provider. It does not reflect your individual circumstances, withdrawal requirements and risk profile as accurately as the recommended investment strategy."

  [G4.6] Existing holdings evidencing investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states the client 'confirmed that you have previously taken out cash savings products' [I page 4], 'have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself' [I page 4], and 'have however held your group pensions for some time and feel that you are comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' [I page 4]. The report lists five existing arrangements: People's Pension (OccupationalDC) holding Global Investments (up to 85% shares); Scottish Widows (PersonalPension) holding SW Pension Portfolio Four CS1 & SW Pension Portfolio Three CS1; Aviva (PersonalPension) holding Aviva pension my future focus consolidation; Zurich (PersonalPension) holding Managed AP (risk rating 7); and Standard Life (PersonalPension) holding SL at retirement universal (PP 10 year) pension fund [I page 5].
    File:     The case file evidences five existing pension arrangements with holdings: People's Pension plan ZU4225575 valued at £6,601.55 as at 27 September 2025, invested in Global Investments (up to 85% shares) [P21 table row showing start date 2021-04-21, plan type Occupational Pension, fund value 6601.55]; Scottish Widows Master Trust plan ZU4225575 valued at £211.32, plan information schedule [P11, P13]; Aviva Designer Stakeholder plan SP50163762 valued at £3,744.36 as at 22 September 2025, holding Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2 [P2]; Zurich Personal Pension Plan P10026-719-DL valued at £103,439.24 as at 24 September 2025, holding Managed AP (accumulation) at 100% [P3, P8]; and Standard Life Flexible Retirement Plan D2301334000 valued at £2,994.30 as at 30 September 2025, invested 100% in SL At Retirement Universal (PP 10 Year) Pension Fd [P4, P5, P14]. The file shows the client is age 69 (date of birth 7 June 1956) [P3, P5, P6]. No document in the file records the client's prior experience with cash savings, stocks & shares, or explains how long the group pensions have been held or what the client's stated understanding is based on.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report names the first arrangement as 'People's Pension' but the file evidences this as plan ZU4225575, a Scottish Widows Master Trust plan [P21 shows plan type 'Occupational Pension' with start date 2021-04-21 and valuation date 2025-09-27]. The report identifies this as 'OccupationalDC' type, which is consistent with the file classification, but the plan provider appears to be Scottish Widows, not People's Pension, creating ambiguity about whether the client held a separate People's Pension arrangement.
      - The report categorizes the first arrangement as 'OccupationalDC' but the file evidence at P21 labels it 'Occupational Pension' with start date 2021-04-21 (recent, 4 years ago). The guard states 'A workplace pension the client was auto-enrolled into evidences ownership, not investment experience; do not count it as either without something more.' No evidence in the file shows whether this is an auto-enrolment arrangement or one the client actively chose, nor does it evidence the client's own investment decision-making.
      - The report asserts the client 'feel that you are comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' based on holding group pensions 'for some time', but the oldest occupational pension arrangement in the file dates to 2021 (4 years old), not established as 'for some time' in any narrative. No fact-find, questionnaire, or interview records the basis of the client's stated comfort or understanding.
      - The report states the client 'have not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself' [I page 4], yet all five existing arrangements shown are pension products, and none evidence whether the client self-selected investments, were defaulted into funds, or participated in investment decisions. The existence of arrangements with equity content (Global Investments up to 85%, Managed AP at risk rating 7) does not contradict the assertion, but also does not corroborate active investment knowledge.
      - No evidence in the file records the client's prior experience with cash savings products as stated in the report. The report names this as confirmed by the client, but no fact-find document, questionnaire response, or adviser notes are present to support this claim.
    The file contains complete and accurate records of the client's five existing pension arrangements with current values, fund holdings, plan types, and valuation dates. These holdings corroborate that the client has experience with pension products and has been exposed to equity-based investments (Global Investments up to 85%, Managed AP). However, the corroboration is partial and limited. First, the guard states that holding a product is not the same as understanding it, and holdings corroborate experience but are not proof of knowledge; the presence of arrangements with equity content does not evidence the client's own knowledge or decision-making. Second, the report's claim that the client 'feel[s] comfortable with how investments work' rests entirely on unsubstantiated client assertion; no evidence in the file (such as fact-find responses, adviser notes, or recorded discussion) supports this claim. Third, the occupational pension arrangement (People's Pension/Scottish Widows Master Trust, started 2021) fits the guard that 'a workplace pension the client was auto-enrolled into evidences ownership, not investment experience; do not count it as either without something more.' No evidence establishes whether this was auto-enrolment or active choice, or whether it evidences the client's investment knowledge. Fourth, the report's assertion that the client has held group pensions 'for some time' is not corroborated; the Zurich arrangement dates to 2013 (12 years ago), the Aviva to 2017 (8 years ago), but the occupational pension is only 4 years old, and no file source confirms duration or circumstances of any of these holdings relative to the client's own experience. Fifth, the report's assertion about experience with cash savings products is not supported by any file evidence; no fact-find, questionnaire, or note records this. The consistency requirement is not fully met: the report attributes knowledge and comfort to the client based on unrecorded client statements and existing holdings that evidence exposure to investments but not necessarily the client's own active knowledge or decision-making capability.

  [G4.7] Adequacy of the rationale supporting a complex recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts that the recommended products are Complex [/knowledgeAndExperience/recommendedProductComplexity[]/complexity]. The complexity drivers identified are: Flexible drawdown access, Wide range of investment funds (6,900+ available), Multi-asset fund selection, Tax planning considerations, Death benefit options, and Inheritance Tax implications from 6 April 2027. The report provides seven distinct rationales for the recommendation: Lower Costs, Lifestyling (risk drift), Greater Investment Choice, Adviser Charging Facilitation, Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments, Greater Investment Choice (repeated), and Multi-asset fund selection with diversification justification.
    File:     The file evidence shows: (1) Client knowledge and experience: File notes [P8] record the client has 'limited' investment experience: 'although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed.' The client is age 69 [P12], approaching retirement in 2 years, with primary objective to consolidate pensions to 'get a better handle on his pensions' [P10]. (2) Attitude to Risk: Natural ATR from questionnaire was 4 [P12], but adviser assessed as risk rating 5 [P8]. File notes [P8] state 'As a Balanced investor, you do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person and have no strong positive or negative associations with the notion of taking risk. If you have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters, then you may be suited to a Balanced approach to investing.' (3) Product complexity discussion: Risk profile report [P22] shows no additional notes recorded for 'Investor Experience' section and 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' section - both state 'No additional notes were recorded' [P14]. (4) Recommended platform: Aviva Platform selected [P4]. (5) Product rationale corroboration: File notes [P10] discuss Zurich as alternative, noting 'Zurich do not have risk level 5 funds, a blend of a 7 and a 2 has been used to create the below 5. They have no multi asset funds in his risk range either...Wouldn't recommend investing in singular asset types.' Standard Life discounted as 'all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva' [P10]. Alternative Aviva option 'Only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR. Discounted switch' [P10]. (6) Client comprehension: No evidence of client questions, explanations or specific discussion of complex features. Risk profile report documents no notes on investment experience or capacity for loss discussions [P14].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report identifies product as Complex with 6,900+ investment funds and flexible drawdown; file records client has 'limited' investment experience to workplace pensions only.
      - Report provides seven rationales for recommending complex product; file notes document selection rationale (cost, consolidation need, ATR mismatch with alternatives) but do not evidence client comprehension of or questions about the complex features identified.
      - Report emphasizes 'Multi-asset fund selection' and 'Greater Investment Choice' as complexity drivers and rationales; file notes [P10] record alternative platforms (Zurich, Standard Life) were evaluated on cost and fund availability but do not record discussion with client about managing complex fund choice.
      - Risk profile report [P14] explicitly records 'No additional notes were recorded' for both Investor Experience section and Capacity for Loss Questionnaire section, indicating no detailed exploration of how the client's limited experience relates to the complex product recommended.
      - Report asserts 'Tax planning considerations' and 'Inheritance Tax implications' as complexity drivers; file contains no evidence of detailed tax planning discussion with client to justify this complexity for a client aged 69 with limited investment experience approaching 2-year retirement horizon.
    The comparison can be performed. The core tension is between (1) what the report claims about product complexity and its drivers, and (2) what the file records about the client's actual knowledge and the adviser's exploration of complexity with the client. The file consistently records the client's investment experience as 'limited' to workplace pensions [P8]. The report identifies the product as Complex with specific drivers including 6,900+ available funds, flexible drawdown, tax planning and IHT considerations. However, the file shows the adviser's selection logic focused on cost comparison and ATR matching across platforms [P10], not on matching complexity to client capability. The Risk profile report [P14] documents that no additional notes were recorded exploring the client's investor experience or capacity for loss in relation to product complexity—both sections are blank. This is significant because the client's natural ATR was 4 (Cautious Balanced) and was only moved to 5 after adviser reassessment [P8, P12], not based on demonstrated investment expertise. The file notes [P10] record adviser reasoning about why alternative platforms were rejected (cost, fund availability, ATR matching) but contain no record of explaining to the client why they needed access to 6,900 funds, flexible drawdown, or complex tax/IHT features, nor any evidence the client sought or questioned these capabilities. The rationale in the report emphasizes adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, and investment choice—benefits to the service model rather than justifications rooted in the client's demonstrated need for complexity given their stated limited experience and two-year retirement horizon. The false-positive guard states 'Complexity is a property of the product, not of the amount invested; a small holding in a complex product still needs a rationale' and 'A rationale written for the adviser's file rather than the client still counts as evidence, but does not satisfy the report's own duty to explain.' The file contains adviser working notes about why Aviva was selected versus alternatives, but these are not evidential of the client's comprehension or agreement that complexity was appropriate. The report's primary duty is to explain to the client and justify in the suitability report why a complex product suits a client with limited investment experience. The file notes show cost and consolidation rationale, but not complexity justification proportionate to the client's knowledge level.

CHK-005 — Financial Resilience, Affordability and Liquidity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G5.1, G5.2, G5.3, G5.4, G5.5, G5.6, G5.7, G5.8). Report states household expenditure is 'approximately £1,700 per month'; Fact Find records exactly £1,718.00 monthly expenditure. Difference: £18 or 1.1%. Report states 'Your combined income comfortably meets your household expenditure'; Fact Find shows Total Net Monthly Income of £1,430.00 against Total Monthly Expenditure of £1,718.00, resulting in a monthly deficit of -£288.00, not a comfortable surplus. Report asserts affordability of £3,305.55 initial fee and £1,101.85 annual ongoing fee without explicit cashflow or income allocation to cover these from the documented monthly deficit of -£288.00. 27 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G5.1] Affordability of initial and ongoing commitments
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts affordability on three bases: (1) combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month; (2) the client holds savings of around £6,000 with no outstanding liabilities; (3) any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living. The initial advice fee is £3,305.55 (3% of amount invested). Ongoing annual costs are circa £1,101.85 (1% per annum based on current values and expected contributions, minus initial fee). The report states these will be paid via the Aviva Platform scheme. No explicit affordability calculation linking the stated commitments to the evidenced income and expenditure is provided.
    File:     The Fact Find [P1] records Total Net Monthly Income of £1,430.00 and Total Monthly Expenditure of £1,718.00, yielding Total Monthly Disposable Income of -£288.00. This is a deficit, not a surplus. The Fact Find also records Calculated Total Monthly Household Expenditure of £1,718.00 and asks if the client expects changes; the answer is No. Current Monthly Cash Flow shows the same £1,718.00 expenditure against £1,430.00 income. The intelliflo planning scenarios [P5, P8] confirm employment income of £15,600 p/a (£300 weekly) plus £1,040 p/a (£20 weekly) from Sea Cadets for John, and £13,200 p/a (£1,100 monthly) for Kim until 2026. Savings of £6,000 are noted in [P5] as a Savings account. The planning scenarios show positive annual in-year surpluses from 2025 onwards in both Baseline and Proposed scenarios [P2, P11], but these include State Pension income from age 66 and exclude the initial years. For 2025 (first year of plan, ages 69/65), the proposed scenario [P5] would need to be examined, but the evidence does not show explicit first-year cashflow supporting the stated £3,305.55 initial fee or £1,101.85 ongoing fee alongside the documented monthly deficit of -£288.00.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states household expenditure is 'approximately £1,700 per month'; Fact Find records exactly £1,718.00 monthly expenditure. Difference: £18 or 1.1%.
      - Report states 'Your combined income comfortably meets your household expenditure'; Fact Find shows Total Net Monthly Income of £1,430.00 against Total Monthly Expenditure of £1,718.00, resulting in a monthly deficit of -£288.00, not a comfortable surplus.
      - Report asserts affordability of £3,305.55 initial fee and £1,101.85 annual ongoing fee without explicit cashflow or income allocation to cover these from the documented monthly deficit of -£288.00.
    The core discrepancy is material. The Fact Find [P1] explicitly records a monthly deficit of £288.00, whereas the report's affordability assertion ('Your combined income comfortably meets your household expenditure') assumes sufficiency. The stated commitments (initial £3,305.55 and annual £1,101.85) are presented as affordable, but the documented monthly cashflow shows expenditure exceeding net income by £288 per month. The report does not explain how this deficit is to be bridged to fund the recommended commitments. The £6,000 savings are noted, but the report does not quantify whether these are to be drawn down to meet the initial fee, or how the ongoing annual commitment of £1,101.85 is afforded given the persistent monthly shortfall. The intelliflo projections [P2, P5, P11] show positive in-year surpluses, but these are modelled from age 66 onwards when State Pension begins; the current deficit is not addressed. The report's statement that income 'comfortably meets' expenditure is contradicted by the contemporaneous Fact Find. The close proximity of the stated household expenditure (£1,700) to the recorded expenditure (£1,718) suggests the figures derive from the same source, making the absence of a matching income figure a deficiency. No evidence reconciles the monthly deficit with the affordability conclusion or explains how the initial and ongoing commitments are to be met from current income or reserves.

  [G5.2] Financial situation plus cashflow supports the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts total monthly net income of £4,486.67, comprising: John's employment £1,300, John's volunteer work £86.67, John's state pension £1,000, and Kim's employment £1,100. Total monthly expenditure is stated as £1,700, yielding a monthly surplus of £2,786.67. Total liquid assets are £6,000 with zero liabilities and net worth of £6,000. The cashflow is projected to run until age 100 with all surplus income spent rather than saved. Five scenarios are modelled (baseline, high inflation 6%, market crash -30% combined with 6% inflation, and two longevity scenarios) all showing 'NoShortfall'. The report states 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement' and that funds are projected to last throughout lifetime based on ONS life expectancy (age 86 for John, age 88 for Kim). The report includes projected pension values of £110,185 at age 75 under mid-growth assumptions. A stress test with -30% market crash and 6% inflation shows no shortfall requiring pension drawdown.
    File:     The Fact Find shows total net monthly income of £1,430, comprising employment income and state pension, with total monthly expenditure of £1,718, resulting in a monthly deficit of £288. The intelliflo planning models show opening liquid assets of £6,000 (labeled as 'Savings') and total defined contribution pensions of £116,997. The Baseline scenario cashflow shows relatively flat liquid assets around £122,953-£122,991 throughout the plan from 2025-2055, then declining from 2056 onwards. The Proposed scenario shows liquid assets growing from £138,235 at age 78/74 (2034) to £184,141 at age 104/100 (2060). Both models show zero 'In Year Surplus / Deficit' for the surplus income percentage saved column for the majority of years. The intelliflo scenarios confirm 0% of surplus is saved within the plan. First-year expenditure across both scenarios is stated as £24,001 (including tax and NI), substantially higher than the £1,700 monthly (£20,400 annualized) stated in the report.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states total monthly net income of £4,486.67 but Fact Find shows total net monthly income of only £1,430
      - Report asserts monthly expenditure of £1,700 but intelliflo models show first-year total regular expenditure of £24,001 including tax and NI
      - Report claims monthly surplus of £2,786.67 but Fact Find shows monthly deficit of £288
      - Report income breakdown lists John's employment as £1,300 but Fact Find shows employed income of £1,200
      - Report lists John's volunteer income as £86.67 but no volunteer income appears in Fact Find income section
      - Report lists John's state pension as £1,000 monthly but Fact Find shows state pension at £230 monthly starting 2025
      - Report lists Kim's employment as £1,100 but Fact Find employment row for second applicant shows £1,200
      - Report states only £1,700 monthly expenditure but intelliflo baseline shows combined state pension only £11,973 annually (£998 monthly) would be insufficient to cover £24,001 annual expenditure without pension drawdown from age 2027 onwards
      - Fact Find shows current monthly disposable income as negative £288 but report assumes positive surplus throughout lifetime
      - Report projects retirement expenditure of £1,700 per month but planning models show first-year expenditure of £24,001 including tax and NI, suggesting either the modelled expenditure or the stated expenditure figure is inaccurate
    The comparison reveals material inconsistencies between what the suitability report asserts as financial inputs and what the supporting evidence documents contain. The report's income total of £4,486.67 cannot be derived from the Fact Find figures: the Fact Find shows £1,430 current monthly income. The report's stated component incomes (£1,300 employment, £86.67 volunteer, £1,000 state pension, £1,100 Kim employment) do not match the Fact Find entries. The Fact Find states current monthly disposable income as negative £288, directly contradicting the report's assertion of a £2,786.67 monthly surplus. The expenditure discrepancy is particularly material: the report works from £1,700 monthly (£20,400 annually) but the intelliflo planning models show first-year expenditure of £24,001 including tax and NI. When mapped to the cashflow data: state pension income of £11,973 annually (£998 monthly from 2027) against £24,001 annual expenditure would require £12,028 annual drawdown from other sources. However, the intelliflo baseline scenario tables show 'In Year Surplus / Deficit' entries of zero for saved surplus, consistent with the stated 0% savings rate. The report's claim that 'surplus income is spent rather than saved' is consistent with the model assumption but the magnitude of the surplus differs radically between the report's assertion and the Fact Find. The stress test showing 'no shortfall' and funds lasting to age 100 in the proposed scenario contrasts with the baseline scenario, which shows liquid assets declining from £122,956 (age 79/75 in 2035) to £111,171 (age 104/100 in 2060). The proposed scenario shows assets remaining around £184,141 at age 104/100. These are derived from different assumptions: the report's stated inputs do not clearly map to either modelled scenario. The Fact Find's negative £288 monthly disposable income suggests the clients cannot currently afford stated expenditure from current income, yet the report does not address this current affordability crisis or explain how it transitions to the projected surplus. The discrepancy between modelled expenditure (£24,001 first year including tax) and stated retirement expenditure (£1,700 monthly) is critical: if £24,001 is the actual modelled outflow, the affordability analysis is based on incomplete disclosure of what 'expenditure' comprises. The report does state assumptions are 'detailed at the end of this report' but the canonical extraction shows those assumptions (2% general expenditure inflation, 0% surplus saved, etc.) do not reconcile the income and expenditure inputs to produce the stated surplus. No guard explains these differences: growth assumptions concern only the investment returns, not income input mismatches. The client cannot currently afford expenditure (Fact Find deficit of £288), making current affordability unsupported by evidence.

    Report:   Report asserts £6,000 held as emergency fund and states client has confirmed this is sufficient to cover unexpected emergencies [P1 from I category].       - Report states client receives £300 per week net HGV income [I category], but Fact Find income table [P18] shows CDH Recruitment employment income as £1,200 monthly net (approximately £277 weekly, not £300). This is internal to the file itself - both the report and the fact find come from the case file.
      - Report asserts emergency fund of £6,000 is 'sufficient' without quantifying what 'sufficient' means or what the adequacy standard is measured against. The term 'confirmed sufficient' appears in the report but no calculation or benchmark is provided to justify this.
      - Fact Find shows monthly disposable income of -£288 [P14], indicating current monthly expenditure exceeds current net monthly income. However, report recommends capital contributions and does not address how affordability can be assured when current cashflow is negative.
      - Report projects positive annual surplus from 2027 onwards [P5], yet Fact Find dated 22/09/2025 shows current deficit of -£288 per month (equivalent to -£3,456 per year). The timing and bridging of this gap is not explained in supporting documents.
      - Liquid assets in Fact Find are stated as £6,000 [P6]. Proposed scenario shows liquid assets projected to decline to £5,956 in 2026, £5,912 in 2027 and further decline over time [P7, P16]. Yet report asserts client 'retain[s] sufficient liquid capital' without addressing ongoing depletion.
      - Report does not quantify the required emergency fund size. No calculation shown for what constitutes adequate emergency cover relative to monthly expenditure (£1,718) or income disruption scenarios.
      - Baseline scenario projects liquid assets declining from £6,000 initially to approximately £5,783 by 2030 [P16, row 2030], yet report does not explain whether this residual level is adequate or how ongoing emergency needs will be met from this eroding base.
    The report asserts that the client has confirmed retention of sufficient liquid capital (£6,000) to cover unexpected emergencies and is comfortable with access to liquid cash. This assertion is corroborated at face value by the Fact Find evidence showing £6,000 held in cash savings [P6, P14]. However, the comparison reveals material gaps and inconsistencies. First, the sufficiency of £6,000 is asserted without quantification of the standard against which it was tested. The report states the client 'confirmed' sufficiency but provides no calculation showing the relationship between £6,000 and either: (a) the monthly expenditure of £1,718, (b) a stated number of months' expenses, or (c) any other methodological benchmark. This is the unquantified-sufficiency guard: an asserted adequacy with no evidenced requirement cannot be verified. Second, the Fact Find shows current monthly disposable income of -£288 [P14], meaning expenditure currently exceeds net income. Yet the proposed solution involves capital contributions which the report does not reconcile against this negative cashflow. The planning documents show this bridged only from 2027 onwards when State Pension income (£11,973 for each spouse from age 66 [P3, P23]) enters the projection. Between 2025 and 2027, the gap remains unaddressed in the evidence. Third, the projected liquid asset values in both baseline and proposed scenarios show continuous decline from £6,000 baseline [P7, P16]. In proposed scenario, liquid assets are modelled to decline from £6,000 (2025) to £5,956 (2026), £5,912 (2027), and continue declining [P7]. The report does not explain whether this declining trajectory leaves adequate emergency reserves or how access needs will be funded. Fourth, the access mechanics for emergency funds are addressed differently from pension access: emergency access is stated to be from cash savings (immediately available), while pension access after retirement requires 10-15 working days [I category]. The risk that liquid cash becomes depleted before age 55 (pension access age) is not analysed in the file. The guard for competing claims on cash applies: cash held may be earmarked for other purposes. The Key Features [P11] note automatic disinvestment if insufficient cash for charges, and Terms and Conditions [P4] note adviser charges deducted from cash account only once sufficient cleared funds are available. These mechanics mean the £6,000 may not be wholly available for emergency use if it is needed to fund platform charges or adviser fees. Fifth, the report's statement that client 'confirmed' sufficiency rests on an assertion within the report itself [I category], not on independent evidence in the file. An [I] category passage (the report stating what the client confirmed) cannot corroborate itself; this is internal consistency at best, not external support. The evidence does not include a signed client note, email, or meeting note recording the client's independent confirmation. The comparison can be performed because the key figures (£6,000 emergency fund, £1,718 monthly expenditure, -£288 monthly disposable income, declining asset projections, access timings) are all present in the file. However, the sufficiency of £6,000 against foreseeable and unforeseeable needs is not supported by any calculation in the evidence. The report asserts adequacy; the file does not demonstrate it.

  [G5.5] Emergency funds not taken into account, or depleted or unavailable when required
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that liquidity and access needs are funded from two sources: 'Cash savings' and 'Pension arrangements via Flexi Access Drawdown'. The canonical model extraction identifies `/financialPosition/liquidityAndAccessNeeds[]/fundedFrom` as containing these two funding sources. However, the extraction notes that `/financialPosition/emergencyFund/shortfall` is absent from the document, meaning the report does not explicitly state whether an emergency fund shortfall exists or how it is addressed.
    File:     The evidence shows initial liquid assets of £6,000 in a Cash Account [P1, P9], described as 'Savings' earning '1.25% expected return p/a' [P1]. The baseline scenario [P4, P6, P9] shows liquid assets starting at £6,000 and remaining relatively stable at approximately £5,900-£6,000 throughout retirement (age 70-96). The proposed scenario [P10, P12] similarly maintains liquid assets at approximately £6,000 in 2025, declining marginally to £5,956 in 2026 and £5,912 in 2027. The cashflow tables [P4, P9, P12] show 'In Year Surplus / Deficit' consistently at £0, indicating no drawing down of the emergency fund to cover shortfalls. Total regular expenditure is stated as £20,616 p/a [P10], with income sources of employment (£15,600-£16,640 p/a depending on year) [P1, P5], Sea Cadets (£1,040 p/a) [P1, P5], and pension access. The liquidation order in the proposed scenario shows: 'LIQUIDATED 1ST: Surplus Account (£0)', 'LIQUIDATED 2ND: Savings (£6,000)' [P1]. This ordering indicates the emergency fund (savings) is scheduled as the second item to be liquidated, after the already-depleted surplus account.
    Discrepancies:
      - The liquidation order in the proposed scenario schedules the £6,000 Savings account as 'LIQUIDATED 2ND', meaning it is designated to be drawn upon if needed for cashflow shortfalls, contrary to typical emergency fund preservation.
      - The report asserts emergency funds are retained but does not quantify what constitutes an adequate emergency fund or state whether £6,000 is sufficient for the clients' circumstances.
      - The report identifies liquidity needs funded from 'Cash savings' and 'Pension arrangements via Flexi Access Drawdown' but does not explain the interaction between these two or prioritise access to the cash emergency fund versus pension drawdown.
    The evidence does not show the emergency fund being depleted during the modelled years (2025-2056), as the cashflow surplus/deficit remains at £0 throughout and liquid assets decline only marginally (from £6,000 to approximately £5,900 over 2-3 years) [P4, P10, P12]. However, the comparative analysis reveals material concerns about emergency fund appropriateness. First, the liquidation order explicitly places the £6,000 savings account second in the queue for drawdown [P1], indicating it is not being ring-fenced but designated as available to cover income shortfalls. Second, the report does not quantify an emergency fund requirement or state why £6,000 is adequate for a couple with combined regular expenditure of approximately £20,616 p/a [P10]. Industry guidance typically recommends 3-6 months of expenses; £6,000 represents only 3.5 months of stated expenditure, and this is not addressed in the report. Third, the report's assertion that liquidity needs are 'funded from Cash savings' is vague about sequencing and does not clarify whether the cash will be preserved as true emergency cover or treated as available working capital. The proposed scenario shows the Surplus Account starting at £0 [P1, P13], meaning the £6,000 savings are the first actual liquid buffer; their designation as 'LIQUIDATED 2ND' (after an empty surplus account) in the liquidation order indicates they are the primary source of emergency drawdown. While the modelled cashflow does not force this drawing during normal years, the structure places emergency reserves at material risk if income or expenditure assumptions deteriorate. The guard 'A cashflow that schedules the emergency fund to be spent has not retained it, whatever the report says about retaining liquid capital' applies: the liquidation order demonstrates the £6,000 is scheduled as an available drawdown source, not protected. The report makes no case for why £6,000 is adequate or how it will be preserved separate from income needs.

  [G5.6] Liquidity mismatch with access needs
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the client's liquidity and access need is 'After retirement/age 55' [from /financialPosition/liquidityAndAccessNeeds]. The recommended solution is a Personal Pension wrapper. The report states the client 'understand[s] the pension freedoms and prefer[s] the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown when you access your pension, allowing you control over how and when benefits are taken' [P1, page 9]. The planned withdrawal type is UFPLS (Uncrystallised Funds Pension Lump Sum) with tax-free lump sum up to 25% of fund value crystallised; remainder taxed at marginal rate.
    File:     The evidence confirms that Personal Pensions through Aviva Platform allow access from minimum pension age (currently age 55, rising to 57 from 6 April 2028) [P3, P4]. Flexi-access drawdown is available as an option from age 55 [P3, P4, P14]. The client can take up to 25% tax-free with the remainder available for flexible income withdrawals [P2, P3, P9, P14]. The intelliflo planning proposed scenario [P19] shows retirement age for one spouse at 66 in 2026 and for the other at 71 in 2027, with minimum pension age set at 55. The Fact Find [P21] shows £6,000 in liquid savings. The baseline scenario [P12] shows total liquid assets of £6,000 in savings plus the pension fund of £116,997. Access to funds through the Personal Pension is not restricted before retirement; the wrapper permits ad hoc withdrawals from age 55 onwards without lock-in periods beyond the minimum pension age.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts access need as 'After retirement/age 55' but does not specify a retirement age. The planning scenarios show retirement ages of 66 and 71 respectively for the two household members [P19]. The access need stated ('After retirement/age 55') is ambiguous: it could mean access is needed at age 55, or access is needed only after retirement (which occurs later, at 66 or 71). This creates uncertainty about whether the wrapper's unrestricted access from age 55 is appropriately matched to an access need that may not arise until 66 or 71.
      - The report does not quantify emergency fund requirements or confirm that liquid reserves of £6,000 [P21, P12] are sufficient to meet the client's stated financial resilience needs prior to accessing the pension fund.
      - The report asserts affordability from a liquidity perspective but does not cross-reference the access timeline to the cashflow projections in the planning scenarios [P5, P24] which show required withdrawals at specific points. No explicit statement confirms that the recommended wrapper's access flexibility aligns with the timing of those projected withdrawals.
    The Personal Pension wrapper selected is appropriate in respect of access restrictions: it permits flexible access from age 55 without lock-in, and Flexi Access Drawdown is explicitly available [P3, P4, P14]. The client is currently age 65 (one spouse) and 61 (the other) [P19], so both are above or approaching the minimum pension age of 55. The access restriction guard applies: pension access restrictions are irrelevant where the client is already over the minimum pension age. The older spouse is already over age 55 and can access immediately if needed; the younger spouse reaches 55 well before retirement at age 71. Therefore, there is no liquidity mismatch arising from access restriction by the wrapper. However, the report's statement of the access need as 'After retirement/age 55' is imprecise. The planning evidence [P19] shows retirement ages of 66 and 71, not 55. The report does not explicitly confirm that the £6,000 liquid reserves are adequate as an emergency fund or that the pension fund access from age 55 onwards provides sufficient liquidity buffer. The cashflow projections in the planning scenarios [P5, P24] model regular expenditure and show a surplus account, but the suitability report does not cross-reference these to affirm that liquidity is assured across the planned timeline. The affordability assertion regarding financial resilience rests on the planning scenarios but is not explicitly reconciled in the report itself with the access needs and emergency reserve position. This is a weak justification rather than a data inconsistency: the wrapper is not restrictive, but the report's articulation of how access needs are met and how liquid reserves are deemed sufficient lacks explicit support.

The affordability assessment relies on pension/State Pension income to bridge a cashflow shortfall (Fact Find shows £-288.00 monthly disposable income without pension), but the report does not quantify how much State Pension supports this or when it begins. The discrepancy between the Fact Find start date (22/09/2025) and the extracted report date (2027-01-01) is material: if State Pension does not begin until 2027, the interim cashflow from 2025 to 2027 is unexplained. The extraction's absence of gross State Pension amount prevents verification against any statutory forecast. The planning documents do not cite a State Pension entitlement statement or include one in the evidence pack. The cashflow projections show positive surplus from 2025 onwards once pensions are included, but without State Pension being separately quantified or confirmed to start when modelled, the corroboration of affordability is incomplete.

    Report:   The suitability report asserts four planned future changes: (1) Client retirement expected 2027-10-14 with financial impact of -£300/week net HGV driver income (estimated -£1,300/month); (2) Kim's retirement at age 66 in 2027 with financial impact of -£1,100 net; (3) Kim begins receiving State Pension 2027-01-01 with financial impact of +£1,000 net (described as 'around £230 per week'); (4) Inheritance Tax treatment change for pensions from 6 April 2027. P2 and P5 (intelliflo proposed scenario) show Summary Cashflow analysis across years 2025-2060, with liquid assets declining gradually and accessible pensions increasing over time. The planning documents show State Pension assumption of 2.5% inflation. P17 (Fact Find retirement planning notes) states client 'needs a household income of £1,700 net per month' and 'probably retire in the next 2 years'. P9 (intelliflo assumptions) states 'Surplus income percentage saved: 0% of any in-year surplus will be saved within the plan'.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Kim receives 'around £230 per week' State Pension equating to +£1,000 net monthly impact; P14 shows State Pension entitlement of £230.00 monthly (not weekly), which at 4.33 weeks per month equals approximately £53.23 per week, not £230 per week
      - Report asserts financial impact of Kim's retirement as -£1,100 net; no corresponding entry in the Fact Find identifies this specific figure or the source of this retirement income loss quantification
      - Report asserts financial impact of client's retirement as -£300/week (-£1,300/month estimated); P14 shows HGV driver income of £1,200.00 monthly employed CDH Recruitment, not £1,300/month, and no £300/week figure appears in the income schedule
      - Report states current monthly disposable income implicitly supports planning; P1 shows actual disposable income of -£288.00 (expenditure exceeds income by £288/month), yet report does not quantify how this deficit is currently funded or managed
      - Report asserts emergency fund is 'funded from cash savings'; P6 and P12 show only £6,000 in Savings/Cash Account as total liquid assets, with no separate quantification of an 'emergency fund' or its adequacy relative to monthly expenditure of £1,718 or planned needs
      - Report does not state whether the -£288 monthly deficit in current cashflow will persist, improve or worsen post-retirement, or how pension drawdown will be sequenced to maintain affordability
    The requirement tests whether planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs are detailed with full support and consistency. The core tension is that the current Fact Find shows a £288 monthly deficit (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718), yet the report does not explain how this gap is currently funded or how retirement—which removes £1,200 HGV income—will be bridged. The intelliflo planning scenarios (P2, P5, P6, P12) model forward cashflow and show liquid assets declining gradually over time, with pension drawdown required in years where income falls short. This forward modelling exists in the file but is not explicitly discussed in the report narrative reviewed. Regarding State Pension: P14 records Kim's State Pension as £230.00 monthly (age of entitlement 66), which the report quotes as 'around £230 per week'—a tenfold discrepancy. £230 weekly would be ~£1,000 monthly, matching the +£1,000 figure asserted in the report's financial impact. The error appears to be in the report's unit conversion: the evidence shows monthly figures, the report has restated them as weekly without adjustment. The HGV income: P14 shows £1,200 employed income from CDH Recruitment monthly. The report states loss of '£300 per week' HGV driver income, which would be ~£1,300/month—slightly higher than the £1,200 recorded. The discrepancy of £100/month is material when the household is already running a £288 deficit. The report asserts client needs £1,700 net household income at retirement (P17). Current expenditure is £1,718, so the need is consistent. However, the report does not detail how this income will be sourced in retirement. State Pension (Kim ~£230/month or £1,000 if the weekly reading were correct—which it is not per evidence) plus any pension drawdown must fill the gap. The intelliflo scenarios show this is modelled, but the report's narrative does not specify the drawdown quantum or sustainability. Emergency fund: the report states it is 'funded from cash savings' but does not quantify how many months' expenditure (or emergency cover) the £6,000 represents. At £1,718/month expenditure, £6,000 covers approximately 3.5 months—potentially adequate but never stated as such in the evidence. The guard on undated plans applies: the planning events in intelliflo (P12) are dated and sequenced. The guard on aspirational vs committed needs applies: retirement is a stated intention, not aspirational. Both the Fact Find (P17) and report agree on the two-year retirement horizon. The consistency check reveals: (1) a unit error in State Pension (weekly vs monthly) inflating the retirement income assumption by ~£800/month; (2) a £100/month discrepancy in HGV income at retirement, material to affordability; (3) forward planning exists in intelliflo outputs but is not synthesised in the narrative to explain how the current £288 deficit and future £1,700 income need will be reconciled; (4) emergency reserves quantified but not assessed for adequacy. These are data inconsistencies and weak justification—the numbers exist but are not reliably tied together in the narrative or explained to the client.

CHK-006 — Recommendation Rationale and Suitable Alternatives
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 9 requirement(s). 9 raised a concern (G6.1, G6.2, G6.3, G6.4, G6.5, G6.6, G6.7, G6.8, G6.9). The report marks all 20 rationale statements as isClientSpecific: true, but 6 of these rationale texts are repeated verbatim across different recommendations (e.g. 'Greater Investment Choice' appears 3 times with identical wording; 'Adviser Charging Facilitation' appears 3 times identically; 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments' appears 3 times with near-identical wording; 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility' appears 2 times identically; 'Lower Costs' appears 3 times identically; 'Lifestyling' appears 2 times identically). These are boilerplate rationales, not client-specific ones, yet marked as such. The report links only 5 of the 20 rationale statements to objective IDs (all 5 mapped to OBJ3 or OBJ1), leaving 15 rationale statements with no explicit linkage to any stated client objective in linkedObjectiveIds, despite the requirement to link each to specified goals. The evidence pack does not define or identify client objectives labelled OBJ1 or OBJ3. The file strategy (P4) names the client's overall goal as simplification and consolidation, but neither the report's rationales nor the evidence identify how 'Greater Investment Choice' or 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility' specifically address a consolidation/simplification objective. The connection is asserted but not evidenced. 14 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G6.1] Client-specific rationale for each recommendation, linked to specified goals
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts 20 discrete rationale statements across multiple recommendations. These are marked in the canonical model as isClientSpecific: true for all 20 entries. The rationales fall into repeating categories: 'Lower Costs', 'Lifestyling', 'Greater Investment Choice', 'Adviser Charging Facilitation', 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments', 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility', 'Multi-asset fund selection', and statements about workplace pension retention and small pots rules. Specific recommendations cited: Switch Aviva (rationale: Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing); Switch Zurich (rationale: Expensive, no adviser servicing); Retain People's Pension (active workplace pension); Encash Scottish Widows (small pension pot, would not detract from retirement plan). The report states linkedObjectiveIds only for the first 5 rationale items, mapping them to OBJ3 (first 4) and OBJ1 (fifth). The rationale texts reference 'your agreed risk range', 'your retirement goals', 'your risk profile', and 'your changing needs'.
    File:     The evidence pack contains: (P4) policy research notes showing the advice summary as 'Client has come to us seeking advice because he has a lot of pensions that he doesn't understand how they work and ideally wants to put them all together to make it easier for him to understand and utilise in his retirement. Will be recommending to consolidate where necessary and provide cashflow planning around his retirement.' Alternative strategies noted: 'Current set up isn't working because it is too much information for him to understand. Wants to simplify things.' (P8) research table identifying 'Investment Choice Goals' as 'ATR 5 CIP funds' and listing recommendations as 'Switch' for Aviva and Zurich, 'Retain' for People's Pension, and notes for each. (P10) suitability report excerpt stating 'Following a recent review of your financial affairs we identified certain needs and objectives as detailed below.' (P12) risk profile report documenting 'Natural risk level' from questionnaire completed 18/09/2025, showing initial score of 4 (Cautious Balanced), but extraction notes ambiguity about agreement on rating 5. (P9, P14) suitability report sections on costs, ongoing services, and appendix with critical yield and charge comparisons. None of the evidence pack passages provide explicit client objectives labelled OBJ1 or OBJ3, nor do they corroborate the specific objective each recommendation is said to achieve. No passage details why lower costs, lifestyling adjustment, or greater investment choice specifically address the client's stated retirement consolidation goal beyond the general goal to 'simplify' and 'understand' the pensions.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report marks all 20 rationale statements as isClientSpecific: true, but 6 of these rationale texts are repeated verbatim across different recommendations (e.g. 'Greater Investment Choice' appears 3 times with identical wording; 'Adviser Charging Facilitation' appears 3 times identically; 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments' appears 3 times with near-identical wording; 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility' appears 2 times identically; 'Lower Costs' appears 3 times identically; 'Lifestyling' appears 2 times identically). These are boilerplate rationales, not client-specific ones, yet marked as such.
      - The report links only 5 of the 20 rationale statements to objective IDs (all 5 mapped to OBJ3 or OBJ1), leaving 15 rationale statements with no explicit linkage to any stated client objective in linkedObjectiveIds, despite the requirement to link each to specified goals.
      - The evidence pack does not define or identify client objectives labelled OBJ1 or OBJ3. The file strategy (P4) names the client's overall goal as simplification and consolidation, but neither the report's rationales nor the evidence identify how 'Greater Investment Choice' or 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility' specifically address a consolidation/simplification objective. The connection is asserted but not evidenced.
      - Rationale for Aviva switch states 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing' in research notes (P8), but the full suitability report rationale cites 'Lifestyling' as the primary reason (existing funds move outside agreed risk range as retirement approaches), not cost or adviser servicing. The condensed notes and full rationale do not align on emphasis.
      - Rationale for Zurich switch states 'Expensive, no adviser servicing' (P8) in research notes, but the suitability report provides detailed multi-asset fund justification ('Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds, so to achieve this profile, a blend of a risk level 7 and a risk level 2 fund has been used. As there are no multi-asset funds available within your risk range, we would not recommend investing in single asset types, as this reduces diversification'). Research notes make no mention of fund blending or diversification rationale.
      - The report asserts 'Lower Costs' as a primary rationale for the switch recommendations, but the derived figures show: (1) Aviva charge comparison is internally contradictory (extraction notes state the plan is '0.06% more expensive' but also lists a -0.06% impact); (2) Standard Life charges are inconsistent (0.52% existing vs 0.18% in switch table vs 0.08% in narrative); (3) the total fund values used to calculate charges do not reconcile (sum of arrangements is £116,998.47 but recommended solution appears to be £110,185, creating inconsistency in which baseline the 'savings' are computed from). Without reliable charge data, the 'lower costs' rationale cannot be verified as accurate.
      - Alternatives considered: the report lists 'Alternative Strategies' in research notes (P4) as 'Current set up isn't working because it is too much information for him to understand. Wants to simplify things.' This identifies the problem (complexity) but does not document consideration of alternative approaches to solving it (e.g. retaining all schemes with consolidated reporting, switching to a unified platform without full consolidation, etc.). No formal alternatives analysis with reasoned rejection is evidenced.
    The report presents rationales for 5 primary recommendations across 20 asserted rationale statements. However, the comparison reveals material weaknesses: (1) Boilerplate duplication: Six rationale texts—'Lower Costs', 'Lifestyling', 'Greater Investment Choice', 'Adviser Charging Facilitation', 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments', 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility'—are repeated verbatim or near-verbatim across 2–3 different recommendations. The canonical model marks all 20 as isClientSpecific: true, but verbatim repetition across unrelated recommendations is the hallmark of boilerplate language, not client-specific rationale. Under the guard, identical bullets repeated across recommendations should be counted once and marked false. This is a material finding: the report's extraction claims 20 client-specific rationales when approximately 6 distinct rationale statements (appearing 2–3 times each) are present, and at least 6 of the 20 instances are boilerplate reuse. (2) Broken objective linkage: Only 5 of 20 rationale statements are mapped to objective IDs (linkedObjectiveIds). The remaining 15 carry rationales but no stated connection to a named objective. The requirement mandates that rationales be 'linked to specified goals / objectives'. Without objective IDs for 75% of the rationales, this requirement is not met. Further, the objectives OBJ1 and OBJ3 are not defined in the evidence pack; they appear only in the canonical extraction. The file strategy (P4) names the goal as 'consolidate where necessary and provide cashflow planning around his retirement' and 'make it easier for him to understand', but this is not labelled OBJ1 or OBJ3. (3) Cost rationale undermined by data inconsistency: The 'Lower Costs' rationale appears 3 times as a primary justification, yet the case file metadata documents multiple internal contradictions in charge figures (Aviva 0.06% inconsistency, Standard Life 0.52% vs 0.18% vs 0.08%, and fund value totals not reconciling from £116,998 to £110,185). The extraction itself flags these as 'internalInconsistencies'. Where a core rationale rests on unreliable underlying data, that rationale cannot be verified as supported. (4) Limited alternatives documentation: The research notes (P4) identify the problem ('too much information') and the direction (consolidate and simplify) but do not evidence formal consideration of alternative approaches to achieving consolidation (e.g. consolidated reporting without switching, switching to a different platform provider, partial consolidation). The requirement calls for 'consideration of relevant alternatives'. The file shows problem identification but not documented alternative evaluation. (5) Specific vs. generic mapping: The Zurich rationale illustrates the tension. The research notes (P8) state 'Expensive, no adviser servicing', suggesting cost and servicing were the basis. The suitability report then offers a detailed, technical rationale about multi-asset fund blending and diversification (not appearing in research notes). This technical justification may be sound, but the mismatch between research notes and final rationale suggests the final rationale may have been constructed post-hoc rather than grounded in the original advice discussion. (6) Vulnerability overlay: The extraction metadata notes no specific vulnerability drivers in the evidence pack. The risk profile (P12) records no additional notes on 'Agreed risk level' and no discussion notes on 'Investment target'. The client's stated desire to 'understand' his pensions is recorded, but there is no assessment of whether complexity itself is a barrier to comprehension that might indicate a support need. This is not a finding on CHK-006 itself (that is CHK-010's role), but the absence of recorded discussion on understanding/support needs makes it harder to verify that the consolidation rationale is tailored to this client's actual capacity and support needs, not a standard recommendation. The report's promise of 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments' and 'Access to a professional, experienced and qualified team of financial advisers at any time' (P14) may address this, but it is not explicitly tied to the client's stated desire to understand his pensions. Conclusion on comparison: A reliable comparison can be performed. The report asserts client-specific rationales linked to objectives; the evidence pack retrieves the general advice direction (consolidate, simplify), but does not corroborate objective-by-objective rationale, does not evidence alternatives formally considered, and contains internal data inconsistencies that undermine the cost-saving claims. The finding is that while rationale text is present, it lacks the required linkage to stated objectives, contains boilerplate duplication marked as client-specific, and rests partly on unverified cost claims.

  [G6.2] Justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts that Aviva was selected as provider based on seven factors: (1) Financial strength — 'Aviva is rated A (Stable) by S&P, with £407bn AUM (31 Dec 2024)'; (2) Choice — 'Over 6,900 funds and leading managers, across ISAs, pensions, and GIAs'; (3) Expertise — 'Aviva Investors manages award-winning strategies across asset classes, plus access to private markets'; (4) Value — 'Transparent, competitive charges with potential tiered pricing'; (5) Convenience — 'Online tools for viewing performance, switching funds, and accessing documents'; (6) Governance — 'Strong regulatory oversight and risk management'; (7) Innovation — 'Ongoing digital investment and enhanced cash management tools'. These are stated as universal provider characteristics, not client-specific. For investment strategy, the report states: 'The funds reflect your risk profile both individually and combined', 'provides exposure to a range of asset classes and market sectors resulting in greater diversification', 'blend of active and passive investments, which allows for some active management, whilst keeping costs low', 'You want to keep the investment strategy of your pension fairly simple', 'The charges are competitive', 'The funds have performed well in comparison with their peers', and 'funds are part of our CIP (Centralised Investment Proposition)'. The recommended funds are HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50% allocation, 0.18% charge) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50% allocation, 0.29% charge). The report states investment pathways were considered and rejected because 'You require a more tailored solution than any of the four generic solutions offered by the provider. It does not reflect your individual circumstances, withdrawal requirements and risk profile as accurately as the recommended investment strategy.'
    File:     Research document P1 (SIPP platform research) shows a shortlist of 4 products: Aviva Platform, Aberdeen Elevate, Aegon Retirement Choices (ARC), and Wealthtime Platform. The research note states 'No Platform DNA analysis was carried out' and 'No filters were applied'. P5 confirms Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice was recommended, noting the Aviva brand history and that Whole of Market was used in product selection. P9 confirms 1 product from 118 matched filters for SIPP: Pension Portfolio - Choice by Aviva. P13 (Policy Research) provides a comparison table showing existing arrangements (Aviva marked 'Switch' with notes 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing'; Zurich marked 'Switch' with notes 'Expensive, no adviser servicing'; People's Pension marked 'Retain' as 'Active workplace pension'; Scottish Widows marked for encashment). P14 (Pension Switching Report) confirms the recommendation to consolidate into Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice on Aviva Platform. P10 and P12 provide performance data for the two recommended funds: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C shows 5-year return of 40.06%, and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc shows 5-year return of 44.73%. Combined aggregated fund costs totalled 0.37% (P10). P8 describes the Aviva Platform as allowing consolidation of investments and combining different tax wrappers. No independent research evidence is cited comparing Aviva's financial strength rating (A Stable, £407bn AUM) to competitors, nor comparing the 0.37% combined cost to alternatives. No evidence addresses whether 6,900 funds, private market access, or online tools were compared to other providers' offerings. P13 notes that existing pension schemes were 'expensive' and had 'no adviser servicing' but provides no quantified comparative analysis.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts provider characteristics (financial strength A-rated, 6,900 funds, private markets access, online tools, governance) as justification, but research documents do not show comparative analysis of these features against the three alternative platforms (Aberdeen Elevate, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime) in the shortlist.
      - Report states 'competitive charges' and investment strategy 'allows for some active management, whilst keeping costs low', but P10 shows combined aggregated fund costs of 0.37% (0.18% + 0.29% + 0.01% transaction), and no evidence compares this to alternatives considered.
      - Report notes Aviva's £407bn AUM as dated 31 Dec 2024, but research documents (P5) cite Aviva data as at 31 Dec 2020 ('18 million customers'), indicating outdated source material or inconsistent dates.
      - Report claims investment pathways were considered and rejected as insufficiently tailored, but no evidence shows which specific pathway option was evaluated or why the four generic pathways failed the client's stated objectives OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3.
      - Research document P1 explicitly states 'No Platform DNA analysis was carried out' and 'No filters were applied', yet report asserts Aviva was selected based on choice, expertise, value and governance features — implying a benchmarking exercise that the research does not evidence.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Comparative analysis of the four SIPP platforms on the shortlist (Aviva Platform, Aberdeen Elevate, Aegon Retirement Choices, Wealthtime Platform) — P1 shows all four matched filters but 'No Platform DNA analysis was carried out'., Quantified cost comparison between Aviva Platform's total ongoing charge and the three competitor platforms., Comparative evidence of fund choice, investment manager expertise, governance processes, or innovation between Aviva and alternatives., Detailed analysis of which investment pathway option was considered and the specific reasons for rejection tied to client objectives OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3., Evidence that the report's dated Aviva metrics (S&P rating A Stable, £407bn AUM as at 31 Dec 2024, 6,900 funds) were current at the time of the recommendation or verified from current sources.
    The report provides generic provider justifications that are not client-specific and not comparative. The seven reasons cited for Aviva (financial strength, choice, expertise, value, convenience, governance, innovation) are absolute claims about the provider's characteristics, not explanations of why Aviva was superior to competitors on the shortlist. The research documents confirm that four platforms were shortlisted and that Whole of Market was used in product selection (P5, P9), but P1 explicitly states 'No Platform DNA analysis was carried out', meaning the quantitative benchmarking tool referenced in the DNA features section was not applied. This creates a discrepancy: the report implies a rigorous provider comparison, but the research shows no comparative analysis was performed. For investment strategy, the rationale links two of the recommended funds to the client's stated risk profile (claiming they 'reflect your risk profile both individually and combined'), but the report does not evidence whether these specific funds were compared to other balanced solutions available within Aviva or other providers. The cost efficiency claim ('competitive', 'keeping costs low') is supported by a 0.37% combined total (P10), but no benchmark comparisons are provided. Investment pathways were considered but rejected; the report states 'You require a more tailored solution than any of the four generic solutions' and references the client's withdrawal requirements and risk profile, but no evidence shows how each of the four pathways was evaluated against the client's documented objectives or why customisation was essential. The existing arrangement comparisons (P13) note that Aviva, Zurich, and Scottish Widows were expensive and lacked adviser servicing, but quantified differences are not shown. The claim that investment pathways lack tailoring is not corroborated by evidence comparing pathway design to the CIP strategy deployed. The report's assertion that the CIP approach provides 'enhanced oversight, risk management, and adaptability' (P2) compared to a single multi-asset fund is asserted but not evidenced by comparative fund research or independent analysis. The provider selection appears to rest on panel/CIP membership (the funds are part of the adviser's CIP, monitored by investment committee, P2, P4), which is a legitimate justification if disclosed; however, the report does not explicitly state that provider choice was constrained to a documented panel or that Aviva was selected because it is part of the firm's panel offering. The absence of filter-based DNA analysis (P1) and the statement that 'No filters were applied' undermines the premise that rigorous benchmarking was used, yet the report narrates the decision as if substantive comparison occurred. The Aviva financial strength rating and AUM are stated without current verification, and the reference to an S&P rating dated 31 Dec 2024 in a report produced in October 2025 suggests reliance on marketing materials rather than independent current research.

  [G6.3] Personalised language linked to individual circumstances
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The canonical model asserts that all 20 recommendations carry isClientSpecific = true, indicating the suitability report claims each recommendation includes personalised language linked to the client's individual circumstances.
    File:     The suitability report [P13] states 'This report is designed to explain how and why my recommendations can help meet these needs and objectives' and references that advice is 'based upon the information you have disclosed.' The Fact Find [P5] records the client's goal: 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month. ATR 5.' The Policy Research document [P14] notes under 'Summary of the Advice': 'Client has come to us seeking advice because he has a lot of pensions that he doesn't understand how they work and ideally wants to put them all together to make it easier for him to understand and utilise in his retirement.' For specific recommendations, the Policy Research [P15] lists recommendations (Aviva: Switch, Zurich: Switch, People's Pension: Retain, Scottish Widows: Encash, Standard Life: Switch) but the 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' field is blank for most entries, and reasons given are generic ('Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing'; 'Expensive, no adviser servicing'; 'Active workplace pension'; 'Small pot').
    Discrepancies:
      - The canonical model claims 20 client-specific recommendations, but the suitability report extract [P13] shows only one recommendation text fragment visible ('Switch your Aviva pension into a new advised Aviva pension - £3,744.36'). The full narrative rationale for each of the 20 recommendations is not present in the retrieved passages.
      - The Policy Research document [P15] shows the 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' field is blank for most recommendations (Aviva, Zurich, Scottish Widows, Standard Life), contradicting the assertion that each recommendation is linked to a stated objective.
      - The reasons given in the Policy Research document are brief and generic ('Expensive, no adviser servicing'; 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing') rather than client-specific rationales tied to the client's stated need to 'consolidate to help him get his head around what he has.'
    The requirement asks whether the suitability report contains a clear, client-specific explanation for each recommendation linked to the client's stated objectives. The client's objectives are clearly recorded in the Fact Find [P5]: consolidation to simplify understanding, retirement in 2 years, and household income of £1,700 net per month at ATR 5. The Policy Research [P14] confirms consolidation as the core objective. However, the retrieved passages do not contain the full narrative rationale for each of the 20 recommendations asserted to be client-specific by the canonical model. The Policy Research table [P15] shows that for four of the five arrangements mentioned, the field 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' is left blank, suggesting either the recommendations are not explicitly linked to objectives in the working documents, or the link is not captured in the retrieved evidence. Where reasons are provided (e.g., 'Expensive, no adviser servicing'), they are generic criteria rather than client-specific explanations addressing why, for instance, switching a particular arrangement helps the consolidation goal or supports the retirement income need. The Fact Find [P5] does not show evidence that alternatives were considered and rejected with reasoned explanation; the Policy Research [P14] states 'Current set up isn't working because it is too much information for him to understand. Wants to simplify things' but does not detail which specific alternatives were evaluated for each recommendation or why they were not suitable. The visible extract from the suitability report [P13] does not provide the detailed personalised narrative per recommendation; only one recommendation is visible in the passages. The canonical assertion that all 20 recommendations are client-specific cannot be verified against the retrieved evidence, as the detailed rationale narratives are absent from the pack. This does not mean they do not exist in the full report, but the pack does not support the characterisation of each as containing personalised language linked to individual circumstances in the way the requirement demands.
