  [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report presents four alternatives considered and rejected: (1) Leave existing Aviva pension untouched – rejected because 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' and lifestyling funds will move portfolio outside agreed risk range; (2) Leave existing Aviva pension and switch investment funds – rejected because 'there are no Multi-Asset funds and only a limited range of other options'; (3) Leave existing Zurich pension untouched – rejected because Zurich does not offer a single multi-asset fund aligned to risk rating 5, would require combining two funds with one in cash holdings leaving 20-30% out of market, Zurich does not allow adviser management which client wants, and transfer would limit flexible withdrawal options; (4) Switch into employer's pension arrangement – rejected because it is current workplace scheme with ongoing contributions so transfer would forfeit employer contributions, funds do not meet risk profile, plan is 0.06% more expensive than recommended solution, and £4.50 annual admin charge. The report also presents a research section listing 'Option 5 - Set up a new pension plan' as the recommended option with no rejection reasons. The report states switching is recommended to consolidate existing pensions into Aviva Platform with lower costs, suitable funds matching risk profile, adviser management capability, and flexible access.
    File:     The pension switch report (P1–P5, P9–P11, P14, P17–P20) provides detailed comparisons showing: For Aviva existing arrangement alone: effect of switching shows £10 decrease at 0.3% decline or similar minimal losses across growth scenarios (P2, P14). For Zurich existing arrangement alone: effect of switching shows £2,000 (2.0% decrease) at low growth, £12,000 (9.4% decrease) at mid growth, £15,000 (10.0% decrease) at high growth (P5). For Standard Life existing arrangement alone: detailed comparison tables present projected maturity values (P3). File notes (P22) show: 'Aviva – Alternative option: Only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR. Discounted switch.' 'Zurich – Alternative option: Zurich do not have risk level 5 funds...For these reasons we would not recommend an internal fund switch.' 'Standard Life – Alternative option: Discounted as all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva.' Research report (P7–P8, P15) shows SIPP platform selection with Aviva Platform recommended, noting 'No Platform DNA analysis was carried out' and 'No additional justification text has been entered' for platform selection.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Aviva existing plan is '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' but pension switch report shows effect of switching Aviva yields minimal decrease (£10 or 0.3% at low growth, similar at other scenarios), creating ambiguity about whether the new arrangement is genuinely cheaper or if switching produces a cost benefit
      - Report rejects leaving Zurich untouched citing inability to achieve risk rating 5 with single multi-asset fund, but the rejection reason text in the report incorrectly references 'Scottish Widows' when discussing Zurich: 'Currently, Scottish Widows does not offer a single multi-asset fund that aligns with your risk rating of 5' (this is in the Zurich rejection section but the provider name is wrong)
      - Report states for employer scheme (Aviva) that 'the plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' but this appears to be same Aviva arrangement discussed earlier as existing arrangement, creating confusion about whether this refers to a different version or iteration of Aviva
      - Pension switch report (P5) shows effect of switching Zurich at mid-growth as £12,000 (9.4% decrease) but report rejection simply states Zurich lacks suitable funds and adviser management without quantifying the cost or performance detriment explicitly
      - Report does not present rejection reasons for options as individual entries in research section – Option 5 (recommended) and specific arrangement options (People's Pension retain, Scottish Widows encash, Aviva switch, Zurich switch, Standard Life switch) appear in research file without stated rejection reasons in alternatives structure, though the recommendations extraction shows alternatives for each existing arrangement separately
    The report identifies and rejects four main alternatives with reasons linked to client objectives (OBJ3 – cost efficiency and flexibility). Rejection reasons are stated and client-specific: lifestyling fund risk drift, lack of Multi-Asset funds, absence of adviser management, limited retirement flexibility, and loss of employer contributions are all tied to the client's stated needs and agreed risk rating. However, discrepancies exist between what the report asserts and what supporting documents show. First, the claim that the Aviva plan is '0.06% more expensive' requires scrutiny: the pension switch comparison (P2, P14) shows minimal negative effect (£10 or 0.3% decline), which does not clearly support a cost advantage narrative for switching, particularly when an initial adviser charge of £3,305 is mentioned (P3) and ongoing adviser charge of 1.00% (P3) applies to the new solution. Second, the report's rejection of Zurich (alternative 3) contains a provider name error: the quoted rejection reason states 'Scottish Widows does not offer a single multi-asset fund' but this appears in the section rejecting Zurich, suggesting either transcription error or misattribution. The file notes (P22) correctly reference Zurich's lack of risk level 5 funds. Third, the file notes show more nuanced research reasoning (e.g., Standard Life funds are 'more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva') that is simplified or omitted from the report's stated rejection reasons. The research file (P7–P8, P15) shows platform selection with no documented justification ('No additional justification text has been entered'), which weakens the evidence that the Aviva Platform recommendation was actively evaluated against alternatives. The pension switch report does provide detailed comparison tables across growth scenarios for each arrangement, supporting that research was done, but the report's narrative does not always align precisely with quantified findings (e.g., the £0.06% cost difference assertion versus the minimal £10 effect shown in tables). The overlay assessment: no vulnerability indicators in the evidence materially affect this requirement's assessment.

  [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report provides five existing arrangements with advice actions: (1) People's Pension — Retain, with rationale that it is a current workplace scheme with continuing employer and employee contributions until retirement, and transferring away would forfeit employer contributions; (2) Scottish Widows — Encash, with rationale to withdraw the full amount under small pots rules, receiving an estimated £170 net from a £200 example calculation, placing proceeds into savings without detracting from retirement plan; (3) Aviva — Switch, with rationale citing lower costs ("0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution"), lifestyling fund risk drift, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, and ongoing monitoring; (4) Zurich — Switch, with rationale citing lower costs, lifestyling fund concerns, greater investment choice, enhanced flexibility, adviser charging, and ongoing monitoring; (5) Standard Life — Switch, with rationale citing lower costs ("0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution"), lifestyling fund risk drift, greater investment choice, enhanced flexibility, adviser charging, and ongoing monitoring. The report states each recommendation will involve switching to Aviva Platform Personal Pension as the receiving scheme.
    File:     The evidence pack contains policy information documents (P7: Aviva Policy Info showing plan value £3,744.36 invested in Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation with auto-switching/lifestaging; P1, P4, P6: Standard Life Policy illustration and valuation showing plan number D2301334000, value £2,994.30 as at 30 September 2025, with lifestyling considerations; P9: Zurich Personal Pension Illustration showing plan P10026-719-DL, estimated retirement value £106,000 in today's prices; P13: Peoples Pension policy info showing investment profiles available; P2: Scottish Widows plan schedule). The SIPP research report [P19] documents the agreed risk level as 5. The policy research table [P3, P5] confirms plan types, start dates, and fund values for all five arrangements. Terms and conditions [P11, P14-P18] describe retirement options including income drawdown, capped drawdown, flexi-access drawdown, and annuity options available across providers. The client agreement [P20, P22] describes fee structures for ongoing advice and review. However, no evidence document articulates the suitability rationale linking each arrangement outcome to the client's stated objectives, needs, and circumstances; no evidence compares alternatives for each arrangement or justifies provider/platform selection against options considered; no evidence supports the cost comparisons stated in the recommendations.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Aviva recommendation has cost impact of '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' while simultaneously asserting 'Lower Costs' as the rationale for switching, creating an internal contradiction—the plan is not cheaper but more expensive.
      - Report states Standard Life recommendation involves cost comparison of '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', inconsistent with the 'Lower Costs' rationale given.
      - Report cites lifestyling fund risk drift as rationale for switching Aviva, Zurich, and Standard Life, but evidence [P7] confirms Aviva itself holds 'Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation' with documented 'Lifestage Investment Approach' — auto-switching investment approach — meaning the recommended receiving scheme also employs lifestyling, contradicting the rationale.
      - Report provides no client-specific linkage between the five recommendations and the client's stated objectives; rationales for Zurich and Standard Life switches are verbatim or near-verbatim repetitions across both arrangements, lacking personalization to the individual circumstances of each plan.
      - Report asserts the People's Pension should be retained because 'you and your employer are still contributing to this and will continue to until your retirement', but provides no evidence of the client's actual retirement date, employer contribution status, or confirmation of intent to remain in employment until retirement.
      - Report estimates Scottish Widows encashment proceeds as '£170 net' based on '£200 pension fund withdrawal' calculation, but policy research [P5] and Peoples Pension policy info [P13] show Scottish Widows fund value as part of the schedule; the actual fund value (£211.32 per derived figures) does not cleanly reconcile to the £200 example used, raising whether the calculation is accurate to this specific arrangement.
      - Report provides generic statements about 'Greater Investment Choice', 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility', and 'Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments' as rationales for switching but offers no evidence of comparison between retirement options, fund ranges, or adviser charging arrangements available in each existing scheme versus the proposed Aviva Platform.
      - Report states rationale for switching but does not clearly state whether the adviser is recommending each switch, whether the adviser will arrange/implement the switch, or the intended outcome (whether full transfer, partial transfer, or phased approach), which is material to suitability.
    The report satisfies the formal requirement that each of the five existing arrangements carries a stated recommended outcome (Retain, Encash, Switch, Switch, Switch) and that each has articulated rationale text. However, the comparison against the evidence reveals material defects in the suitability foundation. First, the cost rationale is internally contradictory: the report asserts 'Lower Costs' as the reason to switch Aviva and Standard Life, but then states the receiving scheme is '0.06%' and '0.08%' respectively 'more expensive' than the existing arrangement. A receiving scheme that is more expensive does not deliver lower costs. This contradiction is not explained by any external fact or guard; it is an error within the report's own reasoning. Second, the lifestyling rationale contradicts the evidence: the report recommends switching away from Aviva because its lifestyling funds will drift outside the client's risk range, yet evidence [P7] shows Aviva Policy Info confirms the recommended receiving plan—Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation—uses an identical 'Lifestage Investment Approach'. The client would not escape the risk drift problem by moving to the recommended scheme. Third, the rationales for the Zurich and Standard Life switches are substantially identical, suggesting boilerplate language rather than arrangement-specific analysis. Fourth, no evidence supports the rationale texts with client objective linkage, alternative consideration, or provider comparison. The advisory documents, policy research tables, and terms of service do not show analysis of why each arrangement's outcome is suitable to this client's particular circumstances, needs, and goals. The People's Pension rationale depends on unstated facts: the client's confirmed retirement date and the persistence of employer contributions, neither of which appear in the evidence. The Scottish Widows encashment calculation works from a £200 example, but the fund value (£211.32) is not clearly mapped to this figure, and no evidence explains the tax net-of-£170 outcome. Fifth, the report does not clearly state for each switch whether the adviser is recommending it, will arrange it, or intends a specific outcome, which COBS 9.4.1R requires. The evidence contains no corroborating research, no comparison of alternatives considered and rejected, and no articulation of how each recommendation addresses the client's documented objectives. The extraction notes flagged internal inconsistencies on charge comparisons and fund value reconciliation; those inconsistencies remain unresolved in the evidence pack and undermine confidence in the cost-based rationale. Under the decision logic for this requirement, the presence of outcome statements and rationale texts without client-specific linkage to objectives, supported comparison of alternatives, or coherent cost justification constitutes a material gap requiring clarification.

  [G6.6] Where a change is suggested, the report states whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report contains five recommendations with actions: Switch Aviva (recommended, adviser will implement), Switch Zurich (recommended, adviser will implement), Switch Standard Life (recommended, adviser will implement), Retain People's Pension (recommended), and Encash Scottish Widows (recommended). Each change is stated with an intended outcome: three switches aim to 'better align with financial objectives and long-term retirement planning' with variations, retention preserves employer contributions, and encashment consolidates a small pot into accessible savings. Implementation details are provided: adviser will implement the three switches, expected timescale is 10-15 working days, cancellation rights are 30 days, and out-of-market risk is disclosed as client-specific. The report states 'I recommend that you complete the nomination of beneficiaries' form' and provides next steps addressing access timescale, performance guarantees, and product literature.
    File:     The case file contains product terms and conditions for the Aviva Platform Personal Pension (including charges, income drawdown options, cancellation, and death benefits), a risk profile report confirming agreed risk level 5 (Balanced), a SIPP research report showing investment recommendations (CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio), and policy information for the People's Pension and Zurich arrangements. The file establishes the platform and product mechanics but does not contain independent corroboration of the adviser's statement that implementation will occur, who specifically will carry it out beyond the generic 'Adviser' label, or client-specific rationales linking each recommendation to the client's stated objectives. The file confirms the Aviva platform exists and supports the stated features but does not independently verify the adviser's commitment to implement.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states adviser will implement three switches but does not name a specific individual adviser or firm responsible for implementation, only the role 'Adviser'. The file does not clarify who that adviser is.
      - The report provides generic intended outcomes ('better align with financial objectives') shared across three different recommendations without client-specific detail explaining why each particular switch is suitable for this client's circumstances.
      - The report does not state whether the client has instructed these changes or whether the adviser is recommending them based on the suitability analysis; 'Recommended' appears as advice status but the distinction between adviser-initiated recommendation and client-instructed execution is not explicit.
      - The encashment recommendation cites small pot rules and estimated proceeds but does not explain why encashment (rather than retention or transfer) aligns with the client's stated retirement goals or financial needs.
    The report clearly states five actions and labels each 'Recommended', satisfying the first limb that a recommendation is made. The report also states adviser will implement the three switches and provides an expected timescale (10-15 working days) and cancellation rights (30 days), satisfying the second limb that implementation and timeline are disclosed. The third limb—intended outcome—is partially met: the report states outcomes for each recommendation, but three of the five (the three switches) share identical or near-identical language ('better align with financial objectives and long-term retirement planning') despite applying to different existing arrangements and different funds. This generic language does not demonstrate that each outcome is tailored to the client's individual circumstances or objectives. The retention recommendation's outcome (preserve employer contributions) is specific and reasoned. The encashment outcome (consolidate into accessible savings) is stated but not linked to the client's retirement timeline or financial needs as revealed in the case. The report does not contradict itself on these points, but the generic framing of intended outcomes for the switches falls short of demonstrating that each recommendation is justified by reference to this particular client's goals. The implementation section confirms adviser responsibility but does not name the adviser or firm. Under the comparison method 'PresenceOnly', the guard states 'An implementation step handled by the provider rather than the adviser is still a stated step; the finding is silence, not who happens to act.' The adviser has stated it will implement; the silence is on the identity of the adviser, not on whether implementation occurs. That silence does not create a discrepancy between report and file: it is a gap in the report itself. The three switch recommendations carry identical or near-identical intended outcomes despite different source arrangements and different target products, which suggests either boilerplate language or incomplete differentiation of rationale. This is a weakness in the report's reasoning, not a contradiction between report and file.

  [G6.7] Recommendations that present multiple possible outcomes without stating which is recommended and why
  Potential Concern — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report makes five discrete recommendations, each with a single action specified: (1) Switch your Aviva pension into a new advised Aviva pension (Recommended); (2) Switch your Zurich pension into a new advised Aviva pension (Recommended); (3) Switch your Standard Life pension into a new advised Aviva pension (Recommended); (4) Retain your People's Pension plan (Recommended); (5) Encash your Scottish Widows pension plan (Recommended). Each recommendation is marked adviceStatus 'Recommended' and each carries a rationale topic linked to objective OBJ3, covering Lower Costs, Lifestyling, Investment Choice, Adviser Charging, Ongoing Monitoring, Diversification, and Enhanced Retirement Flexibility. The report does not extract a field presentsUnresolvedOptions, optionsPresented[], or optionsPresented[]/isRecommended.
    File:     File notes [P1] and [P2] record alternatives considered for each pension plan. For Aviva: 'Only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR.. Discounted switch.' For Zurich: 'Zurich do not have risk level 5 funds, a blend of a 7 and a 2 has been used...The blend of funds have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva marginally. For these reasons we would not recommend an internal fund switch.' For Standard Life: 'Discounted as all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva.' For People's Pension: 'Peoples Pension – alternative option' with note 'No alternative as Defaqto risk rates all of them as a 9'. For Scottish Widows: 'Scottish widows – alternative option – 30% liquid fund and 70% of Portfolio three' followed by 'Discounted as no servicing and reduced flexibility with withdrawing, so no more research was done.' File notes thus evidence that alternatives were researched and rejected with stated reasons, resolving which option was chosen and why.
    The requirement checks whether the report presents multiple possible outcomes without explaining which is recommended and why. The extracted canonical model shows no field presentsUnresolvedOptions, optionsPresented[] or optionsPresented[]/isRecommended, indicating the report itself does not record a structure in which unresolved options appear. Each of the five recommendations carries a single, definitive action (Switch, Retain, or Encash) marked 'Recommended', with rationale linked to objectives. The file notes [P1] and [P2] establish that alternatives were researched for each existing arrangement and rejected with stated reasons: Aviva alternative rejected because the only mixed-asset option was risk-rated 4 and did not meet client's agreed risk tolerance (ATR); Zurich internal switch rejected because blended funds underperformed Aviva and the priority was consolidation; Standard Life rejected because funds were more expensive; People's Pension retained because alternatives were all rated risk 9 (unsuitable); Scottish Widows encashed because alternatives lacked servicing and flexibility. The guard notes that a client decision recorded in file notes resolves an option the report left open. Here, the file notes resolve each recommendation by documenting the adviser's decision pathway and reasons for rejecting alternatives. The report itself states each recommendation plainly without presenting them as options the client must choose between. There is no evidence of an unresolved recommendation—each carries a single recommended action with supporting rationale in the report, and file notes document the decision rationale behind rejection of alternatives. The requirement is satisfied by the presence of a clear recommendation and the absence of presented-but-unresolved alternatives.

  [G6.8] Recommendations state what action will be taken, when, and by whom where that is material to suitability
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states implementation timescale of '10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes' for the Switch recommendations. The report identifies responsible party as 'Adviser' for three Switch actions. At solution level, expected timescale is '10-15 working days'. For specific recommendations, the actions are: Switch (3 instances), Retain (1 instance), and Encash (1 instance). The report includes next steps covering out-of-market risk disclosure, access timescale, performance guarantee disclaimer, product literature reference, nomination of beneficiaries completion ('after the transfer process is begun'), and cancellation rights (30 days). The report states 'I recommend that you complete the nomination of beneficiaries' form that will be provided to you after the transfer process is begun' and 'Should you require access to these funds in the future it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete.' The responsible party for the Encash recommendation and the Retain recommendation are not explicitly stated in the extracted implementation data.
    File:     The product literature (Aviva Terms and Conditions and Key Features) confirms standard processing timescales and procedures but does not corroborate the specific 10-15 working day timescale stated in the report, nor does it specify who (adviser, provider, or client) is responsible for initiating or completing each action. The terms address income drawdown income payment changes requiring 'at least 10 working days' notice before existing payment dates, fund closure requiring '30 days in advance' notification, and general investment instructions, but these are procedural conditions rather than implementation timescales for the recommended actions. The Key Features document states 'You should retain the services of a financial adviser. You'll need an adviser if you want to make certain changes to your Pension Portfolio' but does not specify implementation responsibility for pension transfers or encashments. Scottish Widows Policy Info confirms the plan exists but provides no implementation timescale or process detail. The intelliflo planning scenarios are forward-looking analyses with no implementation instruction detail. The Risk profile report contains no implementation guidance.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report assigns 'Adviser' as responsible party for three Switch recommendations but does not state who is responsible for the Retain recommendation (EA4) or the Encash recommendation (Scottish Widows).
      - The report states a general timescale of '10-15 working days' but does not specify whether this applies equally to all three Switch actions or whether individual arrangements (People's Pension, Scottish Widows encashment, Aviva switch) have different timescales.
      - The nomination of beneficiaries form is stated to be 'provided to you after the transfer process is begun' but no action is attributed to adviser or client to initiate or complete this step beyond the recommendation that the client 'complete' it.
      - The Encash recommendation for Scottish Widows (£211.32 plan) has no stated implementation timescale or responsible party, unlike the Switch recommendations.
      - The Retain recommendation (EA4 - Zurich at £103,439.24) has no stated implementation timescale or responsible party.
    The requirement asks whether recommendations state what action will be taken, when it will be taken, and by whom, where material to suitability. The report does state actions (Switch, Retain, Encash) and provides a general timescale (10-15 working days) and identifies the adviser as responsible for the three Switch recommendations. However, material gaps exist: (1) Two of five recommendations (Retain and Encash) lack explicit identification of responsible party. (2) The timescale is stated as a single range (10-15 working days) for all Switch actions without clarification of whether each individual arrangement follows this or whether variations apply. (3) For the Encash recommendation, which requires the client to understand a taxable consequence (small pots taxation at 75%), no timescale or implementation responsibility is stated. (4) The nomination of beneficiaries action is recommended as a step the client should complete, but no timescale or implementation guidance is provided. The 10-15 working day timescale is stated as provider service standard ('it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete') and thus satisfies the guard that a timescale quoted as provider service standard is a stated timescale. Naming the adviser firm (through 'Adviser' designation) rather than an individual satisfies the guard on 'by whom'. However, the absence of responsible party designation for the Retain and Encash recommendations, and the absence of specific timescale for the Encash action, means the requirement is not fully satisfied. The report's next steps section addresses disclosure and process matters (out-of-market risk, cancellation rights, product literature) rather than implementation responsibility. The file evidence does not contradict the stated timescales but neither corroborates them with specificity to these client's arrangements. The guard on timing materiality ('material where the client is out of the market or working to a deadline') applies here: the client is liquidating and reinvesting five existing arrangements simultaneously, making clear sequencing and responsibility material to understanding suitability.
      · Suitability Report (I)
        "it will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete"
      · Suitability Report (I)
        "I recommend that you complete the nomination of beneficiaries' form that will be provided to you after the transfer process is begun"
      · Suitability Report (I)
        "You have 30 days to review and consider your options"
      · Key Features (1).md [H] (P8)
        "You should retain the services of a financial adviser. You'll need an adviser if you want to make certain changes to your Pension Portfolio"

  [G6.9] Ancillary recommendations — beneficiary nomination, Power of Attorney, will review — are stated with an owner and are consistent with what the file records
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations unverified
    Report:   The report makes three estate-planning related assertions: (1) It recommends the client 'complete the nomination of beneficiaries' form that will be provided after the transfer process begins (page 16, topic NominationOfBeneficiaries, marked isClientSpecific: true). (2) The canonical model records that the client hasWill: true. (3) The canonical model records that isWillUpToDate: false. (4) The canonical model records that isPowerOfAttorneyGranted: false.
    File:     The Fact Find (Test 1) document at page 15 under 'Retirement Risk Replay' and 'Notes' sections contains blank fields for estate planning matters. No explicit statement appears in the retrieved passages confirming or denying whether the client has a will, whether it is up to date, or whether a Power of Attorney has been granted. The file records no evidence of these estate-planning statuses. The file does not record a beneficiary nomination status or recommendation.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts hasWill: true; file contains no corroborating evidence of will existence or status.
      - Report asserts isWillUpToDate: false; file contains no evidence addressing will currency or any review date.
      - Report asserts isPowerOfAttorneyGranted: false; file contains no evidence addressing Power of Attorney status.
      - Report recommends beneficiary nomination completion; file contains no prior nomination status or confirmation that a form will be provided by the provider.
    The report extracts three estate-planning status assertions (will existence, will currency, Power of Attorney status) and makes one ancillary recommendation (beneficiary nomination). The canonical model reflects these as definitive statements: the client has a will but it is not up to date; the client does not have a Power of Attorney. However, the Fact Find document provided contains blank or incomplete estate-planning sections and does not record these determinations. The beneficiary nomination recommendation is stated as an action the adviser will encourage ('I recommend that you complete the nomination of beneficiaries' form that will be provided to you after the transfer process is begun'), and responsibility is assigned implicitly to the client completing the form once provided. This is consistent with the guard that an ancillary recommendation the adviser will not implement is not a defect if responsibility is stated. The core issue is that the report makes definitive assertions about the client's will and Power of Attorney status without corroborating evidence in the file. The Fact Find does not record responses confirming a will exists, whether it is current, or whether a Power of Attorney is in place. Under the rule that evidence absent when the report asserts something registers as a gap, and that an assertion absent from the file is a finding when the report claims it, the absence of supporting documentation for these three estate-planning determinations constitutes an evidence gap. The beneficiary nomination recommendation carries an owner (the adviser recommends it, the client will complete the form), so it does not fail on that limb. However, the three status assertions lack file support.

CHK-007 — Costs, Charges and Value of the Recommendation
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 11 raised a concern (G7.1, G7.2, G7.3, G7.4, G7.5, G7.6, G7.7, G7.8, G7.9, G7.10, G7.11). Report states total platform + investment management + fund charges sum to 0.44% but does not reconcile this to the weighted fund charge approach shown in illustration [P8] which shows weighted average fund charge of 0.23% (implying different total); arithmetic: 0.21% + 0.22% + 0.23% = 0.66%, not 0.44%. Report dollar figures state £484.81 annual charge at 0.44%, which at current transfer values of £116,998.47 would represent £514.39 (0.44%), not £484.81; this implies calculation on fund value of £110,184.09, which matches neither the arrangement totals nor any stated transfer value. Derived figure from charge arithmetic: solution total ongoing £484.81 at 0.44% implies fund value £110,184.09; but individual platform charge £231.39 at 0.21% implies fund value £110,185.71 and investment management £253.43 at 0.22% implies £115,195.45; these three implied fund values are all different and none match the actual arrangement totals of £116,998.47. 18 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G7.1] All material costs disclosed — product, platform, fund, transaction, advice
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report discloses: (1) Platform charge: Aviva Platform Personal Pension 0.21% (£231.39 pa); (2) Investment management charge: 0.22% (£253.43 pa); (3) Fund charges: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 0.18% and CT Universal MAP Balanced 0.29%; (4) Total ongoing charge: 0.44% (£484.81 pa); (5) Initial advice fee: 3% of amount invested, £3,305.55 with current transfer values; (6) Transaction costs excluded with stated reasons: 'charges quoted in this report exclude the transaction costs of the underlying funds, as these are variable and subject to change based on factors such as market conditions and trading activity. Despite their variability, these costs are already accounted for in the fund's performance figures, which are presented net of all charges'; (7) Aggregated total disclosed: yes, 0.44% total ongoing; (8) Cumulative effect disclosed: yes, stated in personal illustration.
    File:     Evidence shows: (1) From Aviva illustration [P8]: Aviva charge 0.21%, investment charges for CT Universal MAP Balanced 0.29% and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 0.18%, matching report's stated fund charges exactly; (2) From KIID [P1]: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio fund ongoing charge 0.18%, matching report; (3) From suitability report [P3]: Platform 0.21%, Investment Management 0.22%, total 0.44% displayed in charge table, with statement that 'charge in monetary terms shown here may differ from illustration due to differences in calculation methods' and that 'annual recurring charges assume no investment growth'; (4) From Aviva Key Features [P6]: Adviser charges deducted on behalf and passed to adviser, subject to restrictions on cancellation, shown in illustration; (5) From suitability report [P17]: Initial advice fee stated as 3% of amount invested, £3,305.55 with current transfer values, paid directly by pension schemes on transfer; (6) Transaction cost exclusion stated in [P3] with detailed explanation matching report verbatim; (7) From Aviva illustration [P8]: OCF/TER charge shown with weighted average 0.23% for the two funds; (8) SIPP research report [P10] shows CT Universal MAP Balanced fund costs broken down as ongoing costs £159.77 (0.29%), transaction costs £145.05 (0.26%), total aggregated fund costs £304.82 (0.55%).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states total platform + investment management + fund charges sum to 0.44% but does not reconcile this to the weighted fund charge approach shown in illustration [P8] which shows weighted average fund charge of 0.23% (implying different total); arithmetic: 0.21% + 0.22% + 0.23% = 0.66%, not 0.44%
      - Report dollar figures state £484.81 annual charge at 0.44%, which at current transfer values of £116,998.47 would represent £514.39 (0.44%), not £484.81; this implies calculation on fund value of £110,184.09, which matches neither the arrangement totals nor any stated transfer value
      - Derived figure from charge arithmetic: solution total ongoing £484.81 at 0.44% implies fund value £110,184.09; but individual platform charge £231.39 at 0.21% implies fund value £110,185.71 and investment management £253.43 at 0.22% implies £115,195.45; these three implied fund values are all different and none match the actual arrangement totals of £116,998.47
      - Report's stated fund charges 0.18% and 0.29% are for HSBC and CT Universal MAP respectively, but illustration [P8] shows 'weighted average charge' of 0.23%, indicating the funds are not held in equal proportions; report does not state allocation percentages between the two funds
      - Report states charges exclude transaction costs with explanation they are in fund performance net of charges, consistent with [P3]; however SIPP research report [P10] separately quantifies CT Universal MAP transaction costs as £145.05 (0.26%) annually, suggesting transaction costs are material and separately identified in research documents but excluded from the report's aggregate charge presentation without noting this research evidence exists
    The report discloses all major cost layers: platform 0.21%, investment management 0.22%, fund charges 0.18% and 0.29%, initial advice fee 3% (£3,305.55), and ongoing adviser charges as per client agreement. Transaction costs are explicitly excluded with stated reasons. However, three material inconsistencies prevent full validation: First, the arithmetic does not reconcile. The report states 0.44% total ongoing charge equating to £484.81 annually, but this figure is internally inconsistent. At the stated fund values of £116,998.47 (sum of existing arrangements), 0.44% would be £514.39, not £484.81. Conversely, £484.81 at 0.44% implies a fund value of £110,184.09, which matches the 'recommended solution' total in the canonical model but differs from the sum of actual arrangement values. This suggests the charge calculation is based on a fund value projection or subset of arrangements not clearly identified in the report. Second, the allocation between the two funds is not disclosed. The report names two funds (HSBC 0.18% and CT Universal MAP 0.29%) but states a combined fund charge implicitly within the 0.44% total. The illustration [P8] shows 'weighted average charge 0.23%' but does not state allocation percentages. This is material because it affects whether a client can verify the calculation and whether platform terms are clearly disclosed. Third, the report's fund charges (0.18% and 0.29%) are corroborated by [P1] (KIID) and [P8] (illustration) respectively, and the platform and investment management charges are corroborated by [P8]; however the stated total of 0.44% cannot be derived by simple addition (0.21% + 0.22% + 0.23% = 0.66%, or 0.21% + 0.22% + 0.18% + 0.29% = 0.90%). The guard that 'charge figures in an illustration may include adviser fee while report excludes it' does not resolve this: the adviser fee is stated separately as 3% initial and ongoing per client agreement, and the 0.44% is stated as platform + investment management + fund charges only. The exclusion of transaction costs is properly disclosed with reasons and is consistent with [P2] and [P3], which note transaction costs are excluded from OCF figures and included in net performance. The initial advice fee of 3% (£3,305.55) is corroborated by [P17], which states 'initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested... in monetary terms this is £3,305.55 with current transfer values'. The charges table in the report [P3] shows platform 0.21% (£231.39), investment management 0.22% (£253.43), total 0.44% (£484.81), with explicit statement that 'charge in monetary terms shown here may differ from illustration due to differences in calculation methods'. This disclosure mitigates but does not cure the underlying arithmetic discrepancy, as the report does not explain why the £484.81 differs from the expected calculation or what fund value it is based upon. The required outperformance calculation of £4,672.00 at 4.24% implies fund value £110,188.68, further suggesting the 0.44% charge is being applied to a fund value around £110,000 rather than the stated arrangement totals of £116,998. The report does not reconcile these figures or explain the difference to the client.

  [G7.2] Initial and ongoing adviser fees, consistent with the client agreement
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts an initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested, stated as per the terms in the Client Agreement, equalling £3,305.55 with current transfer values. The report states this will be paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer. For ongoing fees, the report asserts 1% per annum of fund value, equalling circa £1,101.85 for the first year, paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme. The report lists five services included in the ongoing fee: reviewing attitude to risk and capacity for loss, reviewing ongoing suitability of the investment portfolio, market updates and legislative changes, access to a professional adviser team for ad-hoc advice, and access to full holistic financial planning.
    File:     The Client Agreement [P12] states fees are set out in the suitability report and charges confirmation, with two principal fees: one for initial investment arrangement work and one for ongoing reviews. The agreement confirms 3% initial fee for Investments & Pensions advice [P13], with a minimum of £1,350, and states the service is typically suitable for clients with at least £45,000 to invest. The agreement describes ongoing 1% annual review management charge on investment funds with a minimum fee of £100 per annum [P3]. The on-going service includes reviewing suitability at least annually, statements twice per year, cashflow analysis, family benefit advice, market updates, quarterly e-magazine, online portfolio access, and access to the adviser [P3]. The Client Agreement confirms these fees are exclusive of VAT unless otherwise stated [P12]. The agreement was signed by the client on 31/10/2025 [P11].
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the initial fee of £3,305.55 is 'as per the terms stated in our Client Agreement' but the extracted monetary figure £3,305.55 does not appear in any passage of the Client Agreement or supporting documents. The Client Agreement states the fee basis (3% of value invested) but not this specific calculated amount.
      - The report calculates the initial fee from £110,185 implied transfer value (3% × £110,185 = £3,305.55), but the actual sum of existing arrangements stated elsewhere in the report totals £116,998.47, creating an inconsistency in the base value used for the fee calculation.
      - The report states the ongoing fee 'would be equal to circa £1,101.85 for the first year' but this figure does not appear in the Client Agreement. It is derived from 1% of £110,185, again using the lower transfer value rather than the stated sum of existing arrangements of £116,998.47.
      - The Client Agreement [P3] specifies a minimum ongoing fee of £100 per annum; the report does not reference or acknowledge this minimum, though at 1% of the fund value stated, the £1,101.85 exceeds it.
    The requirement is to verify that adviser fees disclosed in the suitability report are consistent with the client agreement. The comparison can be performed because the Client Agreement is in the evidence pack. Both the report and the agreement confirm 3% initial fee and 1% ongoing fee, and these percentages match exactly. However, there is a material internal inconsistency within the report itself regarding the base value from which these percentages are calculated. The report states initial and ongoing fees are calculated on current transfer values of £110,185 (or in the case of the initial fee, 3% of an amount that yields £3,305.55). This figure appears nowhere in the evidence and contradicts the report's own statement elsewhere that existing arrangements sum to £116,998.47. The Client Agreement on file does not stipulate a particular transfer value or absolute fee amount—it states the percentage basis (3% and 1%) which the report does correctly cite. The agreement confirms these are 'as per the terms stated in our Client Agreement' [P9], and the percentages do match [P13] and [P3]. However, because the fee amounts stated (£3,305.55 and £1,101.85) rest on an undocumented transfer value of £110,185 that conflicts with the report's own figure of £116,998.47, the arithmetic supporting the specific amounts charged is not sound. This is an internal inconsistency in how the report applies its own agreed fee percentages to the client's actual position. The services described in the report match those in the Client Agreement [P3] and [P5]. The Client Agreement confirms the fees are exclusive of VAT unless stated otherwise [P12]; the report does not disclose VAT treatment. The agreement was duly executed on 31/10/2025 [P11], after the report date of 14/10/2025, confirming the fees were agreed.

  [G7.3] Cumulative impact of costs and charges
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report presents cumulative cost impact through Reduction in Yield (RIY) and Critical Yield figures across three growth scenarios (Low, Mid, High). For the recommended solution at Mid growth rate (2.95%), RIY is stated as 2.2%, Critical Yield as 4.8%, with an effect of switching of £12,000 or 9.4% decrease. For Low growth (0.00%), RIY is 2.1%, Critical Yield 1.9%, effect of switching £10,400 or 9.6% decrease. For High growth (5.88%), RIY is 2.3%, Critical Yield 7.8%, effect of switching £15,000 or 10.0% decrease. The report states total ongoing charges as 0.44% (£484.81), comprising platform charge of 0.21% (£231.39) and investment management charge of 0.22% (£253.43). Initial adviser charge of £3,305 is deducted from the new solution. Ongoing adviser charge of 1.00% is stated as approximately £1,101.85 for first year. The report states these charges are based on current values and fund performance is stated as past performance, not guaranteed.
    File:     [P1] provides the same RIY, Critical Yield and effect of switching figures as the report, confirming Low 0.3% RIY and 0.3% for existing, 2.1% RIY and 1.9% Critical Yield for recommended at Low growth; Mid 0.3% existing vs 2.2% recommended RIY with 4.8% Critical Yield and £12,000 effect; High 0.3% existing vs 2.3% recommended with 7.8% Critical Yield and £15,000 effect. [P3] (Illustration) shows product and investment charges reducing the fund from £129,000 to £126,000 (stated as 0.4% reduction in growth rate) and all charges reducing it to £115,000 (stated as 2.1% reduction in growth rate). [P18] shows Aviva platform charge of 0.21%, investment charges split across two funds with weighted average of 0.23%, no initial fund charge on reregistration. [P19] states total charge as 0.44% (£484.81 = 0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management). [P21] (Pension Switch Report) shows Standard Life existing charge as 0.52% and projects maturity values but uses different charge assumptions than the main report for individual plans. [P23] shows Standard Life/SL pension comparison with RIY of 0.6% for both existing and new solution, with effect of switching £2,000 or 1.6% decrease at mid growth, Critical Yield 3.2%. [P10] confirms illustration values at mid growth of £115,000 with notation that all charges reduce fund from £129,000 to £115,000, representing 2.1% reduction in growth.
    Discrepancies:
      - The arithmetic computation identifies that the 0.44% total charge (£484.81) at stated fund value of £110,185 implies a fund value of £110,184.09, which does not match any existing arrangement value (ranging from £211.32 to £103,439.24) nor the stated current total of approximately £110,000 or the computed total of £116,998.47
      - The Pension Switch Report [P21] states Standard Life existing charge as 0.52% but the main report's Pension Switch Charges table [P19] later shows Standard Life with current charge of 0.18% and impact of +0.26%, creating an internal contradiction about the baseline charge
      - The extraction ambiguity notes that the main report's narrative states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' for Standard Life while the Pension Switch Charges table shows +0.26% impact, a mathematical discrepancy of 0.18% vs 0.26%
      - The effect of switching figures (£10,400, £12,000, £15,000) are stated as monetary amounts but are derived as 9.6%, 9.4%, and 10.0% decreases respectively; the arithmetic does not reconcile if the base fund value for these percentages is not £108,000, £127,000, or £150,000 respectively (existing pension values), yet the new solution total is stated as approximately £110,000 implying the comparison may use mixed bases
      - The illustration [P3] shows product and investment charges of 0.4% reduction (£129,000 to £126,000) but does not explicitly add adviser charges in this presentation, while the report separately states adviser charges including 1% ongoing; the cumulative illustration does not transparently show all layers
      - The Pension Switch Report [P23] for Standard Life shows RIY of 0.6% for both existing and new solution at mid growth, which contradicts the main suitability report's focus on the recommended Aviva solution where recommended RIY is 2.2%; it appears a different plan recommendation is modelled in the appendix
      - Transaction costs are explicitly excluded from the report's charge figures [P19] with the statement that transaction costs are not included as they are variable, but this means the cumulative cost disclosed does not capture all material costs that affect fund growth
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: The precise fund value base to which the 0.44% total charge applies is not established; arithmetic implies £110,184.09 but this matches neither existing arrangement totals (£116,998.47) nor narrative descriptions (approximately £110,000), The basis and timing of the £3,305 initial adviser charge deduction is not clearly reconciled with the projected fund values; it is stated as deducted from new solution but not clear if this reduces the transfer value before or after illustration projections, The transaction costs excluded from the charge summary are never quantified or estimated, preventing full disclosure of cumulative cost impact, The interrelationship between the advice charges (initial £3,305 plus ongoing 1% reported as £1,101.85) and the illustrated growth projections is not transparent; it is unclear whether the 2.1% and 2.2% RIY figures incorporate or exclude the 1% ongoing adviser charge, The comparison between existing arrangements and recommended solution does not clearly show on a consistent base whether all five existing pensions are being consolidated or if certain plans are treated differently, The extent to which the Pension Switch Report recommendations differ from the main suitability report's recommendation is not explained; Standard Life appears as a recommendation in the appendix but is not the primary recommendation, creating ambiguity about which charges apply to the actual advised solution
    The requirement calls for cumulative impact of costs and charges to be consistent, arithmetically sound, and clearly explained. The comparison cannot be reliably performed because the fund value base for charge calculations is ambiguous and shifts across documents. The report states approximately £110,000 in new solution value, but the arithmetic of 0.44% charge (£484.81) implies £110,184, which does not reconcile to the computed sum of existing arrangements (£116,998.47). The RIY figures of 2.1% to 2.3% for recommended solution are corroborated by [P1] and [P3], and the effect of switching percentages (9.4% to 10.0%) are confirmed in [P1]. However, the monetary effect of switching (£10,400, £12,000, £15,000) cannot be validated without certainty on the base fund value. The charge layers are partially disclosed: platform 0.21%, investment management 0.22%, adviser initial £3,305, adviser ongoing 1% (stated as £1,101.85). However, the ongoing adviser charge of 1% is stated separately from the 0.44% product and platform charge, and it is not fully transparent how both are incorporated into the projected values. The illustration [P3] shows product and investment charge impact (0.4% RIY equivalent) and total charge impact (2.1% RIY equivalent), but does not break out adviser charges in this table. Transaction costs are explicitly excluded with a general statement that they are variable and already reflected in fund performance figures [P19], but no estimate is provided. The Pension Switch Report [P21] and [P23] introduce alternative plan comparisons (Standard Life showing RIY of 0.6% for both existing and new) which are not the primary recommendation, creating confusion about which charges have been analysed for the advised solution. The internal inconsistency in the extraction notes regarding Standard Life charges (0.52% vs 0.18% vs 0.26% impact) reflects genuine data conflicts between documents that suggest the switching analysis may be computed on wrong plan values, as flagged in the arithmetic review where EA5 impact is computed on Zurich's value not Standard Life's. These errors materially undermine confidence that the cumulative cost impact is arithmetically sound across all documents.

  [G7.4] Cost comparison with alternative options
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts a cost comparison on page 11 stating: 'Based on information supplied by your existing providers, our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans. This means that your proposed plan can underperform your current plan by £264 in the first year (in monetary terms).' The report presents a Pension Switch Charges table showing per-arrangement impacts: EA3 (Aviva) -0.06% (£-2.25), EA4 (Zurich) -0.49% (£-506.85), EA5 (Standard Life) +0.26% (£268.94). The report also discloses existing charges: People's Pension 0.50% fund + £4.50 admin; Scottish Widows 0.19% fund + 0.10% platform; Aviva 0.50% fund; Zurich 0.18% fund + £2.12 monthly admin; Standard Life 0.52% fund. Recommended solution charges are stated as Aviva Platform Personal Pension 0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management = 0.44% total (£484.81 on £110,185 implied value).
    File:     Provider documentation shows: Zurich policy info [P2] confirms 0.75% annual management charge (0.75% pa), with bid-offer spread of 5%. Peoples Pension policy info [P7] confirms 0.50% ongoing management charge. Scottish Widows policy info [P12] shows charges reducing growth from 2.7% to 2.4% after inflation, reduction in yield 0.3% per year.     Discrepancies:
      - Report states comparison basis is -0.24% cheaper overall, but per-arrangement table shows EA3 -0.06%, EA4 -0.49%, EA5 +0.26%: arithmetic of these three does not sum to -0.24%
      - Report EA5 (Standard Life) existing charge stated as 0.18% in comparison table, but existing arrangements section and provider documentation [P8] show Standard Life fund charge as 0.52% or 1.0% (before discount)
      - Report EA5 existing charge £186.19 implied at 0.18% on £103,439, but that is Zurich's fund value, not Standard Life's £3,002
      - Report EA5 impact +0.26% (£268.94) computed on fund value £103,438.46, which is Zurich's value, not Standard Life's £3,002
      - Report states Zurich existing charge 0.93% (£961.98), but Zurich policy info [P2] and [P15] show 0.75% annual management charge plus bid-offer spread 5%, not 0.93%

      - Report states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' for Aviva EA3 (suggesting the new plan is cheaper), but table shows EA3 impact -0.06% (meaning £-2.25 saving, so recommended is cheaper by 0.06%)
      - Report projected values show new solution produces £12,000 or 9.4% decrease vs existing [P21], but report states Aviva recommendation expected to underperform by only £264 (contradiction in scale and scope of cost impact)

    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Standard Life fund value and actual charge rate in context of recommendation (0.52% stated in arrangements, 1.0% in illustration with -0.5% discount; unclear which applies to comparison), Aviva recommended plan total fund value to verify £484.81 charge relates to correct consolidation amount, Basis of -0.24% overall comparison: whether including all five arrangements or subset, Adviser fee treatment: report mentions 3% initial fee (£3,305.55) paid by schemes on transfer, but this is not included in the 0.44% ongoing charge comparison, creating unclear total cost picture, Ongoing adviser fee from Client Agreement referenced but not disclosed in charges section, Scottish Widows small pots encashment treatment: £211.32 plan noted for encashing but its charge impact not separately modelled in comparison
    The comparison cannot be performed reliably due to foundational arithmetic errors. The per-arrangement table contains data misalignment: rows for EA5 (Standard Life, value £3,002) are computed using Zurich's value (£103,439), making the Standard Life charge comparison arithmetically impossible. The report states EA5 existing charge as 0.18% and £186.19, but 0.18% of £103,439 = £186.19, confirming the row is using the wrong plan's value. The report's overall -0.24% saving figure does not reconcile to the three individual impacts shown (-0.06%, -0.49%, +0.26%), which when weighted by approximate fund values would not yield -0.24%. The Zurich charge is stated as 0.93% (£961.98 on £103,439) but provider documentation [P2] [P15] indicates 0.75% annual management charge, creating a 0.18 percentage point discrepancy. Standard Life's charge is ambiguous: stated as 0.52% in arrangements but provider illustration [P8] shows 1.0% fund charge with -0.5% discount = 0.5% net; unclear which basis applies to the recommendation. The report's recommendation is presented as saving 0.24% annually (£264 first year), yet [P21] projects the new solution underperforms by £12,000 (9.4%) over the period to age 75, a magnitude far exceeding the stated annual charge differential. The report discloses an initial adviser fee of 3% (£3,305.55) but does not aggregate this with ongoing charges to show total cost impact. Transaction costs and bid-offer spread are excluded from the comparison with acknowledgement but no impact quantified. The critical yield required (4.8% per [P21]) is not explained in the main comparison as a cost or benefit trade-off. The file notes [P18] discount several alternatives on cost grounds (Zurich more expensive than Aviva, Standard Life more expensive), but the comparison table does not show consistent costing for these plans. A guard explains: comparisons may use different bases (with/without adviser fees), but here the inconsistency is within the same comparison table and relates to which plan's value each charge is based on, not a legitimate difference in scope. The Commonwealth finds the comparison internally contradictory and arithmetically unreliable.

  [G7.5] Benefit versus cost — fair value
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts 'Lower Costs - The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension' and states that 'Based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, including our advice fees, the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year up to age 75 in order to meet the same value as your current plan.' The report identifies six benefits to justify the recommendation: Lower Costs, Lifestyling, Greater Investment Choice, Adviser Charging Facilitation, Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments, and Enhanced Retirement Flexibility. The recommended funds carry charges of 0.18% and 0.29% respectively, totalling recommended ongoing charges of 0.44%. The report states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' when discussing Aviva and 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' for Standard Life. An initial adviser charge of £3,305.00 is deducted from the new solution, with an ongoing annual adviser charge of 1.00%.
    File:     The Pension Switch Charges table [P14] shows for the mid-growth scenario (2.95%): existing pension projected value £127,000 vs new solution £125,000, a decrease of £2,000 or 1.9%, with new solution critical yield of 0.3% and 0.6% respectively. The report's own extracted data reveals internal inconsistencies: EA3 (Aviva existing) shows 0.50% fund charge, yet the Pension Switch table indicates '-0.06% impact (meaning saving)' contradicting the narrative claim. EA5 (Standard Life existing £3,002.00) shows charge calculation of £186.19 at 0.18%, but the Pension Switch impact row (£268.94 at 0.26%) is computed on Zurich's value (£103,438.89), not Standard Life's value. The sum of existing arrangements is £116,998.47, but the recommended solution total shows £110,185 or approximately £110,000 in narrative. Fund performance data [P1, P2] shows Zurich Managed AP GTR: 44.32% (5yr), while recommended Aviva Risk 5 portfolio: 42.40%, meaning existing Zurich has outperformed the recommendation. The Aviva Personal Pension platform charge of £231.39 at 0.21% implies fund value £110,185.71, matching neither arrangement value nor total. The solution total ongoing £484.81 at 0.44% implies fund value £110,184.09, also matching no actual value in the case. Performance comparison [P1] states 'The recommended Aviva advised funds have demonstrated stronger performance compared to your existing Aviva holdings. However, they have not outperformed your current Zurich funds.'
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'Lower Costs' and 'lower overall cost structure' but provides no aggregated all-in cost comparison showing total charges (platform + investment + adviser) for existing vs recommended
      - Report states Aviva plan 'is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' but Pension Switch table shows -0.06% impact (a saving, implying recommended is more expensive)
      - Report states Standard Life plan 'is 0.08% more expensive' but Pension Switch table shows Standard Life impact at +0.26% (0.26% more expensive, not 0.08%)
      - Charge calculations for EA5 (Standard Life £3,002.00) use Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89) in the impact row, making the comparison mathematically incorrect and unreliable
      - Existing arrangements sum to £116,998.47 but recommended solution total is £110,185, a discrepancy of £6,813.47 not explained
      - Recommended fund charges (0.18% + 0.29% = 0.47% blended) plus ongoing adviser charge 1.00% = 1.47% total, but no comparison to existing total ongoing charges is provided in aggregated form
      - Platform charge arithmetic (£231.39 at 0.21%) implies £110,185.71 fund value, which does not match any actual arrangement or the stated total
      - Solution total ongoing (£484.81 at 0.44%) implies £110,184.09, again not matching actual fund values
      - Report states critical yield requirement of 4.8% 'is achievable' based on past performance but provides no contemporaneous performance data supporting this claim for the specific recommended funds
      - Performance tables show Zurich existing arrangement outperformed recommended Aviva on the most recent 5-year data (44.32% vs 42.40%), contradicting the benefit of switching to 'recommended funds' with 'demonstrated stronger performance'
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Aggregated all-in cost comparison (platform + investment + adviser charges) for existing arrangement vs recommended solution in a single reconcilable table, Correct charge calculations for each existing arrangement keyed to its actual fund value, not misallocated to another plan's value, Reconciliation of the £6,813.47 discrepancy between summed existing arrangements (£116,998.47) and recommended solution total (£110,185), Contemporaneous performance data for the specific recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C and CT Universal MAP Balanced C) over the same 5-year period used for existing comparisons, Clarification of which benefits, if any, are unique to the recommended solution and not available in the existing arrangements, particularly 'Greater Investment Choice' and 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility', Evidence that the £3,305 initial advice fee and 1.00% ongoing adviser charge structure has been disclosed to the client in the client agreement and is consistent across all advice documents
    The report's central claim that the recommended solution offers 'Lower Costs' and a 'lower overall cost structure' cannot be verified against the case file. No aggregated cost disclosure exists showing the total of platform, investment, and adviser charges in a format that permits comparison. The report's narrative cost comparisons contain arithmetic errors: the Aviva charge comparison contradicts its own Pension Switch table (text says 0.06% more expensive, table shows -0.06% saving); the Standard Life comparison misstates the impact by 0.18 percentage points (text 0.08%, table 0.26%). Most critically, the EA5 row in the Pension Switch table is computed using Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89) instead of Standard Life's own value (£3,002.00), rendering that comparison unreliable. The discrepancy between the summed existing arrangements (£116,998.47) and the recommended solution total (£110,185) of £6,813.47 is not explained. The apparent fund value reduction raises questions about whether charges, adviser fees, or transfer costs are embedded in this total—information not transparently disclosed. The report's claim that the critical yield of 4.8% 'is achievable' based on past performance is not supported by contemporaneous data for the specific recommended funds; the performance tables cite funds from research reports (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced, CT Universal MAP) without performance curves matching the 5-year comparison period used for existing holdings. The report also states recommended Aviva funds have not outperformed existing Zurich funds (44.32% vs 42.40% over 5 years), directly undermining the 'achievability' of the 4.8% outperformance needed to justify the switch. The benefits listed—Adviser Charging Facilitation, Ongoing Monitoring, Lifestyling avoidance, Greater Investment Choice—are stated in generic terms with no client-specific evidence that these cannot be obtained in the existing arrangements or that they justify a higher total cost structure when the required outperformance cannot be demonstrated. The guard requires supporting performance data before accepting a claim that required outperformance 'is achievable'; none is provided for the recommended funds specifically. The lack of aggregated cost disclosure, combined with internal arithmetic errors in cost comparisons and the absence of performance data supporting the critical yield claim, prevents verification that costs are justified by benefits consistent with the evidence.
