  [G7.6] Costs are excessive relative to the benefits provided
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report states two contradictory positions on cost. First, on page 11: 'Based on information supplied by your existing providers, our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans' (annual recurring charges basis). Second, on the same page 11: 'As we have agreed to be remunerated via the policy (initial and ongoing), our calculations showed that the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans' (including adviser fee basis). The report asserts that when adviser fees are included, the recommended plan requires 4.24% outperformance to break even.
    File:     No supporting documents were retrieved for this requirement. The check compares the report's own cost figures against themselves and against their stated bases, not against external evidence. The arithmetic check of the report's own data shows: (1) the existing arrangements sum to £116,998.47; (2) the recommended solution total is stated as £110,185; (3) a 0.44% ongoing charge on £110,185 yields £484.81, which the report lists as solution total ongoing; (4) the 4.24% outperformance figure at that basis implies a fund value of £110,188.68; (5) the -0.24% difference at 0.24% less expensive implies a fund value of £110,184.09. Neither the -0.24% nor the 4.24% figure can be arithmetically reconciled to the stated fund values or to each other when adviser fees are accounted for.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive (annual recurring charges only) but also states it is 4.24% more expensive when adviser fees are included. These cannot both be true of the same recommendation without specifying what adviser fee is being assumed.
      - The 4.24% outperformance figure, when reversed at the stated basis, implies a fund value of £110,188.68, which does not match any stated plan value (neither the £110,185 stated as the solution total nor the £116,998.47 existing arrangements total).
      - The -0.24% saving figure when applied as a percentage charge difference implies a fund value of £110,184.09, close to but not exactly matching the stated solution total of £110,185. The small discrepancy could be rounding, but the two competing cost figures (one stating a saving, one stating a 4.24% cost increase) cannot both apply to the same fund value without additional breakdown of what is included in each calculation.
      - The report does not itemise what adviser fee amount was assumed in the 4.24% calculation, making it impossible to verify whether the difference between -0.24% and 4.24% (a 4.48 percentage point gap) represents a plausible adviser fee rate or whether the calculations rest on different fund values or different charge bases.
    The requirement tests whether a higher-cost recommendation is justified by stated benefits. The report asserts two contradictory facts: that the plan is cheaper (by 0.24%) when measured by annual recurring charges alone, and that it is more expensive (by 4.24%) when adviser fees are included. The guard permits higher cost where benefits justify it; however, the precondition is that the cost comparison itself must be sound. The two figures cannot both be correct of the same recommendation. When the 4.24% is reversed arithmetically (dividing by 0.0424), it implies a fund value of approximately £110,189, and when -0.24% is reversed, it implies £110,184. Both are close to the stated £110,185 solution total, but the discrepancy between them (a 4.48 percentage point gap, equivalent to roughly £4,672 in annual charges on £110,185) is not explained. The report does not state what adviser fee rate or amount was baked into the 4.24% figure. This makes it impossible to verify whether the cost increase is real, what it comprises, or whether the stated benefits (which the report does not explicitly itemise in relation to this cost increase) actually justify it. The report claims both that charges fall and that the plan requires 4.24% outperformance — a logical impossibility unless the bases differ radically. Since the bases are stated as 'annual recurring charges' (for -0.24%) and 'including adviser fee' (for 4.24%), and no adviser fee rate is disclosed, the comparison cannot be verified as sound. This is not a matter of evidence absence; it is internal incoherence within the report's own assertions.
      · Suitability Report Test 1.pdf, page 11 [I_RecommendationsAndAdvice] (I_RecommendationsAndAdvice_page_11_cost_1)
        "Based on information supplied by your existing providers, our calculations show that the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans."
      · Suitability Report Test 1.pdf, page 11 [I_RecommendationsAndAdvice] (I_RecommendationsAndAdvice_page_11_cost_2)
        "As we have agreed to be remunerated via the policy (initial and ongoing), our calculations showed that the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than your current plans."

  [G7.7] Alternatives provide better cost or benefit to the client
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the Aviva Platform solution is recommended over existing arrangements. For Standard Life (D2301334000), it states 'Lower Costs - The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension' and 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution'. For Zurich (P10026-719-001/DL), it recommends switching for 'Greater Investment Choice' and other benefits but does not explicitly state cost comparison. For Aviva (SP50163762), the report states 'Lower Costs - The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure'. The report provides a Pension Switch Charges table comparing annual recurring charges in percentage and monetary terms for each plan.
    File:     The evidence shows: (1) Pension switch report indicates existing pensions total £110,185.60 with new solution day one at £106,880.60, creating a £3,305 shortfall in death benefit immediately after switch [P1]. (2) Comparison summary tables show for Zurich-only scenario: existing pension at 0.29% vs new solution at 0.6% [P2], indicating the new solution is MORE expensive. (3) For Aviva-only scenario: existing at 0.50% vs new solution at 0.6% [P2], again showing new solution is MORE expensive. (4) Standard Life alternative comparison shows existing at 0.59% vs new solution at 0.59% [P5]. (5) File notes record that alternatives were considered and rejected: Aviva 'only mixed asset Aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR', Zurich 'blend of funds have not performed as well', Standard Life 'all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva' [P18]. (6) The suitability report states Standard Life comparison shows charges of 0.52% existing vs recommendation implying lower cost [P8].
    Discrepancies:
      - The report claims 'Lower Costs' for Zurich switch but the pension switch report shows Zurich existing charge 0.29% vs new solution 0.6%, meaning the recommended solution is MORE expensive, not cheaper
      - The report claims 'Lower Costs' for Aviva switch but comparison tables show Aviva existing 0.50% vs new solution 0.6%, meaning new solution is MORE expensive
      - The report states for Standard Life 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' but the Pension Switch Charges table shows impact of +0.26% (£268.94), representing a 0.26% increase, not 0.08%
      - The derived figures show EA5 (Standard Life) existing charge £186.19 at 0.18% implies fund value £103,438.89, which is Zurich's value not Standard Life's £3,002, indicating the charge comparison for Standard Life is computed on the wrong plan
      - New solution day one value (£106,880.60) is £3,305 lower than existing (£110,185.60), creating immediate shortfall in death benefit
      - The cumulative charge impact tables show higher charges on recommended solution but report emphasizes 'Lower Costs' as primary reason for switch
    The core finding is that the report's primary stated justification for switching—'Lower Costs'—is directly contradicted by the pension switch report evidence. For Zurich, the existing charge is 0.29% and the new solution is 0.6%, making the recommendation 0.31 percentage points MORE expensive, not cheaper. For Aviva, existing is 0.50% and new is 0.6%, making it 0.10 percentage points MORE expensive. The report asserts cost savings but the comparative tables show cost increases. For Standard Life, the report claims the plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' but derived figures show the actual comparison calculation (£268.94 at 0.26%) implies a 0.26% difference, and moreover, the underlying calculation appears to reference Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89) rather than Standard Life's actual current value (£3,002), rendering that specific comparison unreliable. The file notes demonstrate alternatives were considered and explicitly rejected on non-cost grounds (fund range, performance, flexibility), yet the suitability report emphasizes cost as the primary benefit when the evidence shows cost is actually worse on the recommended path. The immediate impact shows a £3,305 reduction in fund value day one [P1], though this reflects the switching costs and not ongoing charges. The report does provide multi-criteria justification (investment choice, flexibility, adviser charging facilitation) but these are secondary to the leading 'Lower Costs' claim, which is factually unsupported by and contradicted by the pension switch report tables. The guard permits rejection of alternatives for 'evidenced non-cost reasons', and the file notes show this occurred (fund availability, performance, flexibility). However, the issue is that the report then claims cost advantage when none exists, rather than leading with the non-cost reasons that legitimately support the recommendation.

  [G7.8] Reduction in yield, critical yield and cumulative cost illustrations
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts reduction in yield (RIY) figures across three growth rate bases (Low, Mid, High): Existing pension RIY of 0.3% at all three bases; Recommended solution RIY of 2.1% (Low), 2.2% (Mid), 2.3% (High). Critical yield figures for the recommended solution are 1.9% (Low), 4.8% (Mid), 7.8% (High). Effect of switching figures are £10,400 or 9.6% decrease (Low), £12,000 or 9.4% decrease (Mid), £15,000 or 10.0% decrease (High). All figures are stated on page 18 of the suitability report.
    File:     Evidence from the pension switch report [P2] and suitability report [P17] shows identical figures: Existing pension RIY 0.3% across all growth bases; Recommended solution RIY 2.1% (Low), 2.2% (Mid), 2.3% (High); Critical yield 1.9% (Low), 4.8% (Mid), 7.8% (High); Effect of switching £10,400 or 9.6% decrease (Low), £12,000 or 9.4% decrease (Mid), £15,000 or 10.0% decrease (High). The pension switch report [P1] shows an alternative comparison with different figures: Existing 0.3% RIY, Recommended 0.6% RIY, Critical yield 0.3%, Effect of switching £2,000 or 1.9% decrease (Low scenario). This appears to be a different recommendation or comparison scenario than the main one.
    Discrepancies:
      - Multiple pension switch scenarios exist in the evidence: [P1] shows one set of cost comparisons (RIY 0.6% for new solution, £2,000 effect of switching) while [P2] and [P17] show a different set (RIY 2.1% for new solution, £10,400 effect of switching). The report asserts only the second set of figures on page 18. The first set appears in the pension switch report but is not the figures the suitability report relies on for its stated recommendation.
    The figures the suitability report asserts on page 18 are directly corroborated by the pension switch report [P2] and repeated verbatim in the suitability report itself [P17], achieving internal consistency across the primary evidence. The RIY, critical yield and effect of switching figures all match exactly at corresponding growth bases. The arithmetic implied by these figures is sound: at the Low growth basis, a £10,400 reduction from £108,000 existing to £97,600 recommended equals 9.6% decrease; at Mid growth, £12,000 reduction from £127,000 to £115,000 equals 9.4%; at High growth, £15,000 reduction from £150,000 to £135,000 equals 10.0%. These calculations are internally consistent. However, the evidence file also contains an alternative pension switch analysis [P1] showing materially different figures (RIY of 0.6% vs 2.1%, effect of switching £2,000 vs £10,400 at the Low growth basis). This alternative comparison relates to a different switching scenario (shown in the 'overall position' sections retaining different pensions). The report has selected one recommendation scenario and does not present alternative comparisons or explain why the alternatives have been rejected. The requirement asks whether the cited RIY and critical yield figures are consistent with evidence provided. The figures are consistent with the evidence presented in [P2] and [P17], but the case file contains a second recommendation analysis with substantially different cost outcomes. The suitability report does not reconcile or explain these different scenarios.

  [G7.9] The service proposition attached to the ongoing adviser charge
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report states an ongoing annual adviser charge of 1.0% per annum. The services included for this charge are: (1) Reviewing your attitude to risk and capacity for loss to ensure your investment portfolio remains suitable for you; (2) Reviewing the ongoing suitability of your investment portfolio; (3) Market updates and updates with any changes in financial legislation; (4) Access to a professional, experienced and qualified team of financial advisers at any time to provide ad-hoc advice as required; (5) Access to full holistic financial planning to suit your needs. The report states review frequency is unspecified. The monetary equivalent for year one is stated as circa £1,101.85. The report asserts these services are the benefits of the ongoing service but does not explicitly state whether the review frequency or service scope matches the client agreement.
    File:     The client agreement [P1] and supporting service documentation [P8] specify an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (subject to a minimum fee of £100). The service includes: (1) Review meeting at least once a year with recommendations which may include bed and ISA, fund switches, drawdown withdrawals, portfolio rebalance, updated fact find and attitude to risk; (2) Statements and Performance Reports twice per year; (3) Cash flow analysis when relevant and beneficial; (4) Family benefit where direct family members can receive adviser fee-free advice; (5) Regular market updates; (6) Quarterly e-magazine; (7) On-line access for portfolio valuations; (8) Access to adviser should you need them in the meantime. The client agreement [P2] confirms 'we will provide you with our Ongoing advice and review service' and fees are 'set out in the suitability report and any subsequent charges confirmation.'
    Discrepancies:
      - The report lists five services; the client agreement and supporting documentation list eight distinct service components, including specific deliverables (statements twice per year, e-magazine quarterly, family benefit arrangement) not mentioned in the report.
      - The report states review frequency is 'Unspecified'; the client agreement and service documentation specify 'at least once a year' and 'Statements and Performance Reports twice per year.'
      - The report does not mention statements, performance reports, e-magazine, family benefit arrangement, or online access to portfolio valuations—all listed in the client agreement as part of the service proposition for the 1% charge.
      - The report's service list is generic ('Market updates'); the client agreement is specific ('Quarterly e-magazine' plus 'Regular market updates').
      - The report does not disclose the minimum fee of £100 per annum mentioned in the client agreement [P1] and [P8].
    The requirement is to establish consistency between the service proposition disclosed in the suitability report and the service proposition set out in the client agreement. Both documents name the same annual charge rate (1%), and the report correctly references the client agreement as the source of the service details. However, the report's summary of services is materially incomplete. The client agreement and the 'Important Information' document [P8] set out a comprehensive service package including frequency (at least annual review, statements twice yearly, quarterly e-magazine), specific deliverables (cash flow analysis when relevant, family benefit arrangement, online access), and the minimum fee floor. The suitability report discloses only five generic service categories without naming the frequency, the frequency of statements, or several material service components. The guard 'A service the client declined is still disclosed' does not apply—no evidence suggests the client opted out of statements or other listed services. The guard 'client agreement and report may name services differently' also does not excuse the omission: the services listed in the client agreement are substantive distinct offerings not named in the report at all. The omissions are not labelling differences but substantive components of the contracted service. The report's assertion of what is included in the ongoing charge is therefore incomplete as against the evidence in the client agreement.

  [G7.10] Benefits relied upon to justify a higher cost
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts six benefits to justify the recommendation: (1) Lower Costs - 'The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension'; (2) Lifestyling - existing funds automatically reduce risk approaching retirement, moving outside agreed risk range; (3) Greater Investment Choice - new scheme offers wider range of investments; (4) Adviser Charging Facilitation - charges deducted directly; (5) Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments - ability to actively monitor and adjust investments; (6) Enhanced Retirement Flexibility - more flexible retirement options. The report also states: 'By switching to this scheme, you will benefit from lower costs, greater investment flexibility, and continued professional oversight.' On cost justification, it states: 'Based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, including our advice fees, the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year up to age 75 in order to meet the same value as your current plan. Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable.' The report claims the Aviva recommendation is cheaper: 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' for Aviva, and 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' for Standard Life.
    File:     Evidence on costs: P10 shows aggregated fund costs for recommended funds total £406.74 (0.37%) comprised of ongoing costs £258.93 (0.24%), transaction costs £147.80 (0.13%). P22 shows recommended investment funds with charges of 0.18% (HSBC) and 0.29% (CT Universal), totalling approximately 0.235% on weighted basis. On existing arrangements, the Pension Switch Charges table referenced in the internal inconsistencies shows Zurich with current charge 0.18% and impact +0.26%, meaning recommended is MORE expensive by 0.26%, not cheaper. For Aviva EA3, existing charge is 0.50% fund, with switch impact -0.06% (saving). For Standard Life EA5, existing charge stated as 0.52%, but Pension Switch table shows current 0.18% with impact +0.26%. Evidence on benefits: P4, P8 (Aviva Key Features) confirm enhanced retirement flexibility with FAD, full/partial UFPLS, annuity options available. P5, P7 show Aviva offers 80+ funds vs existing arrangements offering 29-60 funds, supporting greater investment choice claim. P1 confirms Aviva Pension Portfolio allows adviser charges to be deducted. P6 confirms People's Pension offers investment profile options (balanced, cautious, adventurous). Lifestyling evidence: P7 indicates Aviva 'My Future Focus Consolidation' is invested in lifestyling strategy, while Standard Life 'At Retirement Universal' is not. No evidence found that existing arrangements are definitively moving outside agreed risk range of 5. On critical yield: P13 (Zurich illustration) projects 3.4% growth after inflation; no document evidences that 4.8% is achievable based on past performance of HSBC Global Strategy Balanced or CT Universal MAP funds.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims lower costs and that 'receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure' but the derived figures show: (1) Zurich existing charge 0.18%, recommended impact +0.26% = 0.44% total, meaning higher not lower cost; (2) Standard Life existing 0.52% vs table showing 0.18% creates internal contradiction, but either way the impact of +0.26% indicates higher costs in recommended solution; (3) The report states for Aviva 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' which is internally contradictory to the claim of lower overall cost structure and conflicts with switch table showing -0.06% (a saving, but on the wrong existing base); (4) Critical yield of 4.8% per annum is stated as achievable based on past performance, but no passage provides evidence of past performance data for the recommended funds supporting this rate; (5) Report claims 'lower costs' as primary benefit but arithmetic of existing vs recommended charges contradicts this for at least Zurich and Standard Life arrangements.

      -  The report's primary stated benefit is 'Lower Costs' with the claim that the receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure. However, examination of the evidence reveals contradictions: (1) For Zurich (£103,439.24), the existing arrangement charge was 0.18% per Pension Switch Charges table, with recommended impact +0.26%, implying total recommended charge of 0.44%, higher than existing. (2) For Standard Life (£3,002), similar mathematics apply though the existing charge statement in the arrangements section (0.52%) contradicts the Pension Switch table (0.18%), creating ambiguity, but the impact column consistently shows +0.26%, indicating higher recommended costs. This weakens the exclusive benefit claim. (6) Greater investment choice is evidenced: Aviva offers 80+ funds vs Zurich's 60+, Supporting this claim. (8) Enhanced retirement flexibility is corroborated by P4 and P8 showing Aviva's FAD, UFPLS, and annuity options, but several existing arrangements (People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Standard Life) also offer FAD and UFPLS per P19, so exclusivity is not established. (9) The critical yield claim depends on past performance being achievable but no performance data is cited in any passage for the recommended funds. (10) The arithmetic internal inconsistency between 0.06% and 0.08% claimed savings vs the Pension Switch table showing costs rising for multiple arrangements is material and unreconciled.

    The report's primary stated benefit is 'Lower Costs' with the claim that 'the receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension.' A material cost layer discrepancy persists between stated 'lower costs' and charge table impacts showing cost increases.

  [G7.11] Higher charges producing lower projected outcomes or an increased yield requirement
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts the recommended solution is 0.24% cheaper than the existing arrangements on an annual recurring charges basis. However, when adviser fees are included, the report states the plan would need to outperform the current arrangement by 4.24% annually. The report projects values at age 75 under three growth scenarios: Low (0.0% growth) £97,600; Mid (2.95% growth) £115,000; High (5.88% growth) £135,000. Reduction in Yield figures stated are 0.3%, 2.1%, and 2.3% respectively, with corresponding Critical Yield requirements of 1.9%, 4.8%, and 7.8%.
    File:     The pension switch report [P10] shows projections for a comparison between existing pension and new solution: Low growth (0.00%): existing £108,000, new £97,600 (RIY 2.1%, CY 1.9%); Mid growth (2.95%): existing £127,000, new £115,000 (RIY 2.2%, CY 4.8%); High growth (5.88%): existing £150,000, new £135,000 (RIY 2.3%, CY 7.8%). The switch report [P3] indicates critical yield of 3.2% for mid-growth scenario and 6.2% for high-growth. Report [P1] shows existing pension £110,185.60 and new solution day one £106,880.60, a shortfall of £3,305.00. The switching analysis shows the new solution produces lower projected maturity values at all three growth rates: £10,400 shortfall (9.6%) at low growth, £12,000 shortfall (9.4%) at mid-growth, £15,000 shortfall (10.0%) at high growth [P10]. Report [P3] shows adviser charges of initial £3,305 taken from new solution and 1.00% ongoing annual charge.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states charge difference of -0.24% (cheaper); no passage corroborates this specific percentage figure
      - Report states required outperformance of 4.24% annually including adviser fee; no passage corroborates this figure
      - Critical Yield figures in report (1.9%, 4.8%, 7.8%) are matched in [P10] but report also earlier states alternative critical yields (0.3%, 3.2%, 6.2%) in [P3]
      - Report projects £97,600 (low), £115,000 (mid), £135,000 (high) at age 75; these match [P10] but represent LOWER values than existing pensions (£108,000, £127,000, £150,000 respectively)
      - Report asserts recommendation is cheaper by 0.24% but projections show new solution worth £10,400-£15,000 LESS at retirement across all scenarios
      - RIY values stated as 0.3%, 2.1%, 2.3% in report; [P10] shows 2.1%, 2.2%, 2.3% for corresponding scenarios
      - The report's claim of being 'RecommendedCheaper' contradicts the projected maturity values which are materially lower
    The core tension is between two different cost comparisons. The report states the recommended Aviva plan is 0.24% cheaper based on 'annual recurring charges' alone. However, this narrow comparison fails to account for the material impact of charges on outcomes. When adviser fees are included, the report acknowledges required outperformance of 4.24% annually, but the pension switch report [P10] shows the new solution requires only 1.9%-7.8% critical yield depending on growth scenario, not 4.24% uniformly. More critically, the projected maturity values in [P10] show the new solution consistently produces lower outcomes: at mid-growth (2.95%), the new solution is worth £115,000 vs £127,000 for existing (£12,000 or 9.4% shortfall); at high growth (5.88%), £135,000 vs £150,000 (£15,000 or 10% shortfall). The report's characterization of 'RecommendedCheaper' is contradicted by its own projected values. The initial impact is also negative: switching creates an immediate £3,305 shortfall in fund value [P1, P10]. The reduction in yield at 2.1%-2.3% [P10] is substantially higher than the existing arrangement at 0.3%, meaning charges are eating into returns more aggressively in the new solution. The report states ongoing adviser charge of 1.00% [P3] which has been 'considered as part of the new solution projections' but the discrepancy between the stated 0.24% charge saving and the 4.24% required outperformance suggests the adviser fee impact is not clearly explained or reconciled with the charge comparison. The critical yields required (up to 7.8%) are significantly higher than historical growth rates typically available, indicating substantial headwinds from charges. The report fails to adequately disclose or explain why a 'cheaper' solution produces materially worse projected retirement values across all growth scenarios.

CHK-008 — Disadvantages, Risks and Customer Understanding
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G8.1, G8.2, G8.3, G8.4, G8.5, G8.6, G8.7, G8.8). Report states access requires '10-15 working days' but supporting Terms and Conditions do not quantify this timeframe specifically. Report emphasises 'would be unable to access this pension flexibly' as a disadvantage of the recommended plan, but the basis for this comparison (what flexibility exists in current arrangements versus new plan) is not clearly explained in the evidence. Report states 'no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement' but does not explicitly quantify or illustrate any projected reduction in maturity value, whereas the Pension Switch Report shows specific reductions (e.g. £10 or 0.3% decrease for some scenarios). 28 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G8.1] Material disadvantages, risk factors and limitations of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report states material disadvantages including: (1) Limited withdrawal flexibility if all pensions transferred ('unable to access this pension flexibly') [P15]; (2) Market timing risk during fund realisation and reinvestment ('could be financially disadvantaged insofar as the cost of buying your new fund could be greater') [P15]; (3) Access delay of 10-15 working days for withdrawals [P15]; (4) Cancellation limitation that previous providers are not obliged to take the plan back [P15]; (5) Limited fund range at Scottish Widows leaving '20-30% of your portfolio out of the market' [P15]; (6) No adviser management available on Scottish Widows plan [P15]; (7) No performance guarantee ('no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement') [P15]; (8) Specific performance comparison that recommended Aviva funds 'have not outperformed your current Zurich funds' [P15]; (9) A switch between pension providers 'does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement' [P20].
    File:     The supporting evidence includes: (1) Aviva Terms and Conditions confirming that switching can result in disinvestment periods 'subject to potentially adverse market movements' with Aviva not liable for losses during such periods [P9]; (2) Key Features confirming investment values 'may go down as well as up', client 'could get back less than has been invested', and projections 'are not guaranteed' [P7]; (3) Key Features confirming it 'may not be easy to sell investments such as commercial property' and there may be delays in passing on money [P4]; (4) Key Features confirming that fixed-term products may not be accessible until end of term and may result in 'significantly less than you invested' [P4]; (5) Key Features noting income drawdown risks including that 'investments need to grow to compensate for the income you withdraw' and if that doesn't happen 'the income you take will deplete your pension fund' [P4]; (6) Key Features confirming that tax position uncertainty may affect income payments [P4]; (7) Terms and Conditions confirming 30-day cancellation period but that 'Your plan will continue if you don't cancel within the specified time period' [P5]; (8) Pension Switch Report providing projected maturity values showing the new solution has lower projected value than existing for some arrangements (e.g. effect of switching shown as £10 or 0.3% decrease for Aviva at high growth rate) [P2].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states access requires '10-15 working days' but supporting Terms and Conditions do not quantify this timeframe specifically
      - Report emphasises 'would be unable to access this pension flexibly' as a disadvantage of the recommended plan, but the basis for this comparison (what flexibility exists in current arrangements versus new plan) is not clearly explained in the evidence
      - Report states 'no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement' but does not explicitly quantify or illustrate any projected reduction in maturity value, whereas the Pension Switch Report shows specific reductions (e.g. £10 or 0.3% decrease for some scenarios)
      - Report mentions market timing risk and access delays as generic disadvantages but does not connect these specifically to the client's circumstances or objectives (OBJ3)
      - Report does not explicitly disclose that the Pension Switch Report shows the new solution has a lower projected maturity value than the existing arrangements under certain growth scenarios
    The report identifies and states multiple material disadvantages, risks and limitations that are corroborated by the supporting evidence. Market timing risk is confirmed in both the Aviva Terms [P9] and the report's own narrative. Access delays and withdrawal restrictions are generic risks disclosed in the Key Features [P4] and Terms and Conditions [P5], and the report appropriately flags these as material to this client's objectives. The performance comparison disadvantage (Aviva not outperforming Zurich funds) is stated explicitly [P15] and supported by the performance analysis [P20]. However, the most material disadvantage—a quantified reduction in projected maturity value—appears in the Pension Switch Report [P2] showing effect of switching as £10 or 0.3% decrease for high growth scenarios, but this specific projected reduction is not collected or highlighted prominently in the report's disadvantages section. The report states generally that 'there is no guarantee' but does not translate the Pension Switch Report's illustrated £10 decrease into a client-specific narrative of what this means for retirement outcomes. The report cites the switch 'does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement' [P20] but this is generic boilerplate, not a specific quantification. The cancellation limitation is stated [P15] and supported [P5]. Fund range limitations at Scottish Widows are stated with specificity (20-30% out of market) [P15] but no supporting evidence confirms this figure. Adviser management limitation is stated [P15] but no evidence confirms Scottish Widows does not offer this service. The disadvantages are distributed across sections (pages 6, 8, 16) rather than collected in one prominent place, as noted in the extraction methodology. The vulnerability overlay: no material vulnerability drivers are identified in the evidence that would alter the assessment of disadvantage disclosure.

  [G8.2] Product-specific and investment-specific risks explained
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states two generic product-specific risks about pension switches: (1) 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement and there is no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform those of your existing provider' (page 12); (2) 'There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement for these funds' (page 8). For investment-specific risks, the report discloses: (1) 'Your funds are currently invested in lifestyling funds, which automatically reduce investment risk as you approach retirement. As a result, your portfolio will eventually move outside of your agreed risk range' (page 7, client-specific); (2) Market risk: 'Investment values can rise as well as fall' (page 8, stated three times); (3) Liquidity risk: 'Access to these funds in the future will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes to complete' (page 8, stated three times).
    File:     The evidence provides extensive product literature and fund documentation. KIIDs [P1], [P3] identify material risks including counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, derivatives risk, currency risk, leverage risk, liquidity risk, and emerging markets risk. The HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio KIID [P1] states 'The Fund is classified in category 4 because its price or simulated data has shown medium fluctuations historically.' Fund Factsheet [P5] warns 'The value of investments and any income from them can go down as well as up and investors may not get back the amount originally invested' and details multiple specific risks. Zurich Policy Info [P16], [P17] discloses specific risks including property fund valuation delays (up to 12 months in uncertain conditions), high yield bond fund risk, emerging markets risk, derivatives risk, and OEIC/ICVC liability allocation risk. Key Features [P7], [P14] state general pension portfolio risks: 'The value of your pension pot and the retirement benefits it can provide may go down as well as up. You could get back less than has been invested.' Income drawdown specific risks are documented [P4]: investment income must grow to compensate for withdrawals; high income withdrawal depletes the fund; tax position changes may vary income; means-tested benefits may be affected. SIPP research report [P15] identifies the client as a 'Balanced investor' (risk level agreed after discussion).
    Discrepancies:
      - The report does not disclose specific risks from the HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio KIID [P1], [P3] including counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, derivatives risk, leverage risk, or emerging markets risk, despite these being material risks 'not fully captured by the Risk and Reward Indicator.'
      - The report does not disclose specific risks from Zurich Policy Info [P17] regarding OEIC/ICVC liability allocation risk, which could reduce fund value if another fund within the structure cannot meet liabilities.
      - The report does not mention the up-to-12-month potential delay for property fund transactions documented in Zurich Policy Info [P16], despite pension funds holding property investments.
      - The report does not specifically address income drawdown risks from Key Features [P4] such as the requirement for investments to grow to compensate for income withdrawal, or the impact on means-tested benefits, despite the recommendation involving income drawdown.
      - The report does not disclose specific currency risk, fixed interest securities risk, or credit risk despite fund literature [P5], [P11] identifying these as material risks affecting the recommended investments.
      - The report does not address the risk that high income withdrawal will deplete the pension fund faster [P4], which is material given the client's income drawdown strategy.
    The report discloses generic investment risks (market risk, liquidity risk, 10-15 day access delay) and generic pension switch risks (no guarantee of outperformance). However, the evidence reveals material product-specific and investment-specific risks documented in the fund KIIDs, factsheets, and product literature that are not disclosed in the report. The guard recognises that standard capital-at-risk wording satisfies general warnings but does not substitute for product-specific risk disclosure. The report's generic statements 'Investment values can rise as well as fall' and 'no guarantee of outperformance' do not constitute full disclosure of the material risks identified in the fund documentation. Counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, derivatives risk, leverage risk, emerging markets risk, and OEIC/ICVC structure risk are documented in HSBC KIID [P1], [P3] and not addressed in the report. Currency risk, fixed interest securities risk, and smaller companies risk are documented in fund literature [P5], [P11] but absent from the report. Income drawdown-specific risks, including the need for investment growth to offset withdrawals and impacts on means-tested benefits [P4], are not explained despite the recommendation involving drawdown. The Zurich property fund risk of up-to-12-month transaction delays [P16] is not disclosed. The client's agreed risk level is 'Balanced' [P15], and the report acknowledges lifestyling risk drift away from this agreed range [page 7], but does not explain the specific risks of the recommended funds against this context. The report defers risk disclosure to 'product literature provided' rather than prominently explaining material risks in the report itself. This represents a limitation under the guard: deferring risk disclosure to product literature where benefits are prominent in the report is a disclosure shortfall.

  [G8.3] Tax consequences of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states: (1) a general caveat that 'All statements concerning the tax treatment of products and their benefits are based on our understanding of current tax law and HMRC practice. Levels and basis of tax relief are subject to change' [P3, P7]; (2) client-specific tax consequences including 'You understand the pension freedoms and prefer the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown when you access your pension, allowing you control over how and when benefits are taken' [P3]; (3) death benefit taxation: 'Should you die before age 75, the nominated beneficiaries can receive a lump sum, drawdown an income or purchase a dependents annuity. All these options are paid free of tax. Where you die after the age of 75, the same options are available, but tax is paid at the beneficiary's marginal rate of tax' [P3]; (4) withdrawal taxation: 'Each withdrawal is taxed at their marginal rate of Income Tax' [P3]; (5) small pot taxation: 'Under the small pot rules, a £200 pension fund withdrawal would be taxed with 25% taken as tax-free cash and the remaining 75% taxed as income at your marginal tax rate' [P3]; (6) inheritance tax: 'Pensions are currently outside of your estate for IHT purposes; however from 6 April 2027, most unused defined contribution pensions and death benefits will be included in your taxable estate for IHT' [P3]; (7) deferral benefit: 'If you were to leave this pot to grow until you retire, you could potentially take the full amount within your personal allowance, meaning no tax would be due' [P3]; (8) tax relief limit: 'Tax relief on any personal contributions made is limited to £3,600 per annum or 100% of salary if higher' [P3].
    File:     The evidence corroborates the generic tax treatment stated. [P2] (Key Features) confirms: 'You can normally withdraw up to 25% of your pension fund as a tax-free lump sum. You may have to pay income tax on any income payments and lump sum payments you take. Both are treated as income, and therefore the tax you pay will depend on your personal circumstances' and notes HMRC limits including 'lump sum allowance and the lump sum & death benefit allowance'. [P4] (Key Features) states death benefit rules: 'If you die before you're 75, we'll normally pay any benefits tax free to your nominated beneficiaries... If you die after the age of 75 we can pay the full value of your remaining pension fund to your nominated beneficiaries, at the discretion of the trustees. Any benefits will be taxed at your beneficiaries' marginal rate.' [P6] (Report) explains Flexi-Access Drawdown: 'An individual can take up to 25% of the pension fund being crystallised as a tax-free lump sum... each withdrawal is taxed at their marginal rate of Income Tax' and states 'flexi-access drawdown also provides a tax-efficient way of passing wealth down through the generations as any unused pension fund can usually be passed down to a wide range of beneficiaries free from Inheritance Tax.' [P6] restates the allowance framework: 'the lump sum allowance - £268,275 the lump sum and death benefit allowance - £1,073,100'. [P7] contains the caveat 'All statements concerning the tax treatment of products and their benefits are based on our understanding of current tax law and HMRC practice. Levels and basis of tax relief are subject to change.' The report's stated IHT liability is £0 [P3, P5]. The report states 'Currently, your Inheritance Tax liability will be £0. We will continue to monitor your IHT position at our future reviews' [P3].
    Discrepancies:
      - The report refers to small pot taxation using a £200 example ('Under the small pot rules, a £200 pension fund withdrawal would be taxed with 25% taken as tax-free cash and the remaining 75% taxed as income at your marginal tax rate') [P3, P10], but the Scottish Widows fund value is £211.32 [P5]. The calculation example uses £200 as a round figure for illustration, not the client's actual fund value.
      - The report states the client 'could potentially take the full amount within your personal allowance, meaning no tax would be due' if the small pot 'were to grow until you retire' [P3, P10], but no growth projection is provided to establish whether this scenario is realistic or merely speculative.
      - The report asserts that pensions are 'currently outside of your estate for IHT purposes' but will be included from 6 April 2027 [P3], yet does not reconcile this with the statement that 'Currently, your Inheritance Tax liability will be £0' [P3]. The timing and consequence of the 2027 change are stated as general law but not applied to the client's projected estate position at death.
      - The report does not state the client's marginal tax rate, which is material to understanding the actual tax consequence of small pot withdrawal (the 75% portion taxed as income depends on the rate) and of income drawdown withdrawals. The report references 'your marginal tax rate' [P3, P10] but this rate is not established in the case file or report.
    The report discloses a broad range of tax consequences covering death benefits, withdrawal taxation, small pot rules, IHT, and allowances. These are consistent with the product literature [P2, P4, P6]. The generic legal framework is stated correctly and is corroborated by the evidence. However, material client-specific application gaps exist: (1) The client's marginal tax rate is not recorded in the evidence, yet the tax consequence of both small pot withdrawal and income drawdown depends entirely on this rate. The report uses the phrase 'your marginal tax rate' as a placeholder, not a calculated figure. (2) The small pot example uses £200 when the actual fund is £211.32; this is a minor illustration point and does not affect the principle disclosed, but illustrates generic rather than client-specific framing. (3) The deferral tax benefit claim ('potentially take the full amount within your personal allowance, meaning no tax would be due') is not supported by a projection showing that the pot will grow to a size that fits within the standard personal allowance at the projected drawdown date. The client is age 69 and expects to retire in 2 years; a £211.32 pot growing at standard market rates over that period would remain modest, but no growth rate is cited. This is presented as a generic possibility rather than a realistic client outcome. (4) The IHT position is stated as currently £0 and the 2027 rule change is noted, but the report does not explain the consequence for this client — whether the client's estate, including the pension, is projected to exceed the available allowances or nil-rate bands at the time of death, or how the 2027 change affects the planning. The report notes 'We will continue to monitor your IHT position at our future reviews' but this is a commitment to future action, not a current assessment. The guard (Generic legislative explanation is not the same as stating the consequence for this client) applies: the report explains the law and the generic tax treatment of pensions but does not calculate or illustrate the consequence for this client specifically, except by stating the IHT liability is currently £0. A second guard states: A tax consequence stated without the client's tax band established is unverifiable, not necessarily wrong — this applies to the marginal rate references. The evidence pack does not establish the client's income tax band, so verification is not possible, but the report's silence on the rate is a gap in transparency rather than an error.

  [G8.4] Loss scenarios illustrated
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts one loss scenario: 'if an immediate market crash were to occur in combination with the increased inflation level... if your plan sustained an immediate loss of -30% and the increased inflation continued, there would be no shortfall that requires your pension or investment funds to be used.' The report also states a separate stress test based on 'high inflation rate of 6%' showing 'there would be no shortfall that requires your pension or investment funds to be used.' The report presents these stress tests in the 'Alternative Scenarios & Stress Tests' section on page 15.
    File:     The evidence contains multiple illustrations of investment risks and loss potential across product literature and financial planning documents. [P2] (Key Features) states 'The value of your pension pot and the retirement benefits it can provide may go down as well as up. You could get back less than has been invested.' [P7] confirms 'The value of your investment and retirement benefits depends on: the level of payments made into your Pension Portfolio, the performance of your chosen investments, the charges you pay.' [P10] (Illustration) shows three growth scenarios: Lower 1.9%, Medium 5%, Higher 8%, with corresponding pension values of £97,500, £115,000, and £135,000 at age 75 (after inflation adjustment). [P3] (Suitability Report) presents a detailed comparison table showing Low, Mid, and High growth rates (0.00%, 2.95%, 5.88%) with corresponding projected values and effects of switching, including a 9.6% to 10.0% decrease in value as a result of the switch. [P4] (intelliflo planning Baseline) and related financial planning documents show cashflow projections with surplus and shortfall scenarios. The product literature in [P2] identifies specific loss risks including currency risk, property liquidity risk, fixed-term access restrictions, and income drawdown risks.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report presents only one quantified loss scenario (-30% market crash combined with 6% inflation) whereas the supporting evidence contains multiple forward-looking loss scenarios across different growth rates (0%, 2.95%, 5.88%) in the comparison table [P3], and illustrative scenarios (1.9%, 5%, 8%) in [P10], none of which are explicitly cross-referenced or reconciled with the -30% stress test.
      - The report's stress test conclusion ('no shortfall that requires your pension or investment funds to be used') is not reconciled against the 9.6% to 10.0% reduction in projected value due to switching shown in [P3], creating ambiguity about whether a loss has been illustrated.
      - The report addresses inflation (6% scenario) and market crash (-30%) but does not explicitly illustrate a loss scenario from the investment growth rate falling to 0% or negative real returns, which is contemplated in [P3] (Low growth rate 0.00%) showing £108,000 vs £97,600, a £10,400 or 9.6% decrease.
      - The Key Features document [P2] discloses specific risks (currency, liquidity, fixed-term restrictions, income drawdown depletion risks) that are not referenced or integrated into the suitability report's loss scenario narrative.
    The guard states: 'A stress test in a cashflow model is a loss scenario even if not labelled as one in the risk section.' Under this guard, the stress tests documented in the report (6% inflation; -30% market crash combined with 6% inflation) do satisfy the presence-only requirement for loss scenarios illustrated. The -30% loss scenario is explicitly stated and quantified. However, the discrepancies reveal significant gaps in the integration and prominence of loss illustration. The report presents one stress test scenario but does not reconcile it with or reference the forward-looking loss scenarios already embedded in the suitability report's own comparison table [P3], which shows a 9.6% to 10.0% effect of switching depending on growth rate. The report does not explain how a -30% immediate loss would interact with the switching cost or why the stress test outcome ('no shortfall') is relevant given that substantial reductions in fund value are shown elsewhere in the document. The evidence in [P2] and [P7] identifies material risks (currency, liquidity, drawdown depletion) that are not brought into the loss scenario narrative or illustrated with concrete examples tied to the client's specific investments. The report's loss scenario appears isolated from the broader risk disclosure and does not integrate the product-specific risks disclosed in the Key Features or the adverse scenarios shown in its own growth rate tables. The prominence of the stress test (one section near the end) versus the prominence of projected benefits elsewhere in the document creates a disclosure structure concern, though the scenario itself is stated and quantified.

  [G8.5] Disadvantages and risks are fairly and prominently presented
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report presents risk and disadvantage content scattered across multiple sections (pages 6–8 for switching recommendations, page 12 for fund recommendations, page 16 for general terms). The canonical extraction identifies no dedicated risk section and notes prominence is achieved through distribution rather than consolidation. Key content includes: (1) Loss scenarios presented in projection tables at three growth rates (0.00%, 2.95%, 5.88%), showing Effect of Switching figures of £10,400–£15,000 representing 9.4%–10.0% decreases at mid-to-high growth; (2) Critical Yield requirement of 4.8% per annum stated in narrative [P2] and reiterated in tables [P1]; (3) Reduction in Yield figures (0.3% existing vs 2.1%–2.3% new solution); (4) Past performance caveat: 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement' [P5]; (5) Risk rating increase disclosed with acknowledgment client 'are aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating' and 'funds may go up as well as down' [P7]; (6) Capacity for loss affirmed with statement underperformance 'would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living' [P7]; (7) Tax consequences addressed for small pots encashment but with limited prominence in narrative; (8) Adviser charges of 1% p.a. stated [P8]; (9) Switching costs noted: 'Any fees applied at the point of switching may reduce the capital value of the pension which will reduce any death benefit payable' [P11], with immediate shortfall of £3,305 on day one [P15].
    File:     Supporting evidence from the pension switch report [P9]–[P15] corroborates the projection tables, critical yield methodology, and immediate impact disclosure. Loss scenarios are identical: at mid-growth (2.95%), effect of switching shown as £12,000 or 9.4% decrease [P1] matching [P11]. Reduction in Yield explanation provided consistently [P1], [P11]. Death benefit impact at point of switch quantified identically: £110,185.60 existing vs £106,880.60 new solution day one, resulting in £3,305 shortfall [P11], [P15]. Risk content in [P5] warns 'Past performance is no guarantee of future returns' and 'A switch between pension providers does not guarantee a greater pension fund at retirement.' Product Diamond Ratings disclosed in [P15] for new solution funds (Columbia Threadneedle and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced, both 50% allocation). Charges detailed: Reduction in Yield explicitly defined as 'impact of product, platform and fund charges on the investment growth rate' [P1], [P11]. However, evidence does not contain separate product literature, fund factsheets, or comparative fund charges for existing arrangements. The vulnerability overlay identifies no special communication needs or reasonable adjustments affecting prominence or clarity of disadvantages.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' for Standard Life [P6], yet the derived figure at 0.26% (from the Pension Switch Charges table showing impact +0.26% and current charge 0.18%) contradicts this statement and the internal extraction notes this as an internal inconsistency. The report's own charge figures do not reconcile: Existing Standard Life charge stated as 0.52% platform charge elsewhere, but the switch analysis shows 0.18% current charge, creating ambiguity about which Standard Life plan is being compared.
      - Report states advisor need to 'outperform your current plan by £4,672 in the first year' [P2], but the derived figure implies a fund value of £110,188.68 at 4.24%, which matches neither the £116,998.47 total of all arrangements nor the £110,185 new solution total, creating internal inconsistency in the basis for the critical yield calculation.
      - Loss scenarios presented show effect of switching as £10,400–£15,000 decrease (9.4%–10.0%), but these are framed as disadvantages of the switch yet the recommendation proceeds, raising implicit tension between the stated material disadvantage and the ongoing recommendation without explicit justification for overriding the loss.
      - Report projects at age 75 but does not state client's current age or retirement timeline explicitly in main narrative, though age 75 is used throughout projections. Supporting evidence [P9]–[P15] uses identical retirement age without independent corroboration in file of whether age 75 is appropriate for this client.
    The comparison can be performed on the core requirement: whether material disadvantages, risks and loss scenarios are fairly, clearly and prominently disclosed and consistent with evidence. Both the report and supporting evidence present congruent loss scenarios (9.4%–10.0% decrease), critical yield requirements (4.8%), and immediate switching costs (£3,305 shortfall). The projection tables, reduction-in-yield methodology, and past-performance caveats are internally consistent across [P1] and [P11]. However, several tensions arise that impair the fairness and clarity of the disclosure: (1) Loss quantified prominently but recommendation proceeds without explicit rebuttal — the report states material disadvantages (9–10% loss under mid-growth assumption) but does not explain why these losses are acceptable or how they are outweighed by benefits. The comparison in [P6] claims Standard Life is 'more expensive' (0.08% statement) yet the charge comparison in the Pension Switch table shows a 0.26% impact, contradicting the assertion and undermining confidence in the cost-advantage claim that is offered as the primary benefit of the switch. (2) Disadvantages of lifestyling drift in Standard Life are asserted [P6] but no evidence in the file documents the actual fund allocation, glide path, or timeline to confirm that drift outside the client's risk rating (5) would occur. (3) Tax consequences addressed only briefly via small-pots encashment example for Scottish Widows (£211.32 fund) but not explored for larger transfers or for ongoing withdrawal tax implications at retirement. (4) Prominent benefit claims ('Lower Costs', 'Greater Investment Choice', 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility') in [P6] lack detailed product-specific justification in supporting evidence; these benefits are asserted but not corroborated by product literature. (5) Risk rating increase from 4 to 5 disclosed with acknowledgment of awareness [P7], but the rationale for overriding the client's own questionnaire result is presented as agreement without detailed justification for why the adviser's reassessment was appropriate. (6) Prominence: the main narrative [P6]–[P8] leads with benefits; loss scenarios appear in projection tables (pages not specified in extraction but associated with main recommendation sections); risk content is distributed, not consolidated. This satisfies the guard that distributed content can be prominent if clearly stated, but the asymmetry in narrative framing (benefits first, losses in tables) and the absence of explicit rebuttal or trade-off discussion suggests weakened prominence of the disadvantages. (7) Vulnerability overlay: no actual or potential vulnerability drivers identified in evidence affecting communication needs or reasonable adjustments; CHK-010 would assess vulnerability separately. Under the consistency limb, the core question is whether the report's assertions are contradicted by evidence. The charge discrepancy (0.08% vs 0.26% for Standard Life) is a finding: the report asserts a cost advantage that is contradicted by its own switch-analysis table. The loss scenario itself (9–10% decrease) is not contradicted but is prominently disclosed, so no consistency failure on that point. The missing product-specific evidence (fund factsheets, existing fund charges, lifestyling evidence) prevents full verification but does not itself create a contradiction between what the report says and what the file says; it creates a gap in supporting evidence, which is a disclosure shortfall rather than an inconsistency.

  [G8.6] Benefits, features or protections the client is giving up
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that all five existing pension arrangements contain no safeguarded benefits. The report lists retirement options available for each arrangement: People's Pension and Scottish Widows offer UFPLS and PartialUFPLS only; Aviva and Zurich offer FlexiAccessDrawdown, UFPLS, and PartialUFPLS; Standard Life offers FlexiAccessDrawdown only. The extraction notes that the report contains no explicit statement of other features being lost, features or guarantees in the recommended solution, or comparative analysis of features and protections between existing and new arrangements.
    File:     Policy research documents [P1] and [P2] show that none of the five existing arrangements carry guaranteed annuity rates (GAR), guaranteed minimum pensions (GMP), or guaranteed growth rates. Scottish Widows documentation [P5] confirms no GMP, GMF, reference scheme test, waiver of premium, life cover, reinvestment of charges, fund rebates, or former protected rights. Standard Life [P10, P17] confirms no GAR, GMP, guaranteed fund at retirement, or reference scheme test, and states 'no Safeguarded benefits attached to this member's plan'. Zurich policy info [P3] notes that 'life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans' and that 'You would also need to complete a medical questionnaire to take out similar cover separate to your new pension plan. This will be particularly important if your health has deteriorated since taking out these benefits.' Aviva [P14, P15] shows no guarantees and that 'in-specie transfers (where funds are transferred between pension schemes without first selling the units) aren't possible for this product. We offer cash transfers only.' The pension switch report [P6, P13] shows immediate switch impact of £3,305 reduction in death benefit ('This results in a shortfall in the death benefit immediately after the switch of £3,305.00'). Evidence confirms retirement options available on each arrangement [P8] including FAD availability (some conditional), full and partial UFPLS, and annuity options where applicable.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states all five arrangements have no safeguarded benefits but does not disclose what protections or features exist on the existing arrangements that may be lost or changed on transfer, such as death benefits, specific fund availability, or the loss of ability to maintain in-specie transfers. The report does not explain the £3,305 shortfall in death benefit immediately after the switch ([P13]). The report does not disclose that life cover and waiver benefits, once available, cannot be replicated on the new plan without medical questionnaire and may be unavailable if health has deteriorated ([P3]). The report does not explain the immediate cash transfer requirement and loss of in-specie transfer capability on the new Aviva platform ([P14, P15]). The report does not discuss material differences in retirement options between existing arrangements and the new solution—particularly that People's Pension has no FAD available while some existing arrangements do ([P1, P8]). The report does not address the implications of the recommendations for death benefit provision or successor benefits.
    The report asserts that no safeguarded benefits exist on any of the five existing arrangements. This assertion is consistent with the policy research and provider documentation, which confirm absence of GARs, GMPs, and guaranteed growth rates across all existing plans [P1, P2, P5, P10, P17]. However, the absence of safeguarded benefits does not mean an absence of features or protections being given up. The policy documents disclose material features that either exist on existing arrangements or will be lost or changed on transfer: (1) Death benefits: The pension switch report [P13] explicitly states that switching results in 'a shortfall in the death benefit immediately after the switch of £3,305.00'. The report does not mention this loss or explain its significance. (2) Life cover and waiver benefits: Zurich policy info [P3] states that 'life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans' and warns that 'You would also need to complete a medical questionnaire to take out similar cover separate to your new pension plan. This will be particularly important if your health has deteriorated since taking out these benefits.' The report is silent on this material limitation. (3) Transfer mechanics: Aviva policy [P14, P15] discloses that the existing plan allows in-specie transfers but the new solution does not—'in-specie transfers (where funds are transferred between pension schemes without first selling the units) aren't possible for this product. We offer cash transfers only.' The report does not disclose this material change. (4) Retirement options: Policy research [P8] shows different retirement options available across existing arrangements—some FAD availability is conditional or absent. The report does not explain what options are being foregone or retained in the new solution. The report states retirement options available for each existing arrangement but provides no comparative disclosure of what is lost. The recommendation switches from multiple arrangements with varying features and protections to a consolidated new solution without explaining what material features, death benefit provision, access to life cover, or transfer mechanics are being changed. This is a disclosure shortfall: material information present in the file is not clearly, prominently, or adequately explained in the report. The extraction notes confirm that the report contains no explicit statement in '/replacementAnalysis[]/featuresOrGuaranteesLost[]' or '/existingArrangements[]/otherFeatures[]', meaning the report is silent on these material points rather than extracting them successfully.

  [G8.7] Justification for increased costs or reduced projected outcomes
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts: 'Based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, including our advice fees, the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year up to age 75 in order to meet the same value as your current plan. Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable.' The report also states the comparison direction is 'RecommendedCheaper', implying costs are lower. The report presents disadvantages including market timing risk, no performance guarantee, and that Aviva funds have not outperformed Zurich funds. Three loss scenarios are projected at low (0.0%), mid (2.95%), and high (5.88%) growth rates, with values at age 75 of £97,600, £115,000, and £135,000 respectively.
    File:     The pension switch report [P1] shows a different critical yield requirement: for the mid-growth scenario (2.95%), the new solution requires 3.2% growth to match existing pension value of £127,000, not 4.8%. At mid growth, existing pension projects to £127,000 while new solution projects to £125,000—a £2,000 or 1.6% decrease. The switch report [P1] also documents that at high growth (5.88%), existing projects to £150,000 vs new solution £147,000 (£3,000 decrease). A separate illustration [P4] for Standard Life shows projected maturity values of £102,000 (low), £120,000 (mid), £141,000 (high), which differ materially from the suitability report's figures. The pension switch comparison table [P11] ranks the existing solution second overall (0.3% reduction in yield, £127,000 at mid growth) and the new solution fifth (0.6% reduction in yield, £125,000 at mid growth).
    Discrepancies:
      - Critical yield stated in report as 4.8% vs pension switch report [P1] showing 3.2% for mid-growth scenario
      - Report projects £115,000 at mid-growth (2.95%) vs pension switch report [P1] showing £125,000 for new solution at same growth rate
      - Projection direction: report states projections of £97,600 (low), £115,000 (mid), £135,000 (high) vs [P1] showing £100,000, £125,000, £147,000 for new solution at corresponding growth rates
      - Report claims 'RecommendedCheaper' but [P1] shows new solution with 0.6% reduction in yield vs existing 0.3%, indicating higher ongoing charges impact
      - Existing pension projected at £115,000 mid-growth in report but [P1] shows £127,000 for same scenario
      - Comparison ranking [P11] places new solution 5th (0.6% RIY) below existing solution ranked 2nd (0.3% RIY), contradicting 'RecommendedCheaper' assertion
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: The report's canonical model shows projected values of £97,600, £115,000, and £135,000 but the pension switch report [P1] shows different maturity values (£100,000, £125,000, £147,000). The reconciliation between these two sets of projections cannot be established from the evidence provided. The basis for the 4.8% critical yield figure in the report is not found in any supporting document, and the evidence shows 3.2% instead. Without clarity on which projection basis is correct, the comparison cannot reliably be performed.
    The requirement tests whether the report justifies increased costs or reduced projected outcomes. The guard states that where costs fall and projections improve, absence of justification is not a finding. However, the evidence presents two separate problems: (1) Direction inconsistency: the report asserts 'RecommendedCheaper' but [P1] shows the new solution has a higher reduction in yield (0.6% vs 0.3%), meaning higher charge impact. (2) Projection inconsistency: the report's stated critical yield of 4.8% cannot be located in any supporting evidence; [P1] states 3.2% for the mid-growth scenario. The report's projection figures (£97,600, £115,000, £135,000) do not align with [P1] (£100,000, £125,000, £147,000). The suitability report justifies the recommendation by stating past performance of recommended funds suggests the 4.8% growth is achievable, but this justification rests on a critical yield figure that is not corroborated by the pension switch analysis and contradicts the evidence that shows existing projections are actually higher. The disadvantages disclosed (market timing risk, no performance guarantee, underperformance vs Zurich funds) are generic rather than connected to the specific cost-benefit trade-off. The report does not explain why accepting lower projected outcomes (£125,000 vs £127,000 at mid-growth per [P1], or the reduced yield impact differential) is justified given the client's circumstances. The internal consistency check within the report itself fails: it cannot simultaneously assert 'RecommendedCheaper', require 4.8% outperformance, and be supported by evidence showing the recommendation requires higher ongoing charges and delivers lower maturity values.

  [G8.8] Material risks or limitations identified in the evidence but not disclosed in the report
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report discloses material disadvantages including: Limited withdrawal flexibility (stated as client-specific); market timing risk during pending transactions; access delays of 10-15 working days; cancellation limitations where previous providers need not take plans back. Product-specific risks stated include: no guarantee of greater pension fund or outperformance versus existing arrangement. Tax consequences mentioned for small pots withdrawal (25% tax-free, 75% taxed at marginal rate, estimated net receipt £170 from £211.32 fund). One loss scenario illustrated: switching effect shows £3,000 or 2.1% decrease in projected value at mid-growth rate (from £141,000 to £138,000 at age 75). Investment risks noted generically: value and income can go down as well as up. A limitation disclosed: Scottish Widows does not offer single multi-asset fund aligned to risk rating 5, requiring two funds with 20-30% in cash. Another stated: recommended plan does not allow adviser management, which client wished to benefit from.
    File:     The pension switch research report [P1][P2][P3] shows Reduction in Yield (RiY) impact on all three main existing pensions: Aviva RiY 0.6% existing vs 0.6% new; Zurich RiY 0.3% existing vs 0.6% new (doubling); Standard Life RiY 0.3% existing vs 0.6% new (doubling). Zurich alternative comparison [P10] shows critical yield 1.9% at low growth, 4.8% at mid growth, 7.8% at high growth—substantially higher than existing 0.3%. Effect of switching shows material losses: at low growth £10,400 or 9.6% decrease; at mid-growth £12,000 or 9.4% decrease; at high growth £15,000 or 10.0% decrease for Zurich retention scenario. Product literature [P8][P15][P20][P11] discloses: life cover and waiver benefits no longer available on new plans and require separate medical questionnaire if desired [P8]; existing Standard Life plan has differential fund discount from TER with lower charges that may not be available on transfer [P15]; Zurich policy info does not list guaranteed annuity rates or guaranteed minimum pension [P24]; Key Features state income drawdown could deplete fund if withdrawals exceed growth [P11]; property investment liquidity issues noted [P11]. Research report [P12] describes stochastic modelling by Hymans Robertson and discusses capacity for loss. KIID material risks [P18] include counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, derivatives risk, currency risk affecting asset allocation 45-70% equities, 20-55% bonds, 0-25% other. Zurich policy [P24] notes ongoing portfolio review importance as circumstances change. Aviva policy [P16] confirms plan does not allow adviser charging. No benefits listed in existing products per pension switch report [P7].
    Discrepancies:
      - Reduction in Yield doubled on Zurich and Standard Life (0.3% to 0.6%) is disclosed in technical research [P3][P10] but not highlighted as material disadvantage in report—report states 'no guarantee of outperformance' generically without quantifying RiY increase impact
      - Effect of switching shows substantial losses in alternative comparison [P10]: Zurich alone shows 9.6% to 10.0% decrease depending on growth rate, but main comparison table [P1] shows only £3,000 or 2.1% decrease—the discrepancy relates to whether Zurich is retained or switched, but loss scenarios are not clearly separated by arrangement
      - Standard Life has differential fund discount from TER with lower charges [P15] that 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer'—this material limitation of transferring out is not disclosed in the report's stated disadvantages
      - Life cover and waiver benefits no longer available on new Zurich pension and require medical questionnaire [P8]—this is mentioned in product literature as feature loss but not stated in report's material disadvantages or benefits foregone section
      - Critical yield for Zurich new solution ranges 1.9% to 7.8% [P10] depending on growth scenario—these are substantially elevated compared to existing 0.3%, but report does not break down critical yield by scenario as loss indicator
      - Income drawdown risk disclosed in Key Features [P11] that withdrawals may deplete fund if they exceed growth, and means-tested benefit impact—these specific drawdown risks not stated in report's risk section despite recommendation involving drawdown
    The report discloses some material disadvantages and risks but comparison with the evidence reveals significant gaps and inconsistencies. The report states generic risks (market timing, access delay, performance uncertainty, no adviser management) and one specific loss scenario (£3,000 or 2.1% decrease), but the supporting evidence contains more material disadvantages that are either understated or omitted. The Zurich alternative comparison shows effect of switching ranging from 9.6% to 10.0% loss depending on growth rate [P10], far exceeding the 2.1% shown in the main comparison [P1]. The report does not explain or reconcile these divergent scenarios or clearly present them as loss illustrations. The Reduction in Yield doubling on Zurich and Standard Life (from 0.3% to 0.6%) is a material disadvantage visible in the research data but not explained to the client as a disadvantage in the main report—it appears only in technical tables. The loss of differential fund discount on Standard Life [P15] is material to the 'cost comparison' mandate but absent from stated disadvantages. Life cover and waiver benefits no longer available [P8] should be listed under 'benefits foregone' but is not. The income drawdown risks detailed in Key Features [P11]—particularly that withdrawals may deplete the fund and affect means-tested benefits—are not translated into the report's risk section despite drawdown being part of the stated retirement strategy. The report does reference 'no guarantee of outperformance' and generic investment risks but does not quantify material loss scenarios across the three growth rates or explain the critical yield thresholds that make the recommendation dependent on 4.8% to 7.8% growth (depending on arrangement). The critical yield concept is not explained to the client at all. The report discloses tax consequences for the small pots encashment but does not address income tax implications of drawdown withdrawals or the interaction with means-tested benefits. Material information from the research and product literature is present in the file but not prominently disclosed in the suitability report itself; loss scenarios are scattered across alternative comparisons rather than integrated into the main recommendation. The guards note that retrieval order does not indicate page position and that risks in separate documents are disclosed if given to the client, but the question is whether the report 'fairly, clearly, accurately and prominently' explains all material disadvantages—and it does not prominently surface the magnitude of switching losses, the RiY penalty, or the drawdown depletion risk.
