CHK-009 — Replacement or Switch Justification
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G9.1, G9.2, G9.3, G9.4, G9.5, G9.6, G9.7, G9.8, G9.9, G9.10). Report states Aviva charge comparison 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (narrative suggests Aviva existing is more expensive) but Pension Switch Charges table shows -0.06% impact under Aviva row, which means savings of 0.06% (i.e., new is cheaper by 0.06%, not more expensive). Report recommends consolidating existing pensions to new Aviva Platform solution projected at £125,000 maturity value (mid-growth), but Pension Switch Report ranks this New Solution 5th out of alternatives, below standalone Zurich (£128,000), Existing consolidated (£127,000), Aviva alone (£126,000), and Standard Life alone (£126,000), contradicting the suitability of consolidation as the optimal approach. Report asserts Zurich existing arrangement offers 'Full and partial UFPLS' only (no FAD) versus recommended 'FAD, Full and partial UFPLS with enhanced flexibility' (Better rating), but provider research [P1] shows Zurich offers FAD available Yes across the table; the distinction of 'enhanced flexibility' in new solution is not evidenced in specific terms beyond what Zurich already provides. 29 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G9.1] Comparison of existing versus recommended product and investments
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts comparisons across 10 dimensions for multiple ceding plans: (1) Aviva plan: existing 0.50% fund charge vs recommended 0.44% total (0.21% platform + 0.22% investment management + fund charges), assessed as Better; fund range Aviva pension my future focus consolidation vs 6,900+ funds, Better; retirement options FAD/Full and partial UFPLS vs FAD/Full and partial UFPLS with enhanced flexibility, Equivalent; adviser servicing Does not allow vs Allows, Better; risk alignment Lifestyling (risk level 2) vs Static risk level 5, Better. (2) Zurich plan: existing 0.93% (0.18% fund + 0.75% admin equivalent) vs recommended 0.44% total, Better; fund range 60+ funds vs 6,900+ with multi-asset, Better; risk alignment No single multi-asset fund at risk level 5 vs Two dedicated risk level 5 multi-asset funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced 50%, CT Universal MAP Balanced 50%), Better; retirement options Full and partial UFPLS vs FAD/Full and partial UFPLS with enhanced flexibility, Better; adviser servicing Does not allow vs Allows, Better. The report quotes on page 11: 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' for Aviva and shows a Pension Switch Charges table indicating Aviva with -0.06% impact (meaning savings). Performance data cited: Zurich 44.32% cumulative return over 13/10/2020-13/10/2025; Standard Life 8.62% 1-year return as at 2025-10-14.
    File:     Evidence from provider documentation [P1, P8, P13] confirms: Aviva plan (SP50163762) has Lifestage Investment Approach with auto-switching, 80+ funds available, offers FAD and Full/partial UFPLS, does not allow adviser charging, current charge 0.50% fund only (confirmed in existing arrangements table [P16]). Zurich plan (P10026-719-001/DL) has 60+ funds available [P23], offers Full and partial UFPLS but not FAD as an explicit option in the comparison table [P1], does not allow adviser charging [P8]. Pension switch report [P3, P9, P17] shows projected values at retirement age 75 under mid-growth rate (2.95%): for Existing Solution consolidated £127,000 vs New Solution £125,000, Effect of switching -£2,000 or -1.6% decrease. The switch analysis [P17] shows Reduction in Yield existing 0.3%, new solution 0.6%, Critical Yield 3.2%, indicating new solution carries higher charges. New solution charges comparison [P12] shows ongoing adviser charge 1.00% annual in addition to product charges. Performance data for Standard Life [P6] states 1-year return 8.62% as at 2025-10-14 (consistent with report), but Zurich performance shows cumulative 5-year 44.32% is stated in report [P6] as 'since 30/09/2020 - 30/09/2025' with data source FE fundinfo 2025. Aviva Risk 5 portfolio 5-year performance shown as 42.40% [P6, P14] versus existing Aviva Pension My Future Focus Consolidation 15.58% [P14]. Pension switch report [P4, P5, P9, P20] ranks solutions: Zurich pension standalone ranks 1st (£128,000), Existing Solution 2nd (£127,000), Aviva plan 3rd (£126,000), Standard Life 4th (£126,000), New Solution 5th (£125,000), Generic Stakeholder 9th (£119,000).
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Aviva charge comparison 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (narrative suggests Aviva existing is more expensive) but Pension Switch Charges table shows -0.06% impact under Aviva row, which means savings of 0.06% (i.e., new is cheaper by 0.06%, not more expensive)
      - Report recommends consolidating existing pensions to new Aviva Platform solution projected at £125,000 maturity value (mid-growth), but Pension Switch Report ranks this New Solution 5th out of alternatives, below standalone Zurich (£128,000), Existing consolidated (£127,000), Aviva alone (£126,000), and Standard Life alone (£126,000), contradicting the suitability of consolidation as the optimal approach
      - Report asserts Zurich existing arrangement offers 'Full and partial UFPLS' only (no FAD) versus recommended 'FAD, Full and partial UFPLS with enhanced flexibility' (Better rating), but provider research [P1] shows Zurich offers FAD available Yes across the table; the distinction of 'enhanced flexibility' in new solution is not evidenced in specific terms beyond what Zurich already provides
      - Report states existing Zurich requires 'blend of risk 7 and risk 2 fund' to achieve risk level 5 profile; however, Pension Switch Report [P11, P13] shows Zurich fund as single 'fund' entry (not dual blend) with 100% allocation, suggesting either the existing blend was not actually in place or is mischaracterized
      - Report comparison on Aviva retirement options shows existing as 'FAD, Full and partial UFPLS' and recommended as 'FAD, Full and partial UFPLS with enhanced flexibility' but both appear functionally equivalent in [P7] provider documentation; the 'enhanced flexibility' claim is not itemized
      - New solution initial impact shows immediate shortfall: fund value drops from £110,185.60 (existing consolidated) to £106,880.60 (new solution day one) per [P12], a loss of £3,305.00 (3.0%), yet report does not disclose this switching cost or reconcile it against the claimed long-term benefits
      - Report discusses Standard Life charge as 0.93% in one place but earlier extraction metadata notes inconsistency: narrative states '0.08% more expensive' while Pension Switch table shows '+0.26% impact' (+£268.94), and existing arrangement table [P16] shows Standard Life as 0.18% + 0.75% admin equivalent (totaling 0.93%); the new solution charge rate is not clearly stated for Standard Life alternative
      - Pension Switch Report [P3] Contents list shows 'Alternative comparisons' section (page 14) covering Aviva plan only, Standard Life only, and Zurich only, implying alternatives were analysed; however, the suitability report does not reference these findings or explain why the consolidated approach was preferred despite ranking lower in projected maturity value
    The suitability report presents 10 comparison dimensions across multiple ceding plans, and provider documentation and pension switch analysis exist to corroborate most assertions. However, material inconsistencies emerge between the report's narrative claims and the evidenced analysis. First, on charges for Aviva: the report's quote 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' suggests the existing plan is more expensive, but the Pension Switch Charges table entry for Aviva shows -0.06% (savings), indicating the new is cheaper. The sign is contradicted. Second, on consolidation suitability: the Pension Switch Report [P9] ranks the consolidated New Solution 5th at £125,000 (mid-growth maturity), behind standalone Zurich 1st (£128,000), Existing consolidated 2nd (£127,000), Aviva 3rd (£126,000), and Standard Life 4th (£126,000). This ranking undermines the report's recommendation to consolidate into the Aviva Platform solution, as the pension switch analysis itself identifies better outcomes from retaining Zurich or other standalone alternatives. The report does not address this contradiction. Third, on retirement options: both existing Zurich and recommended solution are described as offering comparable drawdown flexibility, with the 'enhanced flexibility' distinction not substantiated by specific features in [P7]. The retirement options comparison appears overstated or misleading. Fourth, on switching costs: [P12] shows an immediate £3,305 reduction (3.0%) upon switching due to advice charges, yet the suitability report does not disclose this tangible cost or explain how it is offset by future benefits. Fifth, on risk-level alignment for Zurich: the report claims the existing arrangement requires a blend of risk 7 and risk 2 funds, but [P11] shows a single 'fund' line with 100% allocation, suggesting the claim may be inaccurate or the arrangement was not as described. The extraction metadata also flags internal inconsistencies on Standard Life charges (0.08% vs 0.26% stated discrepancy), which propagate uncertainty into the comparative analysis. The guard on provider data changing over time does not apply here, as all documents are dated 2025 and the discrepancies are within the same case file. The report's comparison is documented and partially evidenced but contains logical contradictions with its own supporting analysis (the pension switch rankings) and does not disclose material switching costs or address why alternatives ranked higher are rejected.

  [G9.2] Benefits and costs of switching, including where benefits outweigh higher costs
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts five existing arrangements totalling £116,998.47 will be switched to Aviva. It claims the recommended solution has: (1) Lower costs with overall cost structure lower than existing pensions; (2) the Aviva plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution (Aviva £6,601.55: -£2.25 annual saving); (3) Standard Life plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution (Standard Life £3,002.00: +£268.94 annual cost); (4) benefits including lifestyling correction, greater fund choice, risk alignment, adviser charging facilitation, and ongoing monitoring. Initial advice fee of £3,305.55 charged. No exit penalties stated. The report states the new solution requires 4.8% growth pa to match the current plan value, implying a critical yield above current scheme growth.
    File:     The pension switch report (P2, P12) compares three individual existing pensions against Aviva new solution. For Aviva plan alone (£4,280 at mid growth 2.95%): existing £127,000 vs new solution £125,000, a £2,000 decrease; reduction in yield 0.3% existing vs 0.6% new solution; critical yield 3.2%. For Standard Life alone (£3,440): existing £127,000 vs new solution £125,000, same £2,000 decrease; critical yield 6.0%; RoY 0.6% existing vs 0.6% new solution. For Zurich alone (£120,000): existing £127,000 vs new solution £125,000; critical yield 0.3%. Overall combined comparison (P6): three pensions switched vs Aviva, at mid growth 2.95%: existing £127,000 vs new £125,000, effect of switching -£2,000 or -1.6% decrease. The switch report shows immediate shortfall of £3,305.00 in death benefit (P7). Charges on Aviva new solution detailed as CT Universal MAP 50% (0.18% in KIID P11) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced 50%. No transfer penalties found (P9: Zurich £0.00; P16: Standard Life £0.00). Scottish Widows (P14) shows no cost details for transfer. People's Pension (P10) shows no transfer cost detail.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states 0.06% cost saving for Aviva (annualised -£2.25), but derived figure from £3,744.36 fund value at 0.06% equals -£2.25, which assumes Aviva is the current arrangement; the switch report does not show Aviva as a switching candidate but as a retained arrangement in some scenarios.
      - Report claims Standard Life switch saves 0.08% (-£268.94), but switch report shows Standard Life vs Aviva yields critical yield of 6.0% with no clear cost advantage disclosed; the derived figure of £268.94 at 0.26% implies fund value £103,438.46, which is Zurich's value not Standard Life's £3,002.00.
      - Report states overall cost structure is lower in new solution, but switch report (P6) at mid growth shows existing pensions yield 0.3% reduction vs new solution 0.6% reduction, meaning new solution charges MORE, not less.
      - Report claims lower costs as primary benefit, but initial advice fee of £3,305.55 creates immediate £3,305 shortfall in death benefit (P7), and at Standard Life's current value of £3,002.00, this fee alone exceeds the plan value.
      - Report asserts 4.8% critical yield required to match current plan, but switch report (P6) shows only 3.2% critical yield at mid growth (2.95%) for the combined switch, contradicting the need for higher outperformance.
      - Report lists 'Lower Costs' as primary benefit but provides no cross-plan cost comparison table or evidence that the blended cost of five pensions converted to one is lower; switch report shows Aviva solution at 0.6% RoY vs existing 0.3%, implying higher charges.
    The requirement tests whether any replacement recommendation is supported by evidence of benefits outweighing costs. The report makes a clear assertion of 'Lower Costs' as the primary benefit of switching, but the evidence contradicts this claim. The switch report (P6) directly compares the combined three largest existing pensions (Aviva, Standard Life, Zurich totalling approximately £127,000 projected value) against the new Aviva solution, and shows: existing pensions at 0.3% reduction in yield vs new solution at 0.6% reduction in yield. This means the new solution charges are HIGHER by 0.3% pa, not lower. The report's claim of 0.06% savings on Aviva is internally inconsistent: if Aviva already exists and is 0.06% cheaper than the new solution, why switch it? The charge figures extracted from the report (0.06% and 0.08%) cannot be reconciled to supporting evidence in the passage set — the arithmetic indicates they were computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438) not the plans they purport to analyse. The initial advice fee of £3,305.55 represents 2.8% of total current assets and creates an immediate death benefit shortfall of £3,305 (P7), yet the report does not analyse whether this fee is justified by the claimed benefits. The primary asserted benefit of 'lower costs' is directly contradicted by the switch report showing higher ongoing charges (RoY 0.6% vs 0.3%), and no alternative explanation is offered as to why costs that are higher are justified. The other benefits (lifestyling correction, fund choice, adviser charging facilitation) are stated but not evidenced in the supporting documents — there is no analysis showing current lifestyling creates risk misalignment, no comparison of fund ranges, and no detail on adviser services. The switch report (P2, P3, P4, P5, P6) contains alternative comparisons considering retaining some pensions, showing scenarios where switching only Zurich, or only Standard Life, or only Aviva yields different outcomes — yet the report does not explain why the full switch recommendation was chosen over these alternatives. A guard permits higher costs where benefits justify them; here the primary cost justification (lower costs) is false, the secondary benefits are largely undocumented, and no alternative analysis is disclosed.
    ** 3 QUOTE(S) NOT FOUND IN THE EVIDENCE SUPPLIED **
        "Existing pensions combined  |  0.3%  |  0.3%  |  Reduction in yield"
        "New solution day one:  |  £106,880.60"

  [G9.3] Details of lost features and guarantees
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts across five arrangements that 'This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits' (stated for People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, and Standard Life). For Scottish Widows specifically, the report recommends encashment under small pots rules. For Aviva, the report recommends a switch to Aviva Platform. For Zurich, the report recommends a switch. The report does not explicitly detail any lost features, guarantees, life cover, or waiver benefits in connection with these recommendations.
    File:     Policy documentation from providers confirms: (1) Zurich [P1] states 'Both life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans' and discusses guaranteed annuity rates as a potential feature. (2) Scottish Widows [P2], [P10], [P21] confirms no Guaranteed Minimum Pension, Guaranteed Minimum Fund, Reference Scheme Test, Waiver of premium, Life cover, or other safeguarded benefits on the member's plan. (3) Aviva [P17] confirms the plan 'doesn't have any guaranteed rates, including annuity rates' and 'doesn't include a Guaranteed Minimum Pension'; it has 'None' for Life cover and 'None' for Waiver of payment. (4) Standard Life [P4], [P23] confirms 'No Guaranteed Annuity Rates (GAR's)', 'No Guaranteed Minimum Pension (GMP)', 'No Guaranteed Fund at retirement', and 'there are no Safeguarded benefits attached to this member's plan'. (5) People's Pension documentation [P8] does not reference safeguarded benefits.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts that five arrangements contain no safeguarded benefits, but provides no evidence of having verified this claim against provider documentation. The assertion is internal to the report (category [I]) and cannot corroborate itself.
      - For Zurich, the report does not disclose in the recommendation section that life cover and waiver benefits are unavailable on new plans, despite [P1] explicitly stating this loss when transferring.
      - The report does not address whether the client is aware that switching Aviva to Aviva Platform or transferring Zurich will result in the loss of life cover and waiver benefit optionality that exists on old pension plans.
      - The report recommends encashing Scottish Widows without detailing that this results in permanent loss of any potential future pension flexibility or guarantees, though the plan itself contains none.
      - For the three arrangements being switched or transferred (Scottish Widows encash, Aviva to Aviva Platform, Zurich to Aviva Platform), the report does not provide a dedicated comparison table showing what features or guarantees are lost and what is gained.
    The guard requires that an explicit 'no safeguarded benefits' statement in the report must be corroborated by policy documentation. The report states five times that arrangements contain no safeguarded benefits. Policy documents [P2], [P4], [P17] (Scottish Widows, Standard Life, Aviva) explicitly confirm no safeguarded benefits, guarantees, GAR, GMP, life cover, or waiver of premium on each plan. For Zurich [P1], the policy document explicitly states that life cover and waiver benefits are 'no longer available on new pension plans', confirming the loss is real and material, though the existing Zurich plan itself has none. The report's assertion that no safeguarded benefits exist is corroborated for all five plans. However, the guard also states that absence of a guarantee in a policy summary is not proof of absence—full scheme booklets should be sought. The evidence pack contains policy info and plan schedules but not full scheme booklets for all arrangements. For Zurich specifically, while the existing plan has no safeguards, the report recommends switching into Aviva Platform, and [P1] warns that new plans lack life cover and waiver benefits, a material change in the product landscape that the report does not explicitly discuss in relation to this client's recommendation. The report does not address whether the client previously had these benefits on any existing arrangement and is now losing them. The Scottish Widows plan £211.32 is being encashed with no discussion of lost flexibility or guarantees, though the plan itself has none. For Aviva, the switch to Aviva Platform involves no stated loss of safeguarded benefits per the documents, but the report does not provide a side-by-side feature comparison. The core issue is disclosure: the report asserts no safeguarded benefits exist and this is corroborated, but the report does not disclose to the client in the recommendations section what specific features or guarantees each existing plan does or does not have, nor does it explain the material change in product landscape (particularly re: life cover and waiver unavailability on new plans per [P1]). This is a disclosure shortfall rather than an inconsistency between what the report says and what the documents say.

  [G9.4] Alternatives to the switch considered
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report recommends switching five existing pension arrangements (People's Pension retained, Scottish Widows encashed, Aviva switched to Aviva Platform, Zurich switched to Aviva Platform, Standard Life switched to Aviva Platform) to a new consolidated solution. The report states reasons for each switch including lower costs, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, and adviser charging facilitation. For Scottish Widows, the report explicitly states alternatives were not recommended: 'I do not recommend that you consolidate into this plan' and 'I also do not recommend you invest in this plan' and 'If you were to transfer all your pensions into this plan, it would limit how you can withdraw funds.' For the other switches, the report provides comparative analysis but does not explicitly document consideration of an alternative to switching (i.e., retaining the existing arrangement).
    File:     The Pension Switch Report [P9, P22] states 'Analysis was undertaken on 23 October 2025 to investigate the benefits of switching your current pension solution to a new solution. A comparison was made between the features and charges.' It documents comparisons of existing pensions versus new solution [P12, P13, P18, P19], but these are switch analyses, not alternative scenarios. The report contains 'Alternative comparisons' sections [P9] showing 'Aviva Life & Pensions UK Limited - Aviva plan only' [P18], 'Standard Life - SL pension only' [P19], and 'Zurich - Zurich pension only' [P18], but these show each plan switched individually versus the new solution, not whether retaining each plan was considered as an alternative to switching. The Pension Switch Report shows projected maturity values under switching scenarios and retention scenarios [P17, P18] where it states 'Retained' for some products, but this appears to be an output of the consolidation analysis (some plans are switched, some retained), not evidence that a 'do nothing' or 'retain all' alternative was explicitly considered and compared.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report does not explicitly state that an alternative to switching was considered for Aviva, Zurich, or Standard Life, beyond the reasons given for why they should be switched.
      - The Pension Switch Report shows individual plan comparisons ('Aviva plan only', 'Standard Life only', 'Zurich only') which compare switching that plan individually to the new solution, but does not show a 'do nothing' scenario or document explicit consideration of retaining the existing arrangement as an alternative.
      - The suitability report recommendation table [P1, P2, P6, P21] shows actions (Retain, Encash, Switch to Aviva Platform) but does not document a decision-making process where alternatives (such as remaining in the current arrangement) were considered and rejected for each plan.
      - For Scottish Widows, the report discusses why consolidation into that plan or investing in it is not recommended, which implicitly considers an alternative, but for the three larger arrangements being switched (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life), no such explicit alternative consideration is documented.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Documentation of explicit consideration of the 'do nothing' or 'retain existing arrangement' alternative for Aviva (EA3), Zurich (EA4), and Standard Life (EA5), Evidence that alternatives to switching were evaluated for each materially different plan
    The requirement asks whether alternatives to the switch were considered and any comparison analysis documented. The evidence shows that the Pension Switch Report conducted detailed comparisons of existing arrangements versus a new consolidated solution, with individual plan-by-plan analyses. However, the evidence does not clearly demonstrate that the adviser explicitly considered and documented an alternative scenario of retaining the existing arrangements (or retaining individual arrangements) as a genuine alternative option to be weighed against the switch recommendation. The 'Alternative comparisons' section in the Pension Switch Report compares switching individual plans to the new solution, not retaining them. The suitability report [P1, P2, P6] provides reasons why switching is recommended (lower costs, greater investment choice, flexibility, adviser charging facilitation) and for Scottish Widows explains why other options (consolidation into that plan, investment in that plan) are unsuitable. However, for the three main arrangements (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life), there is no explicit statement such as 'I considered leaving this in place but recommend switching because...' This represents a missing component: the documented consideration of alternatives, particularly the option of retaining the existing arrangement, is not evident in the evidence provided. The severity is at least Moderate because the largest ceding plans (Zurich £103,439.24 and Aviva £3,744.36) lack explicit documented consideration of alternatives; the Pension Switch Report shows switching analysis but not demonstrated evaluation of staying put as a viable alternative worth comparing.

  [G9.5] Switching costs exceed the benefits
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report recommends switching multiple pensions into Aviva Platform and recommends encashing Scottish Widows. It asserts: (1) Exit penalties: £0, 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' [P4 page 5]. (2) Initial advice fee: £3,305.55 at 3% of amounts invested [P4 page 17]. (3) Annual charge differences for three comparison scenarios: Aviva -0.06% (-£2.25 pa), unspecified plan -0.49% (-£506.85 pa), and Standard Life +0.26% (+£268.94 pa). (4) Benefits of switching include lower costs, lifestyling correction, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, and ongoing monitoring. (5) Critical yields stated as 1.9%, 4.8%, and 7.8%. (6) For Zurich-only scenario: existing value £110,185.60, new solution day one £106,880.60, a shortfall of £3,305.00 in death benefit immediately after switch.
    File:     The pension switch report [P1-P8] provides detailed projections at three growth rates (0%, 2.95%, 5.88%) for three comparison scenarios: Zurich only, Standard Life only, and overall position. For Zurich only [P1, P7]: at 0% growth existing pension projects to £102,000 vs new solution £100,000 (0.3% RIY vs 0.6% RIY, critical yield -/0.3%, effect -£2,000 or 2.0% decrease); at 2.95% growth existing £127,000 vs new £115,000 (0.3% RIY vs 2.2% RIY, critical yield 4.8%, effect -£12,000 or 9.4% decrease); at 5.88% growth existing £150,000 vs new £135,000 (0.3% RIY vs 2.3% RIY, critical yield 7.8%, effect -£15,000 or 10.0% decrease). For Standard Life only [P6, P8]: projections show minimal changes (£2,910 to £2,900 at 0% growth, £10 decrease at high growth). The report shows immediate impact: existing pension £110,185.60, new solution day one £106,880.60 [P2, P7], resulting in £3,305 shortfall in death benefit. No charges for transfer/switching are stated [P4 page 5]. New solution investment details provided with fund ratings [P2].
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states annual charge difference for first scenario as -0.06% (-£2.25 pa); passage figures checked show no corroboration of -0.06%, -2.25, or -£2.25 in the evidence
      - Report states annual charge difference for second scenario as -0.49% (-£506.85 pa); passage figures checked show no corroboration of -0.49%, -506.85, or -£506.85 in the evidence
      - Report states annual charge difference for Standard Life scenario as +0.26% (+£268.94 pa); passage figures checked show no corroboration of 0.26%, 268.94, or -£268.94 in the evidence
      - Report states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' for one arrangement; contradicts stated annual charge difference of -0.06% which implies lower costs in new solution
      - Report states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' for Standard Life; contradicts stated annual charge difference of +0.26% (positive indicating higher costs in recommended plan)
      - Report's critical yield of 1.9% matches passage evidence [P7]; critical yield of 4.8% matches passage evidence [P7]; critical yield of 7.8% matches passage evidence [P7]
      - Report shows immediate post-switch death benefit shortfall of £3,305.00 [P2, P7], which exactly equals the initial advice fee of £3,305.55 stated as charged to client—connection unexplained in recommendations
      - Report claims benefits include 'lower costs' and 'the receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure'; however, evidence for the largest ceding plan (Zurich £103,439.24) shows the new solution has HIGHER reduction in yield (2.2% vs 0.3% at mid growth, 2.3% vs 0.3% at high growth), contradicting the cost benefit claim
      - Report lists annual charge differences for three scenarios but only one is corroborated: the Zurich scenario shows 'critical yield' of 4.8% and 7.8% which match the report's stated critical yields, but the annual charge differences themselves (the percentage point differences between 0.3% RIY and 2.2%/2.3% RIY) are not stated in the report's charge comparison section
    The requirement asks whether switching costs are justified by benefits and supported by evidence. The evidence in [P1-P8] is a pension switch report that provides projections and comparisons. However, critical information is missing or contradictory. (1) The report's stated annual charge differences (-0.06%, -0.49%, +0.26%) are not found in the pension switch evidence passages. The figures checked in code confirm none of these percentages or their monetary equivalents appear in [P1-P8]. (2) The pension switch evidence shows reduction in yield (RIY) figures: Zurich scenario 0.3% existing vs 0.6%/2.2%/2.3% new; Standard Life 0.6% existing vs 0.6% new. These RIY differences do not align with the stated annual charge differences in the report. (3) The report's core cost-benefit claim—that switching produces lower costs—is contradicted by the evidence for the largest plan (Zurich £103,439). The evidence shows the new solution has materially higher RIY (2.2% vs 0.3% at 2.95% growth, 2.3% vs 0.3% at 5.88% growth), meaning higher ongoing charges, not lower. (4) The immediate death benefit shortfall of £3,305 exactly matches the initial advice fee, indicating the fee erodes value at day one. The report lists this as a benefit ('adviser charging facilitation') but does not clearly address that clients lose this amount upfront. (5) For Standard Life (£3,002), the evidence shows no meaningful charge difference (both 0.6% RIY), so the claimed benefit is not supported. (6) The report claims to consider alternatives; the pension switch report shows alternative comparisons (Standard Life only, Zurich only, overall position), but the suitability report does not discuss why the 'overall position' (switching all three) was chosen over retaining some plans. The guard permits non-financial benefits to justify cost increases where argued explicitly, but the report does not argue that risk alignment or adviser access justify the 2.0% RIY increase for Zurich; it asserts lower costs as a benefit without caveat. (7) Safeguarded benefits or guarantees are not addressed in either document for the existing plans being surrendered. The extraction metadata notes that regulatory considerations are absent ('NotPresentInDocument'), which is material for a switching recommendation.

  [G9.6] Lost guarantees or benefits disadvantage the client
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts for all five existing arrangements: 'No charges for transfer and/or switching'. The canonical model records exit penalties uniformly across all arrangements as hasPenalty=false with the description 'No charges for transfer and/or switching'. The report makes no explicit statement in the extracted model about safeguarded benefits being given up, lost guarantees, or valuable features that may be forfeited through the replacement.
    File:     Evidence from P13 (Scottish Widows Further Info) explicitly states: 'I can also confirm that there are no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached to this member's plan.' This covers the Scottish Widows arrangement. The Pension Switch Report [P1, P2] compares existing pensions against new solutions with detailed charge analysis and projected maturity values at various growth rates, showing comparisons across low, medium and high growth scenarios. P4 shows 'Existing pension' vs 'New solution' with critical yield calculations. P7 and P9 show alternative scenarios retaining some existing pensions. P6 (Policy Research) documents transaction history, transfer values, and policy status. P11 (Standard Life Policy Value) shows the arrangement value as £2,994.30. P8 shows Standard Life value as £3,002.02. P14 (Fact Find) contains a table with rows for 'GMP Amount', 'Enhanced Tax Free Cash', 'Guaranteed Annuity Rate / Pension (or similar)', 'EFI/Loyalty Bonus/Terminal Bonus', 'Guaranteed Growth Rates' and 'Death in service spousal benefits' - all appear blank or marked £0.00, suggesting no such benefits are in force. However, no comprehensive provider documentation for all five arrangements explicitly confirming absence of guarantees or safeguarded benefits for Aviva, Zurich, or People's Pension is present in the retrieved evidence.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' for all five arrangements but provides no corroborating evidence from provider documentation for most arrangements; only Scottish Widows is evidenced in P13.
      - Report extracted model contains no field recording whether safeguarded benefits exist or are being given up (/existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp is absent), yet the requirement demands analysis of lost guarantees or benefits.
      - Fact Find table [P14] shows empty cells for Guaranteed Annuity Rate, EFI/Loyalty Bonus/Terminal Bonus, and Guaranteed Growth Rates, with 'Death in service spousal benefits' marked £0.00, but these are incomplete records - not confirmed absence across all five arrangements.
      - Report narrative does not address whether any existing arrangement contains safeguarded benefits, guaranteed annuity rates, market value reductions, or other protections that would be forfeited on transfer.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Provider documentation confirming absence of guarantees, safeguarded benefits, market value reductions, or other valuable features for Aviva (EA3) arrangement., Provider documentation confirming absence of guarantees, safeguarded benefits, market value reductions, or other valuable features for Zurich (EA4) arrangement., Provider documentation confirming absence of guarantees, safeguarded benefits, market value reductions, or other valuable features for Standard Life (EA5) arrangement., Provider documentation confirming absence of guarantees, safeguarded benefits, market value reductions, or other valuable features for People's Pension (EA1) arrangement., Explicit statement in suitability report addressing whether any existing arrangement contains safeguarded benefits being given up, or confirmation from all providers that none exist.
    The requirement [G9.6] demands that where safeguarded benefits, guarantees or valuable features may be lost through replacement, the suitability report must demonstrate the client is not disadvantaged. The report's canonical model extracted from it does not include any analysis of safeguarded benefits being given up (/existingArrangements[]/safeguardedBenefits/isBeingGivenUp is absent). The extraction notes flag this as NotPresentInDocument, meaning the report is silent on this point. Evidence for Scottish Widows [P13] confirms 'there are no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached to this member's plan', and P14 (Fact Find) shows blank entries for Guaranteed Annuity Rates, terminal bonuses and guaranteed growth rates. However, comprehensive provider documentation for the other four arrangements (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life, People's Pension) confirming the absence of safeguarded benefits is not retrieved in this evidence pack. The report recommends switching three arrangements into a new Aviva solution (per P2 contents and P7, P9, P10 showing 'New solution: Aviva Life & Pensions UK Limited - Pension Portfolio - Choice') but provides no explicit analysis comparing what is being lost from the existing arrangements against what is gained. The Pension Switch Report [P1-P10] addresses charges, projected maturity values and critical yield, but does not expressly analyse safeguarded benefits, guarantees or other valuable protections specific to each arrangement. The comparison cannot be completed because provider confirmation that the four arrangements lack safeguarded benefits is not in the pack. Without that confirmation, or without the report's own documented analysis of any safeguarded benefits and why they do not disadvantage the client on switching, the requirement cannot be judged met. The report's silence combined with missing provider documentation creates an evidence gap.

  [G9.7] Other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report identifies five structured options: (1) Leave existing schemes untouched; (2) Leave schemes in place and switch investment funds; (3) Switch into another existing pension arrangement; (4) Switch into employer's pension arrangement (rejected due to loss of employer contributions, higher costs at 0.06% more expensive, limited fund range); (5) Set up new pension plan (recommended). The report also recommends specific actions on each existing arrangement: People's Pension retain; Scottish Widows encash; Aviva switch to Aviva Platform; Zurich switch to Aviva Platform; Standard Life switch to Aviva Platform. The pension switch report provides detailed comparisons for each transferred arrangement showing projected maturity values at age 75 under low, mid and high growth scenarios. For the Zurich arrangement, the report projects £102,000 (low), £127,000 (mid), £150,000 (high) under existing scenario versus £100,000, £125,000, £135,000 under new solution, showing an effect of switching of £2,000 decrease at mid growth. For Aviva, existing £3,630/£4,280/£4,020 versus new £3,620/£4,260/£4,010, effect £10 decrease. For Standard Life, existing £2,910/£3,440/£4,020 versus new £2,900/£3,440/£4,010. Reduction in yield figures provided (ranging 0.29% to 0.93% existing; 0.44% to 0.6% new). The report states for Aviva 'the plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' and for Standard Life 'the plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution'.
    File:     The pension switch report contains alternative comparison tables [P1, P3, P5, P6, P8] showing consolidated existing pensions versus new solution, with specific plan-by-plan comparisons for Aviva, Zurich, and Standard Life. Overall position shows: new solution £117,000-£127,000 maturity value across scenarios with 0.6% reduction in yield; individual plan comparisons show marginal decreases (Aviva £10 decrease, Zurich £2,000 decrease, Standard Life £2,000 decrease at mid growth). The Pension Switch Charges table [P13] shows existing Zurich charge 0.3% with new solution 0.6% reduction in yield; existing Aviva 0.6% with new solution 0.6%; existing Standard Life 0.6% with new solution 0.6%. The option analysis [canonical model] shows Option 4 (employer scheme) rejected with stated reasons but no numerical cost comparison provided. No alternative comparison is documented for the three schemes being switched (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) considering retention versus switching. Provider documentation [P9, P20, P21, P22] confirms transfer mechanisms exist and no safeguarded benefits are listed for any arrangement. The suitability report [P10, P12, P17, P19] provides narrative justification for switches emphasising greater investment choice, multi-asset fund access, enhanced retirement flexibility, and adviser charging facilitation, but does not cross-reference the specific cost and benefit analysis figures from the pension switch report.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states Aviva is '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' but Pension Switch Charges table shows existing Aviva charge 0.6% and new solution 0.6% reduction in yield, with impact -0.06% (meaning £2.25 savings, i.e., new solution is cheaper not more expensive)
      - Report states Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' but Pension Switch Charges table shows existing charge 0.18% (contradicting the 0.52% stated in arrangements table) and impact +0.26% (meaning new solution is 0.26% more expensive, not 0.08%)
      - Pension Switch Charges table at [P13] shows EA5 (Standard Life) computed on £103,438.46 (Zurich's fund value), not on Standard Life's actual £3,002.00 fund value, making the cost comparison for Standard Life unreliable
      - Report provides narrative cost comparison for employer scheme (Option 4) stating it is '0.06% more expensive' but no supporting numerical analysis table is included, unlike the detailed comparisons provided for the three recommended switches
      - Overall position in Pension Switch Report [P3] shows total maturity value of £127,000 when all pensions switched versus £127,000 retained (no material difference), but no explicit statement that switching provides comparable or superior outcomes justifies the recommendation
      - Suitability report [P10, P12, P17] emphasises multi-asset fund selection and blended approach as superior to existing, but Pension Switch Report shows Zurich's existing fund is Managed AP fund (risk level not disclosed) and new solution uses two funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced 50% and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced 50%), with no performance comparison provided between single Managed AP fund and the blended two-fund approach over the holding period
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Numerical cost/benefit analysis for Option 4 (employer scheme consolidation) to support the stated rejection that it is '0.06% more expensive', Plan-specific alternatives analysis for Aviva arrangement: whether retention, in-situ fund switch, or consolidation into another non-Aviva arrangement was considered and rejected, Plan-specific alternatives analysis for Zurich arrangement: whether retention, in-situ fund switch, or consolidation into another arrangement was considered and rejected, Plan-specific alternatives analysis for Standard Life arrangement: whether retention, in-situ fund switch, or consolidation into another arrangement was considered and rejected, Clarification of whether the charge figure states in the suitability report narrative (0.06% for Aviva, 0.08% for Standard Life) refer to platform charges, fund charges, or total ongoing charges, as the Pension Switch Report tables use different charge categories, Reconciliation of the Pension Switch Charges table EA5 row which appears calculated on Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's own fund value, to establish whether the Standard Life cost comparison is reliable, Performance comparison or projection showing that the new blended two-fund approach (CT Universal MAP 50% + HSBC Global Strategy 50%) will outperform or match the existing single Managed AP fund for Zurich over the client's investment horizon
    The trigger is present: the report recommends multiple replacements and switches across five existing arrangements. The requirement is that alternatives be considered and evidence demonstrate that other alternatives do not provide better outcomes.
    
    Structured alternatives are identified in the options analysis: Options 1-5 present a conceptual framework covering do-nothing, in-situ fund switch, consolidate into existing, consolidate into employer scheme, and set up new plan. Option 5 (new plan) is recommended alongside specific actions (retain People's Pension, encash Scottish Widows, switch three arrangements to Aviva Platform).
    
    However, comparisonPerformed must be set false because the evidence does not support reliable cost/benefit comparison for the critical switching decisions:
    
    1. The Pension Switch Report tables provided [P1, P3-8, P13-14] contain systematic errors in the cost analysis for Standard Life (the EA5 row is computed on Zurich's fund value of £103,438 rather than Standard Life's £3,002). This makes any cost comparison for Standard Life unreliable and undermines confidence in the recommendation to switch this arrangement.
    
    2. The suitability report narrative states Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive' [P17], but the Pension Switch Charges table at [P13] shows the impact as +0.26% (0.26% more expensive), a threefold discrepancy. No source document reconciles these figures or explains which is correct.
    
    3. For Aviva, the report states it is '0.06% more expensive' [P10], but the Pension Switch Charges table impact is -0.06% (meaning a saving, i.e., new solution is cheaper). This is a sign-reversal contradiction — the report's assertion directly contradicts the table on which the recommendation is supposed to rest.
    
    4. For all three arrangements being switched (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life), no evidence is provided that plan-specific alternatives were considered: retention, in-situ fund switch, or consolidation into an arrangement other than Aviva Platform. The canonical model indicates `/replacementAnalysis[]/alternativesConsidered[]` is absent — the extraction's own report confirms this path is not present. This is silence on the point, not extraction failure (it would have been flagged in expectedButAbsent). The evidence does not show that alternatives to switching each arrangement into Aviva specifically were evaluated.
    
    5. The projected maturity values in the Pension Switch Report show minimal or negative effect of switching: Zurich £2,000 decrease (1.6% at mid growth), Aviva £10 decrease (0.3%), Standard Life £2,000 decrease (1.6%). The overall position table [P3, P6, P8] shows total maturity values are comparable whether switching or retaining. The projected outcomes do not demonstrate that switching provides superior returns; they suggest switching carries a cost (reduction in yield 0.6% vs. 0.3% or 0.29% existing) with returns that are equal or lower.
    
    6. The suitability report emphasises non-financial benefits: "greater investment choice", "multi-asset fund selection", "enhanced retirement flexibility", and "adviser charging facilitation" [P10, P12, P17]. However, these narrative justifications are not quantified or evidenced against the Pension Switch Report's own projections, which show lower or comparable returns. The claim that multi-asset fund selection through Aviva is superior to existing is stated but not supported by fund performance comparison or fund rating analysis in the case file. The Pension Switch Report itself provides Diamond Ratings for funds [P4] (Columbia Threadneedle and HSBC both shown) but these are not compared to the existing Managed AP and Interest Deposits funds used for Zurich.
    
    The false-positive guard states: consolidation for its own sake is valid if the client asked for it (check CHK-002 objectives). The report does mention "consolidate your pensions, gain better control over them" as a priority [P10]. However, consolidation does not necessarily require switching to Aviva Platform; consolidation could be achieved by retaining the existing arrangements if the objective is simplification or moving the smaller pots into one arrangement.
    
    The second guard states: where research ranks an option above the recommendation, name both. The Pension Switch Report does not rank options. It provides comparisons but does not state that staying with existing arrangements would provide materially better outcomes. However, the maturity value totals suggest no material benefit from switching.
    
    The comparisonPerformed is false because the cost comparisons in the evidence are internally inconsistent (Aviva sign reversal, Standard Life computed on wrong fund value, cost statements in narrative contradicting table figures). A recommendation cannot be said to rest on reliable evidence when the evidence that is supposed to support it contains unresolved numerical contradictions and one plan's cost analysis is computed on another plan's fund value.
    
    Missing from evidence: plan-specific alternatives analysis for the three arrangements being switched, numerical justification for the cost claims in the suitability report narrative, reconciliation of the Standard Life charge computation error, and performance evidence that the new Aviva solution will outperform the existing arrangements given that projected maturity values are lower.

  [G9.8] Evidence supporting claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report claims 'Lower Costs - The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension' and asserts this as a benefit of switching. For Aviva (EA3), it states 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' with an impact of -0.06% / -£2.25 (meaning a saving). For Standard Life (EA5), it states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' but the Pension Switch Charges table shows a charge difference of +0.26% / +£268.94 (indicating higher costs in the recommended solution, not lower). The report concludes benefits include 'lower costs, greater investment flexibility, improved retirement options, and continued professional oversight.' It also claims 'Greater Investment Choice - The new scheme offers access to a wider range of investment options' and 'Multi-asset fund selection - Aviva offers dedicated risk level 5 multi-asset funds, eliminating the need for sub-optimal blended approach.' For Zurich, it asserts 'the blend consists of the Managed AP fund (in which you are already invested) and an asset-specific fund, Interest Deposits. We would not recommend investing in single asset types, as this reduces diversification.' No cost analysis supports the Zurich switch where the recommended solution costs 0.44% vs existing 0.93%, a saving of 0.49% / -£506.85.
    File:     The Pension Switch Report (category G) provides detailed switching analysis. For Aviva: comparison shows existing pension 0.6% reduction in yield, new solution 0.6% reduction in yield, with effect of switching -£30 or -0.6% decrease at mid-growth. For Standard Life: existing pension shows 0.5% reduction in yield, new solution 2.2% reduction in yield, with effect of switching -£12,000 or -9.4% decrease at mid-growth. For Zurich: existing pension shows 0.93% total ongoing charges (comprised of 0.6% + 0.33% from different charging components), new solution shows 0.44% total charge, with effect of switching -£2,000 or -1.6% decrease. The switch report summary ranks solutions by maturity value at mid-growth: Zurich existing £128,000 (1st), Existing Solution overall £127,000 (2nd), Aviva existing £126,000 (3rd), Standard Life existing £126,000 (4th), New Solution £125,000 (5th). The Zurich-only comparison shows low growth: existing £102,000 vs new £100,000 (-£2,000 or -2.0% decrease); mid-growth: existing £121,000 vs new £119,000 (-£2,000 or -1.6% decrease). For Standard Life-only: mid-growth existing £127,000 vs new £115,000 (-£12,000 or -9.4% decrease), requiring critical yield of 4.8%. The Standard Life analysis notes: 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' in the suitability report narrative but Pension Switch Charges table shows current 0.18%, impact +0.26%, implying recommended is 0.26% MORE expensive, not less. Fund choice analysis in Zurich section confirms: 'As there are no multi-asset funds available within your risk range, the blend consists of the Managed AP fund (in which you are already invested) and an asset-specific fund, Interest Deposits' — the same blend available now. Aviva offers 'dedicated risk level 5 multi-asset funds' and features like Flexi Access Drawdown are confirmed available on both existing (Zurich shows FAD Yes) and new solutions.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report claims 'Lower Costs' as a benefit for Standard Life switch but Pension Switch Charges table shows +0.26% impact (cost increase, not decrease); narrative states '0.08% more expensive' but table shows 0.26% more expensive — these three figures (0.08%, 0.26%, and narrative claim of lower cost) are mathematically inconsistent.
      - Report asserts multi-asset fund selection as a benefit for Zurich switch, but the existing Zurich arrangement already uses a blend of Managed AP and Interest Deposits funds — the same combination described as necessary in the new solution, so this is not a new benefit.
      - Report claims 'Greater Investment Choice' for Zurich but provides no evidence of fund count difference or specific named client requirement that distinguishes the new solution's offering from existing choice.
      - Zurich switch justified by 'Greater Investment Choice' and 'Multi-asset fund selection' benefits, yet Pension Switch Report shows new solution ranks 5th (£125,000 mid-growth maturity) vs Zurich existing 1st (£128,000), a £3,000 loss; no cost saving to offset the lower projected value.
      - Standard Life switch shows critical yield of 4.8% required to match existing plan value; this is the investment growth needed to compensate for the switch costs and higher ongoing charges, yet report frames switch as lower-cost benefit.
    The report asserts 'Lower Costs' as a primary benefit of switching for multiple pensions, but the evidence contradicts this claim for Standard Life and shows a net loss for Zurich. For Aviva, the -0.06% / -£2.25 annual saving is modest and offset by the £3,305.55 initial advice fee, requiring outperformance of £4,672 in the first year. For Standard Life, the report simultaneously states (1) 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' in narrative and (2) shows Pension Switch Charges table impact of +0.26% / +£268.94, yet claims 'Lower Costs' as a justification. The Pension Switch Report's mid-growth projections show Standard Life existing £127,000 vs new £115,000 (-£12,000 or -9.4% decrease), requiring a critical yield of 4.8% to match — this is not a cost saving but a cost burden requiring above-average growth to recover. For Zurich, the existing solution is ranked 1st in maturity value (£128,000) vs new solution 5th (£125,000), yet 'Greater Investment Choice' and 'Multi-asset fund selection' are cited as benefits. The Zurich existing arrangement already employs a blend of Managed AP fund and Interest Deposits to achieve risk level 5 — the same approach described as necessary in the new solution — so this is not a new benefit gained. The report provides no evidence of either (a) a fund count difference or (b) a specific client requirement that justifies switching to a lower-ranked solution. Flexi Access Drawdown and retirement flexibility benefits are confirmed available on both existing (Zurich) and new solutions per policy documentation, so these are not new. Adviser charging facilitation is presented as a benefit but is a service feature, not a financial benefit offsetting higher or maintained costs. The guard 'Where the recommended plan is cheaper, this requirement does not bite' does not apply here: the Aviva saving is marginal (£2.25 pa), Standard Life is more expensive, and Zurich ranks lower by projected value. For Standard Life and Zurich, the justification rests on non-financial benefits (fund choice, flexibility) without evidence that these are (1) unavailable on the existing plans or (2) required by the client. The report lacks substantiation that claimed benefits justify the costs or losses incurred.

  [G9.9] Evidence elsewhere in the file that supports or contradicts the replacement rationale
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report asserts multiple benefits of switching: (1) 'By switching to this scheme, you will benefit from lower costs, greater investment flexibility, and continued professional oversight' [P21, P22]; (2) 'Lower Costs - The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension' [P22]; (3) 'Lifestyling - Your funds are currently invested in lifestyling funds, which automatically reduce investment risk as you approach retirement. As a result, your portfolio will eventually move outside of your agreed risk range' [P21]; (4) 'Greater Investment Choice - The new scheme offers access to a wider range of investment options' [P21]; (5) 'Multi-asset fund selection - Zurich does not offer risk level 5 funds, so to achieve this profile, a blend of a risk level 7 and a risk level 2 fund has been used' [P21]. On small pots: 'Under the small pot rules, a £200 pension fund withdrawal would be taxed with 25% taken as tax-free cash and the remaining 75% taxed as income at your marginal tax rate. We estimate you would receive £170 net' [P21]. The recommendation is to consolidate into Aviva Platform Personal Pension SIPP [P20].
    File:     The file contains limited corroboration of the stated rationales. On costs: the Pension Switch Charges table [P22] shows Aviva with 0.50% current charge, but states 'plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution', creating internal contradiction. For Standard Life, the table shows current 0.18% and impact +0.26% (meaning the new plan is MORE expensive, not less). File notes [P16] state Zurich 'blend of funds have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva marginally' and that for Standard Life 'all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva' [P16]. On lifestyling: Aviva's own literature [P8] confirms 'An auto-switching investment approach is a way of investing your client's money over time, where it moves to different funds as your client moves closer to retirement' and the fund is 'Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2' showing it uses lifestaging. The new Aviva solution [P22] has platform charge 0.21% and investment management 0.22% totalling 0.44%. On alternatives: file notes [P16-P17] show alternatives were considered (People's Pension 'discounted as no servicing and reduced flexibility', Aviva alternative 'only mixed asset aviva fund available is risk rated 4 which does not meet the clients ATR', Zurich alternative rejected because 'no multi asset funds in his risk range', Standard Life rejected on cost grounds). On Scottish Widows encashment: the plan value is £211.32 [P21], but the report's small pots example uses £200, and calculates net proceeds as £170 (80.95% of £211.32, not 80% of £200 as the narrative suggests). Scottish Widows information [P14] confirms 'no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached to this member's plan' and 'Money4Life Plans do not support ongoing advisor charging'.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report asserts 'lower costs' as primary switch benefit, but Pension Switch Charges table shows Standard Life impact +0.26% (new plan MORE expensive); Aviva comparison states 'plan itself is 0.06% more expensive' which contradicts 'lower costs' claim
      - Report states small pots example uses £200 with £170 net proceeds, but actual plan value is £211.32; the calculation basis and projected proceeds are misaligned
      - File notes discount Zurich alternative on grounds that 'blend of funds have not performed as well as what we are offering on Aviva marginally', yet report does not disclose this performance comparison or explain why Zurich was rejected in final recommendation narrative
      - Report recommends consolidation to Aviva Platform but file notes state Zurich alternative fund blend 'have not performed as well', creating question whether performance data was weighted in recommendation
      - Aviva's own literature confirms lifestyling approach [P8], but report's rationale that 'portfolio will eventually move outside of your agreed risk range' is not explained in terms of why the new Aviva solution avoids this same outcome
    The comparison can be made. The report makes explicit assertions about lower costs driving the switch, but the Pension Switch Charges table contradicts this for at least one plan (Standard Life shows +0.26% impact, meaning MORE expensive). For Aviva, the narrative states the 'plan itself is 0.06% more expensive' yet claims 'lower costs' overall—this internal inconsistency undermines the 'lower costs' rationale. The guard 'A rationale resting on charges is contradicted by a charges schedule showing the opposite' applies: the report rests the recommendation partly on cost savings, yet its own charges comparison table shows Standard Life would be more expensive post-switch, not less. The lifestyling rationale is partially supported: Aviva does use lifestyling (confirmed in P8), but the report does not explain why switching to the Aviva Platform solution prevents the portfolio moving outside the agreed risk range if the new solution also uses auto-switching. File notes show alternatives were considered and evaluated, which is positive, but the performance-based rejection of Zurich ('have not performed as well...marginally') is documented in case notes but not disclosed to the client in the suitability report itself. The small pots calculation uses a £200 example when the actual fund value is £211.32; this discrepancy between example and reality is minor but creates ambiguity about whether the calculation is client-specific. Overall, the replacement rationale is weakly supported by contradictions in the charges analysis, incomplete explanation of how the new solution addresses the lifestyling issue, and insufficient disclosure of alternative evaluation.

  [G9.10] Material replacement-related information omitted from the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' across all 5 existing arrangements (stated on page 5 of the suitability report). The report recommends consolidation and switching of multiple pensions including People's Pension, Scottish Widows (encash), Aviva, Zurich, and Standard Life to new Aviva platform solutions. A Pension Switch Charges table is provided comparing existing and recommended solutions with impact figures. The report mentions 'Simplify and consolidate where necessary' as the strategy. For Scottish Widows (£211.32), the report states 'Under the small pots rules, a £200 pension fund withdrawal would be taxed with 25% taken as tax-free cash and the remaining 75% taxed as income at your marginal tax rate. We estimate you would receive £170 net.' The report does not explicitly address safeguarded benefits, guarantees, or features lost on any plan. No discussion of employer contributions or whether the client remains an active member of any scheme. The report does not document alternatives considered for each individual plan being replaced.
    File:     Evidence confirms nil exit penalties exist: Zurich Policy Info states 'The charge is capped at 1% from age 55' but also 'Transfer value and early retirement charge: There are no deductions taken from the current value if benefits are transferred or taken. The initial set up charges have already been paid.' [P13]. Standard Life Policy Info confirms 'No bid offer spread' and 'There is no platform charge' [P16], implying no exit charges on Standard Life. Zurich info confirms 'Fund switch charge £0.00' and 'We don't currently make a charge for switching funds' [P13]. People's Pension Policy Info shows ongoing employer and employee contributions as recently as June 2023 [P1, P2, P7], indicating the client is an active member of an employer-sponsored scheme. The People's Pension is therefore a live workplace pension. The policy documents do not disclose any safeguarded benefits, protected rights, or guaranteed features beyond standard pension protections. Scottish Widows Policy Info shows current value of £211.32 [P15] and projected growth illustrations but no protected benefits. No document in the file provides evidence of alternatives considered for individual plan replacements. The Pension Switch Charges analysis [P22] compares existing vs. new solutions and projects significant reductions (9.4%-10.0% decrease at projected values), but this is a single consolidated comparison, not plan-by-plan alternatives. No evidence in the file records whether employer contributions continue or cease upon transfer from People's Pension.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report claims £211.32 Scottish Widows fund will result in £170 net after tax, but the calculation basis uses £200 as the example amount, creating inconsistency in the arithmetic (£170/£211.32 = 80.4%, not the stated tax treatment). However, this is an approximation/rounding, not a material discrepancy about the existence of information.
      - The report states switch projections show 9.4%-10% decrease in value [P22], yet recommends proceeding; the critical yield required (1.9% to 7.8%) is materially positive, meaning funds must grow to break even. No evidence the report addresses whether this switching sacrifice is justified by claimed benefits.
      - The report does not disclose whether the People's Pension employer contributions (evidenced as ongoing through June 2023) will cease upon transfer, despite this being material loss of income.
      - The report asserts 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' uniformly but provides no plan-specific evidence: Zurich documentation confirms this for Zurich, Standard Life documentation confirms for Standard Life, but no evidence from People's Pension, Scottish Widows, or Aviva policies regarding transfer/switching charges is present in the file.
      - The report recommends consolidation but does not document whether alternative structures (e.g., retaining People's Pension as active workplace scheme) were considered, or why consolidation outweighs continuation of employer contributions.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Whether the client remains an active member of the People's Pension workplace scheme and the impact of transfer on future employer contributions, Plan-specific exit penalty and switching cost documentation for People's Pension, Scottish Widows, and Aviva existing arrangements, Documentation of safeguarded benefits, protected rights, or guaranteed features on any of the 5 existing arrangements, Documentation of alternatives considered for each plan (e.g., retention vs. transfer, consolidation vs. partial consolidation), Regulatory considerations for transferring from a live workplace pension (People's Pension), including COBS 9A.2.18R requirements for defined benefits with safeguards, Explicit comparison of employer contribution loss quantified against proposed benefits of consolidation
    The comparison cannot be fully performed because critical information necessary to assess replacement suitability is absent from the evidence pack. The report asserts no switching charges across all five arrangements but provides documented evidence only for Zurich and Standard Life. More significantly, People's Pension is demonstrably a live workplace pension with ongoing employer contributions through at least June 2023 [P1, P2, P7]. The report does not address the loss of these employer contributions upon transfer, which is material under COBS 9.2.5R (requirement to explain any disadvantages of the recommended arrangement). The Pension Switch analysis [P22] projects a 9.4%-10% reduction in projected values at age 75 across all growth scenarios, with critical yields of 1.9% to 7.8% required to break even. The report does not reconcile this projected loss against any named countervailing benefits (cost reduction is not evident; the switching saves only £268.94 annually on one plan while costing more in others). No alternatives are documented as considered, despite the decision to consolidate five different product types (occupational, personal, platform) suggesting a discrete choice point for each. The extraction's own confidence is 0.82 and flags multiple internal inconsistencies in figures (transfer value totals, charge calculations) and ambiguities in risk rating and property ownership, suggesting the report itself contains tensions between assertions that undermine the reliability of the replacement recommendation. The report does not address whether safeguarded rights exist on any transferred plan; Zurich historically may hold former protected rights [P11 and P13 reference S2P contracting-out history] but this is not explicitly confirmed as present or absent on this client's plan. Under COBS 9A.2.18R, where a transfer involves safeguarded benefits, specific protections apply; the report's silence on this is a gap. The evidence supports that no transfer charges apply, but it does not support that all material consequences of replacement have been disclosed.

CHK-010 — Vulnerability, Support Needs and Foreseeable Harm Overlay
Outcome: Potential Concern

  No trigger was recorded; this check applies to every case and was assessed anyway. Potential Concern across 5 requirement(s). 4 raised a concern (G10.1, G10.2, G10.3, G10.4). The report asserts isPowerOfAttorneyGranted = false but provides no assessment of whether one should be in place given the client's stated lack of understanding of investment products, pensions, and financial concepts. The report contains no documented vulnerability assessment despite the Risk Profile showing the client has 'No understanding/knowledge' of stocks and shares, EIS, and pension planning — factors that may indicate need for additional support or explanation. No support needs, communication adjustments, or specialized formats are documented in either the report or the file, despite the client's limited financial literacy being evident from the risk profile responses. 6 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G10.1] Details of additional support and needs, including third-party support
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that Power of Attorney is NOT granted (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted: false). The report does not mention any support needs, third-party support, family involvement, communication adjustments, or vulnerability indicators related to the client. The report contains no assessment of vulnerability, capacity constraints, or specialized communication requirements.
    File:     The Fact Find (P7, P21) records 'Preferred delivery method: No Preference' and shows the client completed the fact-finding process, risk profile questionnaire, and capacity for loss questionnaire without annotation of special communication needs or third-party involvement. The Risk Profile Report (P1, P5, P8, P11) shows the client answered 'No understanding/knowledge' to questions on stocks and shares, Enterprise Investment Schemes, and pension planning, indicating limited financial literacy. The client answered 'No' to investment experience questions on Unit Trusts, stocks, and EIS/VCTs within the last 5 years. Risk profile notes record 'No additional notes were recorded' for investor experience (P5) and capacity for loss discussion (P5). The Client Agreement (P15, P17) includes standard terms allowing third-party instructions via Power of Attorney, stating 'You must inform us promptly should you wish to revoke this and/or the Power of Attorney is terminated or expired.' No such arrangement is documented as in place. No specific vulnerabilities, support needs, communication preferences beyond 'no preference', or third-party involvement are recorded in any document.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts isPowerOfAttorneyGranted = false but provides no assessment of whether one should be in place given the client's stated lack of understanding of investment products, pensions, and financial concepts.
      - The report contains no documented vulnerability assessment despite the Risk Profile showing the client has 'No understanding/knowledge' of stocks and shares, EIS, and pension planning — factors that may indicate need for additional support or explanation.
      - No support needs, communication adjustments, or specialized formats are documented in either the report or the file, despite the client's limited financial literacy being evident from the risk profile responses.
      - The Fact Find records 'No Preference' for delivery method, but the report does not reference this or confirm what communication method was actually used to convey the advice.
    The report does not explicitly assert the presence of vulnerability, support needs, or third-party arrangements; rather, it is silent on these matters entirely. The canonical model shows isPowerOfAttorneyGranted = false and the report contains no sections on /vulnerability/perClient[]/supportNeeds[], /vulnerability/perClient[]/thirdPartySupport, or related fields. The extracted metadata confirms these paths are 'NotPresentInDocument'. This is not an internal inconsistency within the report itself, but rather an absence of documentation. However, the evidence in the file shows material indicators of limited financial capability: the client answered 'No understanding/knowledge' to fundamental questions about stocks, EIS/VCTs, and pension planning (P1). The risk profile notes explicitly record 'No additional notes were recorded' for investor experience and capacity for loss discussion (P5), indicating no exploratory conversation took place to probe these gaps or discuss support needs. The client's recorded literacy concerning investments is low, yet the suitability report makes recommendations involving pension transfers, investment portfolios, and complex financial planning with no documented discussion of how the client's stated lack of understanding was addressed or supported. The agreement to a Risk Rating 5 (Balanced) is noted in the extraction's ambiguity section, and the client answered comprehension questions revealing lack of knowledge, yet the report does not acknowledge this tension or document how understanding and informed decision-making were ensured. The failure to record or address support mechanisms for a client with demonstrated limited investment knowledge constitutes a gap in the process and in the evidence, rather than a contradiction between two stated positions.
